\title{\bfseries The Evaluator Is the Instrument: A Forensic Audit of Degenerate Consensus in Multi-Agent LLM Forecasting}

\author{Anonymous Authors
\thanks{Manuscript draft updated August 26, 2026. This research was conducted independently utilizing university High-Performance Computing (HPC) infrastructure. Three complete 30-event campaigns (Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1, Qwen2.5-7B-GGUF) were executed in July 2026 and form the primary empirical basis of the reported results. In August 2026 all 270 simulation transcripts were re-scored with a second, independent evaluator; that replication is reported in Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication} and both scored passes are released separately. Two analyses postdate the campaigns and were run against hosted endpoints rather than the cluster: the evaluator-comparison and truncation experiments of Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism}, and the single-agent baseline of Section~\ref{sec:cot_baseline} (2026-08-17). Both are marked as such wherever they enter a claim.}
}

\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
Multi-agent LLM social simulations are increasingly proposed for synthesizing calibrated probabilistic forecasts from a swarm of interacting personas---an operationalized ``Wisdom of Crowds''~\cite{surowiecki2005}. This paper is a forensic audit of one such benchmark: it documents a failure mode of that mechanism, then relocates the cause from the swarm to the instrument that measures it. \textbf{ECN-BENCH} elicits forecasts for $E=30$ resolved Polymarket events under direct elicitation and full social simulation ($N=300$ agents, $R=60$ rounds) across three open-weight models. Under simulation, $20.6\%$ of units return an exactly uniform probability vector against $3.3\%$ without it, and mean modal probability falls from $0.664$ to $0.54$--$0.56$ among the remainder. A proper scoring rule charges both effects as miscalibration~\cite{gneiting2007} while directional accuracy is unchanged, so this \textbf{loss of sharpness and degenerate consensus} alone explains the worse simulated scores---which would otherwise read as evidence that deliberation degrades reasoning.

A source-code audit traces the degeneracy to the \emph{extraction instrument} rather than the swarm: a keyword-matching heuristic silently disabled structured-output enforcement for the fixed LLM evaluator (Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}). Re-scoring the transcripts under a schema-enforced evaluator cuts the flat-forecast rate from $31/132$ to $1/132$ on the simulated units both evaluators read byte-identically, and the cross-model pattern the paper set out to explain vanishes. Degeneracy rises monotonically from $5\%$ to $65\%$ across terciles of evidence length, and re-running five further evaluators in the \emph{identical} unconstrained condition on the same transcripts localises it: four return nothing uniform at all, but a small reasoning model does ($8/58$ units), and $6$ of its $8$ failures fall on the very transcripts the original instrument failed on ($2.6$ expected by chance, $p = 0.012$). A $2 \times 4$ grid then assigns evidence length and enforcement experimentally, and neither survives: shortening the transcript does not reduce degeneracy but mildly increases it ($7/59$ at $2$k against $1/60$ untruncated, $p = 0.032$), and enforcement pooled over lengths changes nothing ($12/239$ against $11/239$, $p = 1.000$). What decides is which model reads the transcript. Neither pass is a reference measurement---three re-reads of byte-identical input reproduce only $4/30$ units exactly.

Any benchmark extracting probabilities with an LLM must therefore calibrate that extractor as an instrument: verify enforcement at runtime, repeat $k \geq 3$ times per unit, and score with two evaluators. We treat this \emph{relocation}---from a claim about deliberation to a reproducible failure of LLM-based probability extraction---as the paper's central contribution, and report flat-unit rate as its cheapest diagnostic. Remaining findings are context rather than validated results, with one exception (Section~\ref{sec:predictive_signal}): on the $20$ events resolving after every model's release date, the swarm's modal forecast is the outcome in $81.9\%$ of $80$ (model, event) pairs against a $32.6\%$ baseline (exact $p = 3.7\times10^{-22}$), and replacing the injected evidence with topically unrelated material costs $0.112$ Brier $[+0.042, +0.182]$. A single-agent baseline added after the audit bounds what that buys: one chain-of-thought call on the same evidence matches or slightly exceeds the swarm's directional accuracy ($0.834$ pooled) and its Brier, with no arm on which the swarm's advantage is distinguishable from zero (Section~\ref{sec:cot_baseline}).
\end{abstract}

\begin{IEEEkeywords}
Benchmark Forensics, Instrument Calibration, LLM-as-Judge Reliability, Structured-Output Enforcement, Multi-Agent Systems, Social Simulation, Calibration and Sharpness, Proper Scoring Rules, Forecast Degeneration, Large Language Models, Brier Score.
\end{IEEEkeywords}

\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}

Multi-agent LLM simulations are increasingly proposed as a way to convert language models' latent knowledge into calibrated collective judgments---an artificial instantiation of the ``Wisdom of Crowds''~\cite{surowiecki2005}, in which a swarm of independently reasoning personas is expected to average out individual error. Whether this happens is a question about the aggregate probability distribution the swarm returns, not only about whether that distribution points in the right direction. This paper is about a specific way the expectation can fail: iterative multi-agent deliberation can converge on a distribution that is directionally correct but has lost the \emph{sharpness}---the concentration of probability mass on the realized outcome---that a calibrated forecast requires. In the most severe form, deliberation returns no distinguishable answer at all: an exactly uniform probability vector over the outcome space. We call this failure mode \textbf{loss of sharpness and degenerate consensus}. Characterizing it---how often it occurs, what it costs under a proper scoring rule, and why an aggregate calibration metric can misattribute it to a collective reasoning failure that need not have occurred---is where this paper began. It is not where it ends. The failure turns out to belong one level down, not to the deliberating swarm but to the LLM instrument that reads the swarm's transcript and compresses it into a number. This paper is the forensic audit that establishes that relocation, and the instrument-calibration protocol that follows from it.

We arrived at this question while auditing a more conventional benchmark result. \textbf{ECN-BENCH} is a multi-agent social simulation testbed (the MiroFish-Offline pipeline, built on CAMEL-AI OASIS~\cite{oasis2024}) originally designed to test three hypotheses about LLM-based crowd intelligence:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Prediction Lift (Claim 1):} Does structuring LLMs as a multi-agent social simulation outperform direct, single-pass elicitation? We hypothesize that social simulation yields a lower Brier Score (better calibration) than a direct query.
    \item \textbf{Convergence (Claim 2):} Does the multi-agent simulation converge systematically toward a real-world-calibrated consensus across rounds? We model this opinion dynamics process via DeGroot consensus models and quantify it using Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) and variance decay.
    \item \textbf{Susceptibility (Claim 3):} Do individual agents update their beliefs in response to new information rationally (reflecting content-specific reasoning), or do they exhibit mere stimulus-reactivity? We isolate this by injecting controlled relevant breaking news versus structurally identical null-data at a fixed simulation checkpoint.
\end{enumerate}
Running this protocol across three models produced an aggregate result that, read at face value, looks like a clean rejection of the first hypothesis: mean Brier score under simulation exceeded the no-simulation baseline for every model tested. Tracing that result to the per-unit level, rather than accepting the aggregate, is what surfaced the phenomenon this paper reports. A fifth of simulated forecasts were not degraded predictions---they were uniform non-answers---and the surviving, non-degenerate forecasts were themselves measurably flatter than their no-simulation counterparts. Both effects are penalized by any proper scoring rule~\cite{gneiting2007} independently of whether the swarm's underlying judgment was correct, and together they are large enough to produce the negative aggregate result without any change in reasoning quality. That is where the investigation started rather than where it ended, and we report the three original hypotheses---along with the testbed's engineering, taxonomy, and statistical machinery---as the context in which it was found, subject to caveats detailed in Section~\ref{sec:condition_a} and Section~\ref{sec:serving_note} that limit what the original benchmark comparison can support on its own.

The audit that followed changed what this paper claims. Tracing the degeneracy through the code path that produces it shows the flat forecasts are introduced by the \emph{extraction instrument}---the fixed LLM evaluator (DeepSeek-R1-14B)---rather than by the swarm: a keyword-matching heuristic silently disabled structured-output enforcement across all three campaigns (Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}). Re-scoring all $270$ evaluated units (Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B, Qwen2.5-7B; $E=30$ events each) with a second, schema-enforced evaluator drops the flat rate from $20.6\%$ to $6.1\%$ and dissolves the cross-model pattern entirely (Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication}). Within the single campaign where evidence length varies without a confound, degeneracy climbs from $5\%$ to $65\%$ across terciles of input length while those same transcripts return sharp forecasts under four independent re-reads (Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism}), identifying input volume as the trigger and the unconstrained evaluator as the locus---neither alone produces the effect. Neither evaluator pass is a reference measurement: re-reading Condition A's byte-identical input three times reproduces only $4/30$ units exactly, with a mean Brier spread of $0.128$ (max $0.715$)---large enough to reverse the sign of one model's headline lift.

We therefore report the testbed's remaining results as context for this discovery rather than as validated benchmark findings. The No-Sim baseline was shared rather than run per model (Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}); all three campaigns are statistically underpowered ($\leq 9\%$ achieved power at $E=30$, Section~\ref{sec:power_limitation}); the models differ simultaneously in generation, precision, and serving stack (Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}); a run-integrity audit finds the reference campaign's backend failed silently on 23 of 30 events while recording itself as complete, and a first attempt to repair those events was itself defective in a way that produced two spurious findings before being caught (Appendix~\ref{sec:outage}); resolution-date stratification localises the aggregate lift to memorisation in that shared reference for two of the three campaigns, but not for the reference model itself (Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff}); round-level convergence telemetry is $27.7$--$87.7\%$ non-functional, inverting a previously reported convergence result (Section~\ref{sec:jsd_convergence}); and the reported difficulty--lift correlation of $r=0.847$ is an arithmetic artifact of regressing a difference on its own minuend (Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology}). One substantive claim survives the audit: on the $20$ events resolving after every model's release date, the swarm's modal forecast is the realised outcome in $81.9\%$ of $80$ (model, event) pairs against a $32.6\%$ chance baseline (exact $p = 3.7\times10^{-22}$), and it tracks the \emph{content} of what it deliberates over---replacing the injected evidence with topically matched but unrelated material costs $0.112$ Brier $[+0.042, +0.182]$ (Section~\ref{sec:predictive_signal}). A single-agent chain-of-thought baseline, run after the audit on the same evidence, then bounds the first half of that claim: it reproduces the directional accuracy on its own, so only the content-sensitivity contrast is evidence about deliberation rather than about what the base models already know (Section~\ref{sec:cot_baseline}).

Related literature offers competing accounts of why iterated multi-agent LLM interaction might homogenize output: multi-agent debate has been reported to improve factual accuracy through explicit disagreement~\cite{du2023debate}, while sycophantic convergence toward perceived majority or authority opinion has been documented in single-turn settings~\cite{sharma2023sycophancy}. Neither literature reports a mechanism by which prolonged deliberation would degrade a forecast's \emph{sharpness} specifically, as opposed to its accuracy---the distinction is central to what follows, since we find directional accuracy to be largely preserved while sharpness is not.

ECN-BENCH organises its event catalogue by a three-way taxonomy (Social/Electoral, Senate/Binary, Technical/Economic; see Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}), superseding the $2\times2$ Information-Type $\times$ Resolution-Horizon grid of the original specification, which the seed corpus does not carry as per-event metadata. The benchmark is not designed to measure what LLMs ``know,'' but rather how effectively the multi-agent \emph{process architecture} translates latent knowledge, personas, and dynamic deliberation into calibrated probabilistic forecasts---and, as this paper shows, whether that process preserves the forecast's informativeness at all.

To deploy this benchmark at scale (simulating 3,000 agents per event across dozens of configurations), standard cloud APIs are economically prohibitive and methodologically unstable due to undocumented model updates. ECN-BENCH is designed specifically for university-grade, air-gapped High-Performance Computing (HPC) clusters. This paper details the systematic system engineering and surgical patches (Architecture v5.0 through v5.10) developed to resolve critical bottlenecks, including NFS database locking on shared network storage, Apptainer/Singularity container isolation, outlines dependency resolutions, and multi-GPU server routing protocols.

\textbf{How this paper is organised.} It is a forensic audit report, and its structure follows the audit rather than the original benchmark protocol. Sections~\ref{sec:related}--\ref{sec:confound} describe the testbed and its instrumentation; Section~\ref{sec:full_campaign} then presents the campaign result exactly as the benchmark originally reported it, at face value and before any audit, because that is the artifact under examination. The body then divides in two.

\textbf{Part~I} establishes the instrument failure. Section~\ref{sec:degenerate} characterises the degeneracy phenomenologically; Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping} traces it to a specific defect in the scoring code, read directly from source; Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication} tests whether the finding survives a second, schema-enforced evaluator reading the same transcripts (it does not); Section~\ref{sec:four_model_extension} carries out the resulting calibration protocol in full---four models, five independent evaluator reads, contamination controlled; and Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} isolates the interaction between input volume and instrument that produces the failure.

\textbf{Part~II} reports what is left standing once the audit is applied: a single content-sensitive predictive signal, measured on the contamination-clean subset with the calibrated protocol (Section~\ref{sec:predictive_signal}), and a single-agent baseline that bounds how much of it the deliberation is responsible for (Section~\ref{sec:cot_baseline}).

Sections~\ref{sec:discussion} and~\ref{sec:conclusion} discuss limits and conclude. The detailed lookup tables and the long-form audit logs are not in the body: the consolidated errata are in Appendix~\ref{sec:errata_appendix}, the per-event score tables in Appendix~\ref{sec:per_event_tables}, and the run-integrity, contamination and event-structure analyses---which are context for the audit rather than validated benchmark results---in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_campaign_supporting}.

\section{Related Work}
\label{sec:related}

\subsection{Static vs. Dynamic Agent Benchmarking}
Traditional LLM benchmarks evaluate models in isolation. Knowledge benchmarks such as MMLU~\cite{mmlu}, and more recently Humanity's Last Exam (HLE)~\cite{hle2024}, focus on expert-level questions where models are scored on binary accuracy. While highly useful, they represent single-pass reasoning. In contrast, SWE-bench~\cite{swebench} and WebArena~\cite{webarena2023} evaluate models as active agents within a sandbox, executing code or navigating interfaces. ECN-BENCH occupies a unique intersection: it evaluates models as a collective, measuring emergent epidemiological and forecasting dynamics within an agent society.

\subsection{Large-Scale LLM Social Simulations}
The study of multi-agent societies has transitioned from classical agent-based models (ABMs) to generative agent architectures. Foundational work by Park et al.~\cite{park2023generative} demonstrated that LLMs could maintain believable individual memories and social behaviors in virtual towns. To scale these systems, the CAMEL-AI community introduced \textbf{OASIS} (Open Agent Social Interaction Simulations)~\cite{oasis2024}, a framework capable of scaling up to one million agents to study dynamic network properties, recommendation systems, and behavioral cascades. ECN-BENCH utilizes a localized, air-gapped fork of the MiroFish platform~\cite{mirofish2024}, built underneath on CAMEL-AI OASIS, converting a generative social environment into a precise, predictive forecasting instrument.

\subsection{GraphRAG and Graph-Based Agent Memory}
Standard Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) retrieves flat vector chunks, which fails to capture long-term relationship structures or temporal context. Microsoft's GraphRAG~\cite{graphrag2024} demonstrated that building a semantic knowledge graph over source documents allows for query-focused summarization over entire corpora. ECN-BENCH builds on this by implementing \textbf{Graphiti}~\cite{graphiti2024}, an open-source temporal context graph engine coupled with Neo4j. This allows individual agent memories to evolve dynamically, tracking entity associations and fact transitions with explicit temporal metadata (e.g., \texttt{valid\_at} and \texttt{invalid\_at} properties).

\subsection{Deliberative Degeneration and Forecast Sharpness}
\label{sec:related_degeneration}
Proper scoring rules such as the Brier score reward forecasts that are simultaneously calibrated and sharp; Gneiting and Raftery's ``maximize sharpness subject to calibration'' principle~\cite{gneiting2007} formalizes why a hedged-but-correct forecast is penalized relative to a confident-and-correct one, and is the theoretical basis for the mechanical account of our central finding (Section~\ref{sec:degenerate}). Separately, a growing literature examines how iterated multi-agent LLM interaction changes output diversity: multi-agent debate frameworks report that structured disagreement between model instances can improve factual accuracy relative to single-model sampling~\cite{du2023debate}, while work on sycophancy shows that models shift stated positions toward a perceived interlocutor's view even in single-turn settings~\cite{sharma2023sycophancy}. Both literatures speak to whether interaction changes what a model says; neither directly addresses whether it changes how \emph{confidently} the model says it, which is the axis on which we observe degradation. To our knowledge, the concentration of exactly-uniform, non-informative outputs under iterated social-simulation deliberation---rather than under adversarial or debate framings---has not been previously reported as a distinct failure mode with its own measurable rate and its own candidate serving-stack mechanism.


\subsection{Constrained Decoding and Structured Output}
\label{sec:related_constrained}
The defect this paper documents is the absence of grammar-constrained generation, and the repair is its presence, so the constrained-decoding literature is where our contribution sits even though we make no methodological contribution to it. Guided decoding restricts sampling to tokens permitted by a grammar or JSON schema, and is available in the serving stack this benchmark ran on through the outlines backend~\cite{vllm_guided,vllm}. The engineering trade-offs are well documented---schema compilation cost, and instability on quantised weights, which is precisely why the bypass we found was introduced in the first place (Appendix~\ref{sec:schema_bypass}).

What is less discussed, and what this paper supplies a case study for, is what an unconstrained model does \emph{instead} when the task is probability extraction rather than free text. Asked to compress a long, multi-sided, partly contradictory transcript into a distribution over $K$ outcomes, a model with no schema to satisfy has a degenerate option available that a constrained one does not: it can express its uncertainty in prose and then emit the uniform vector, which is the minimax point under a symmetric loss it is not in fact being scored by. Enforcement does not make the model better informed; it forecloses the hedge. The measurement consequence is severe because a proper scoring rule charges that hedge the full uniform loss and cannot distinguish it from a swarm that genuinely reached no consensus.

We are careful about how far this generalises, because our own data bounds it. Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} runs five further evaluators in the identical unconstrained condition on the identical transcripts and finds essentially no degeneracy, so ``unconstrained decoding over long input causes uniform forecasts'' is \emph{not} what we are claiming. The hedge is available to any unconstrained model; whether a given deployed model takes it is an empirical property of that deployment. That is the practical point for benchmark designers: enforcement is cheap insurance against a failure whose incidence you cannot predict from the model card.

\subsection{LLM-as-Judge Reliability and Evaluation Instruments}
\label{sec:related_judge}
A parallel literature treats the LLM evaluator itself as an unreliable instrument rather than a neutral scoring function. Zheng et al.~\cite{zheng2023judging} establish LLM-as-a-judge as a scalable proxy for human preference while documenting systematic biases (position, verbosity, self-enhancement) and quantifying disagreement against human raters. That literature typically audits judge-versus-human agreement on a single pass; it does not, to our knowledge, report judge-versus-judge or pass-versus-pass reproducibility under byte-identical input, which is the axis Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication} measures directly. Our source-code-level finding that a keyword-matching heuristic silently disables structured-output enforcement for a specific evaluator model (Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}) is a concrete instance of a failure mode that judge-reliability critiques generally treat as evaluator model capability rather than evaluator configuration---a distinction that matters because the second is fixable by construction and the first is not. Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} shows the distinction is not merely conceptual here: five other evaluators in the same misconfiguration do not reproduce the failure, so what we document is a property of one deployed extractor rather than a capability limit of unconstrained LLM judges.

\subsection{Benchmark Contamination and Temporal Data Leakage}
\label{sec:related_contamination}
Detecting whether a benchmark's evaluation set has leaked into a model's training data is an active research program largely separate from ours: Oren et al.~\cite{oren2024proving} give a black-box statistical test for contamination based on canonical-ordering likelihood, requiring no access to weights or pretraining data. We do not attempt anything comparable in rigor; Appendix~\ref{sec:contamination} instead uses the coarser, cheaper proxy of each event's resolution date relative to each model's public release date, which only bounds the \emph{possibility} of memorization rather than proving its occurrence. We report this as a limitation, not an equivalent method, and flag Oren et al.'s likelihood-based test as the natural next instrument to apply to ECN-BENCH's own seed corpus.

\subsection{LLM Forecasting and Calibration Benchmarks}
\label{sec:related_forecasting}
ECN-BENCH's Brier-score framing sits inside a growing body of work that evaluates LLMs directly as forecasters against real-world, resolvable questions. Halawi et al.~\cite{halawi2024approaching} build a retrieval-augmented forecasting system that approaches the accuracy of competitive human forecasters on questions drawn from live prediction platforms, and Karger et al.~\cite{karger2024forecastbench} introduce ForecastBench, a continuously refreshed benchmark designed specifically to avoid the kind of resolution-date leakage Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff} audits after the fact. Both efforts evaluate single-model, single-pass forecasting; neither introduces a multi-agent deliberation stage between elicitation and scoring, which is the axis ECN-BENCH varies and the one on which we report loss of sharpness. Positioned against this literature, our contribution is not a new forecasting benchmark in this sense---it is closer to a finding about what happens to forecast quality, measured by the same Brier-score machinery this literature already uses, when a deliberation process is inserted before the LLM commits to a number.

The local serving stack and the HPC systems engineering (Architecture v5.0--v5.10) that make the following simulations executable on an air-gapped cluster---hybrid vLLM/Ollama routing, multi-GPU server isolation, Apptainer containerization, and NFS database concurrency fixes---are a precondition for the results below rather than part of the central finding, and are reported in full in Appendix~\ref{sec:architecture}.

\section{Experimental and Benchmark Design}
\label{sec:design}

\subsection{Taxonomy and Event Selection}
\label{sec:taxonomy}
ECN-BENCH curates $E=30$ resolved real-world categorical markets from Polymarket. Polymarket is used purely as a curation mechanism (identifying clear, multi-outcome categorical events with verified ground truth) rather than as a baseline price signal. The catalogue grew from the 25 events of specification v3.0 to the 30 events used in all campaigns reported here; references to $N=25$ in earlier drafts describe the superseded specification.

The original specification organised events on a $2\times2$ grid of Information Type $\times$ Resolution Horizon. In practice the seed corpus is labelled by a flat three-way prefix, and all analysis in this paper uses that partition, shown in Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}. We report the three-way scheme rather than the $2\times2$ grid because it is what the data carries; horizon was never recorded as a per-event field, so the quadrant assignment cannot be reconstructed. Note that ``Senate/Binary'' is defined by question format rather than subject matter, so the partition mixes a topical axis with a structural one---an imperfection we retain for traceability with the released seed files, and which readers should bear in mind when interpreting the taxonomy breakdown of Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy_breakdown}.

\subsection{Experimental Conditions}
To isolate the predictive value of multi-agent social interaction, each model is evaluated under three distinct conditions:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Condition A (No-Sim Baseline):} The model is presented with the Seed Document and directly queried via the ReportAgent to produce a categorical probability forecast. This represents the raw knowledge baseline.
    \item \textbf{Condition B (With-Sim Benchmark):} A 3,000-agent MiroFish-Offline simulation is initialized. Personas read the Seed Document and participate in a 60-step social network simulation. At step 30, a \textbf{Content-Specific Relevant Injection} (breaking news related to the event) is introduced. The final forecast is extracted at step 60.
    \item \textbf{Condition C (Null Control Simulation):} Identical to Condition B, but the perturbation injected at step 30 is a \textbf{Null Injection} (structurally identical, length-matched, but entirely unrelated content). This serves as the control to measure information susceptibility against general noise susceptibility.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Simulation Output Fidelity: Action Density and Scope Conditions}
\label{sec:action_fidelity}

As a design-transparency note, we audited social-action density by directly querying the authoritative SQLite \texttt{trace} tables across 1,380 database files on the HPC cluster. We note that the \texttt{actions.jsonl} summary files---used in earlier drafts of this analysis---systematically undercount organic social actions due to a cursor-tracking bug: during telemetry-probe rounds (12, 24, 36, 48, 60), the probe function advances the \texttt{last\_rowid} cursor past all new rows but only logs \texttt{INTERVIEW} actions to the jsonl, silently discarding organic actions written during those rounds. The \texttt{.db} files retain all actions and are the authoritative source. Table~\ref{tab:action_density_30event} reports the exact aggregate; Table~\ref{tab:action_breakdown} reports the per-type breakdown.

The data reveals a strong model-dependent social-action density. Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ is by far the most socially interactive model (27,087 actions, 40.31/DB), with the highest \texttt{follow} (5,993) and \texttt{comment\_like} (3,010)---indicating it builds the densest peer network and deepest peer-to-peer engagement. Mistral-7B is second (9,364, 35.47/DB) but exhibits a different interaction profile: high \texttt{post} (4,476) and \texttt{like} (1,946) but disproportionately high \texttt{dislike} (67 vs.\ Llama's 7) and low \texttt{comment\_like} (167 vs.\ Llama's 3,010), suggesting antagonistic/\allowbreak broadcast-style rather than deliberative engagement. Qwen2.5-14B---whose full 30-event campaign (180 DBs) was scanned directly---produces moderate, analysis-focused interaction (2,407, 13.38/DB): substantial \texttt{post} (1,215) and \texttt{comment} (585) but minimal \texttt{follow} (11), meaning agents write substantive contributions but build almost no social network. Qwen2.5-7B is the only near-dormant model (723, 2.74/DB). Persona generation quality is high across all models (4-axis DNA, $\sim$40\% institutional accounts, English-only rule enforced). See Appendix~\ref{sec:interaction_realism} for mechanism implications.

\section{The Seven-Dimension Grading Framework}
\label{sec:grading}

To analyze both the ultimate forecasting capability of the swarm and the internal dynamics of emergent intelligence, ECN-BENCH implements a mathematically rigorous 7-dimension scoring system. 

\subsection{D1: Prediction Accuracy (Weight: 0.25)}
The primary forecasting metric is the \textbf{Multi-Category Brier Score ($BS$)}, which evaluates the calibration of the swarm's forecast. For a given event with $K$ mutually exclusive categories, let $f_k$ be the swarm's predicted probability for category $k$, and $o_k$ be the one-hot indicator of the realized outcome ($o_k = 1$ if category $k$ occurred, $0$ otherwise). The Brier Score is formulated as:
\begin{equation}
    BS = \sum_{k=1}^K (f_k - o_k)^2
\end{equation}
This is the unnormalised multi-category (Brier) score, bounded in $[0, 2]$, where $0$ represents a perfect deterministic forecast and $2$ represents a confident forecast on the wrong category. We report this convention---rather than the $\frac{1}{2}$-scaled variant bounded in $[0,1]$---because it is what the pipeline computes: $34$ of the $360$ unit scores in the primary pass exceed $1.0$, which the $\frac{1}{2}$-scaled convention cannot produce. Across all six evaluator passes of all four campaigns ($2{,}070$ scores) the largest is $1.883$ (Mistral-7B, \texttt{C12\_C\_r1}, Evaluator~A read~3); the bound of $2.0$ is attained exactly, six times, only in the truncation grid of \S\ref{sec:truncation_grid}, where a unit places its entire mass on one wrong category. No score anywhere in the released data exceeds $2.0$. Readers comparing against $[0,1]$-normalised Brier scores elsewhere in the literature should halve all values reported here. Note that the uniform-forecast reference used below, $BS_{random} = 1 - 1/K$, is the correct expected score for $f_k = 1/K$ under \emph{this} unnormalised convention, so the Brier Skill Scores we report are internally consistent.

The aggregate Brier Score across $E$ events is $BS_c = \frac{1}{E} \sum_{e=1}^E BS_{e,c}$ for condition $c \in \{A, B, C\}$. 

We formally define \textbf{Simulation Lift ($Lift$)} as the empirical delta in Brier calibration:
\begin{equation}
    Lift = BS_A - BS_B
\end{equation}
A positive $Lift$ indicates that the social simulation workflow produced a better-calibrated forecast than direct elicitation on the events measured. It is a descriptive statistic, not a proof: establishing that the difference is not attributable to sampling variation requires the inference machinery of Section~\ref{sec:statistics}, and interpreting it as a property of the \emph{model} requires that Condition A be run per model, which was not the case in the campaigns reported here (Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}).

To normalize performance across heterogeneous question formats with varying option counts $K \in \{2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 9\}$ and prevent probability dispersion bias, ECN-BENCH mandates the \textbf{Brier Skill Score (BSS)} as the primary skill evaluation metric:
\begin{equation}
    BSS = 1 - \frac{BS_{model}}{BS_{random}} \quad \text{where} \quad BS_{random} = 1 - \frac{1}{K}
\end{equation}
Where $BS_{random}$ represents the expected multi-category Brier error under uniform random guessing ($f_k = \frac{1}{K}$). The BSS metric possesses intuitive econometric bounds:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $BSS = +1.0$: Perfect deterministic prediction ($f_{correct} = 1.0$).
    \item $BSS = 0.0$: Performance equivalent to uniform random guessing.
    \item $BSS < 0.0$: Sub-random performance (worse than uninformed guessing).
\end{itemize}
The score $D1$ is directly mapped from the Brier Skill Score: $D1_{score} = \max(0, 100 \times BSS_B)$. BSS enables fair aggregation of Boolean ($K=2$) and Multiple Choice (MCQ, $K \ge 3$) events into a unified predictability index.

\subsection{D2: Polarization (Weight: 0.15)}
To measure whether the agent swarm fragments into deeply entrenched, opposing camps, we utilize the \textbf{Esteban-Ray (ER) Index of Polarization}. Rather than measuring general dispersion, the ER Index captures both identification (internal group cohesiveness) and alienation (distance between groups). Assume the simulated population forms $G$ distinct opinion clusters regarding the event outcome. Let $p_i$ be the population proportion of cluster $i$, and $y_i$ be the mean probability vector of group $i$. The ER Index is defined as:
\begin{equation}
    ER(\alpha) = \kappa \sum_{i=1}^G \sum_{j=1}^G p_i^{1+\alpha} p_j \|y_i - y_j\|_1
\end{equation}
Where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\alpha$ is the polarization sensitivity parameter. We fix $\alpha = 1.0$ to weight internal identification and external alienation equally.
    \item $\kappa$ is a normalization constant scaling $ER \in [0, 1]$.
    \dots
\end{itemize}
A higher $ER$ score represents severe fragmentation. The normalized dimension score is $S_2 = 100 \times (1 - ER_{norm})$.

\subsection{D3: Herd Effect (Weight: 0.15)}
The Herd Effect measures excess conformity—the degree to which individual agents abandon their private signals to adopt the majority consensus purely due to social peer pressure, decoupled from real-world facts. For each step transition $t-1 \rightarrow t$, let $M_t = \text{argmax}_k \bar{p}_{t,k}$ be the swarm's majority opinion class. We define the individual agent conformity indicator as:
\begin{equation}
    Conform_{i,t} = \mathbf{1}\left[\text{argmax}_k p_{i,t,k} = M_t \quad \text{and} \quad \text{argmax}_k p_{i,t-1,k} \neq M_{t-1}\right]
\end{equation}
The raw herd index for a simulation is:
\begin{equation}
    Herd = \frac{1}{T \cdot N_{agents}} \sum_{t=1}^T \sum_{i=1}^{N_{agents}} Conform_{i,t}
\end{equation}
To isolate conformity driven by social mimicry rather than rational updating, ECN-BENCH calculates the excess herd score under relevant information relative to control:
\begin{equation}
    D3_{raw} = Herd_B - Herd_C
\end{equation}
Lower is better. A highly positive $D3_{raw}$ indicates that relevant injections trigger massive uncritical herd conformity, whereas a rational swarm updates beliefs diversely.

\subsection{D4: Deliberation Quality (Weight: 0.15)}
To evaluate whether agent interactions are reasoned or reactive, ECN-BENCH adapts the \textbf{Discourse Quality Index (DQI)} from political science. The fixed evaluator (Section~\ref{sec:confound}) scores a stratified random sample of $M=100$ agent interactions. Each interaction is graded across four sub-criteria (each scored $0$ to $3$ or $0$ to $4$):
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Participation:} Equality and accessibility of speech acts.
    \item \textbf{Level of Justification:} Whether claims are accompanied by logical assertions ($0$: no justification; $1$: inferior; $2$: qualified; $3$: sophisticated).
    \item \textbf{Respect:} Tone toward peers ($0$: disrespectful; $1$: neutral; $2$: explicitly respectful).
    \item \textbf{Constructive Politics:} Collaboration toward a consensus ($0$: positional; $1$: alternative proposals; $2$: mediating consensus).
\end{enumerate}
The normalized score is $S_4 = 100 \times \frac{Score_{observed} - Score_{min}}{Score_{max} - Score_{min}}$.

\subsection{D5: Susceptibility (Weight: 0.10)}
Susceptibility quantifies whether the swarm updates its beliefs in response to meaningful information (Claim 3). We track the belief updating efficacy under meaningful perturbation (Condition B) relative to structurally identical null noise (Condition C). We define the post-injection calibration improvement as:
\begin{equation}
    PostImprove_{e,m,c} = BS_{e,m,c, (t=29)} - BS_{e,m,c, (t=60)}
\end{equation}
Where $t=29$ is the step immediately preceding the perturbation. The raw Susceptibility score is the difference in lift:
\begin{equation}
    D5_{raw} = \frac{1}{E} \sum_{e=1}^E \left(PostImprove_{e,m,B} - PostImprove_{e,m,C}\right)
\end{equation}
A positive $D5_{raw}$ mathematically demonstrates that the swarm updates its beliefs productively when presented with real signal, while remaining resistant to null noise.

\subsection{D6: Convergence (Weight: 0.10)}
We model consensus formation through the \textbf{DeGroot Consensus Model}. In the classical model, agent beliefs evolve via a transition probability matrix $W$ representing social trust, such that $x(t+1) = W x(t)$. In ECN-BENCH, we measure the mathematical distance to consensus by the final round. First, we define the dispersion trajectory of agent probability vectors at step $t$:
\begin{equation}
    Disp_t = \frac{1}{N_{agents}(N_{agents}-1)} \sum_{i < j} \sum_{k=1}^K |p_{i,t,k} - p_{j,t,k}|
\end{equation}
The opinion convergence (contraction rate) is defined as:
\begin{equation}
    Contr = 1 - \frac{Disp_{T}}{Disp_{1}}
\end{equation}
To prevent the swarm from converging on the wrong answer (i.e., highly confident but incorrect consensus), we implement an \textbf{Accuracy Guard}:
\begin{equation}
    Guard = 1 - BS_B
\end{equation}
The convergence score is formulated as the joint product:
\begin{equation}
    D6_{raw} = \text{mean}_e (Contr_e \times Guard_e)
\end{equation}
A high score requires both high consensus formation and high accuracy.

\subsection{D7: Information Diversity (Weight: 0.10)}
If all agent personas act identically, the simulation holds no architectural value. We quantify semantic diversity using \textbf{Shannon Entropy}. The fixed evaluator maps the arguments extracted from the Graphiti temporal memories into $S$ discrete semantic bins. Let $q_i$ be the relative frequency of argument bin $i$. The entropy is:
\begin{equation}
    H = - \sum_{i=1}^S q_i \ln(q_i)
\end{equation}
To normalize this across varying bin sizes, we compute Pielou's Evenness (Shannon Equitability):
\begin{equation}
    E_H = \frac{H}{\ln(S)}
\end{equation}
The final score is $S_7 = 100 \times \text{mean}_{e} \text{mean}_{t \in \{1..T-1\}} E_{H, e, t}$.

\subsection{Composite Aggregation and Grade Bands}
The final \textbf{ECN-Predictability Index (EPI)} is a weighted composite of the seven normalized dimension scores ($S_1 \dots S_7 \in [0, 100]$):
\begin{equation}
    EPI = 0.25 S_1 + 0.15 S_2 + 0.15 S_3 + 0.15 S_4 + 0.10 S_5 + 0.10 S_6 + 0.10 S_7
\end{equation}
The resulting $EPI$ maps directly to standardized academic grade bands, as defined in Table~\ref{tab:grades}.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{ECN-BENCH Score Grading Bands}
\begin{tabular}{lcl}
\toprule
\textbf{EPI Range} & \textbf{Grade} & \textbf{Interpretation} \\
\midrule
90 - 100 & A+ & Exceptional multi-agent forecasting and dynamic deliberation \\
80 - 89  & A  & Strong, well-calibrated, and resilient swarm intelligence \\
70 - 79  & B  & Solid performance, with minor mcalibrations or conformity issues \\
60 - 69  & C  & Mixed/unstable quality; susceptible to noise \\
50 - 59  & D  & Weak simulation lift; poor deliberation quality \\
$<$ 50   & F  & Fails baseline forecasting expectations \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\label{tab:grades}
\end{table}

\section{Statistical Inference and Econometric Validation}
\label{sec:statistics}

Given the high compute costs, sample sizes are severely constrained: the campaigns reported here use $E=30$ events per model, and the specification anticipates as few as $E=10$ for expensive API runs. Section~\ref{sec:power_limitation} shows that $E=30$ delivers under $9\%$ power against the effect sizes actually observed, so the tests below should be read as descriptive rather than confirmatory. Reporting simple empirical means is statistically invalid due to the non-normal distribution of squared calibration errors. ECN-BENCH mandates two strict statistical protocols:

\subsection{The Diebold-Mariano Test for Predictive Superiority}
To determine if the With-Sim Brier scores (Condition B) are significantly better than the No-Sim scores (Condition A), we utilize the \textbf{Diebold-Mariano (DM) Test}. The DM test compares the forecast accuracy of two competing series, adjusting for potential autocorrelation. 

Let the Brier loss for event $e$ under Condition A be $L_A(e)$ and under Condition B be $L_B(e)$. We define the loss differential as:
\begin{equation}
    d_e = L_A(e) - L_B(e)
\end{equation}
The null hypothesis $H_0$ is that the expected loss differential is zero: $\mathbb{E}[d_e] = 0$. The DM statistic is formulated as:
\begin{equation}
    DM = \frac{\bar{d}}{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_d^2 / N}}
\end{equation}
Where $\bar{d}$ is the sample mean of the differentials, and $\hat{\sigma}_d^2$ is a consistent estimate of the asymptotic variance of $\sqrt{N}\bar{d}$. Assuming independent event-level observations across Polymarket dates, the autocovariance at lags $>0$ is zero, and $\hat{\sigma}_d^2$ reduces to the sample variance of $d_e$. Under $H_0$, the DM statistic asymptotically follows a standard normal distribution. A positive DM statistic with $p < 0.05$ (critical value $> 1.64$ for a one-sided test of superiority) rigorously validates the Simulation Lift Claim.

\subsection{Bootstrap Confidence Intervals}
To compute robust bounds for the Brier score of each condition, researchers must use non-parametric bootstrapping:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Draw $B = 1,000$ bootstrap replicates of size $N$ from the observed event loss set $\{BS_e\}_{e=1}^N$ with replacement.
    \item Compute the mean Brier score $\overline{BS}^*_b$ for each replicate $b \in \{1 \dots B\}$.
    \item Determine the $95\%$ Confidence Interval ($CI_{95}$) using the percentile method, extracting the $2.5\text{th}$ and $97.5\text{th}$ percentiles of the bootstrap distribution.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Anti-Confound Protocols and Weight Integrity}
\label{sec:confound}

To ensure that observed variations in simulation performance are strictly attributable to the benchmarked models rather than systemic variables, ECN-BENCH mandates two architectural controls:

\subsection{Methodological Constants}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Fixed Graph-Building LLM:} Zep/Graphiti uses an internal LLM to extract entities and relations from agent transcripts. ECN-BENCH mandates that this internal model remains absolutely fixed (e.g., Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct running locally) across all benchmarks. If each benchmarked model is used to build its own memory graph, differences in memory structure become a severe confounding variable.
    \item \textbf{Fixed Evaluator LLM:} a single model is hard-coded as the MCQ ReportAgent interviewer and DQI evaluator across all runs, to prevent circular self-evaluation bias. For the July 2026 campaigns reported here this was \textbf{DeepSeek-R1-14B}, served locally, because the HPC compute nodes are air-gapped and no external API call is possible from within a batch job. The benchmark specification names GPT-4o-mini as the reference evaluator and earlier drafts of this paper described it as such throughout; that description was inherited from the specification and does not match what was executed. All evaluator-reliability results in Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_reliability} pertain to DeepSeek-R1-14B, and the low $\kappa$ values reported there should be read as a property of that evaluator at this rubric granularity, not of GPT-4o-mini.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The Gold Standard Context Protocol}
To completely eradicate lookahead bias and ``web-leakage'' (where models bypass local prompts and retrieve resolved event dates from pre-training data), ECN-BENCH context files (\texttt{data/seeds/*/context.md}) undergo a complete sanitization:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Zero-URL Policy:} All URLs are completely stripped.
    \item \textbf{Strict Resolution Contracts:} Resolution criteria are written as strict legal contracts defining exact institutional sources of truth and resolving fractional edge cases.
    \item \textbf{Cutoff Enforcement:} All data is strictly frozen on the eve of the event, replacing absolute predictive language with probabilistic models to force active reasoning.
\end{enumerate}

The multi-criteria parameter sweep that selected the $N=300$, $R=60$ production configuration used for every result below, and the sub-15B model exclusion criterion derived alongside it, are engineering calibration steps rather than part of the central finding; the full sweep is reported in Appendix~\ref{sec:results}.

None of it is a validated benchmark result. Section~\ref{sec:replication_implications} states precisely which of these numbers we withdraw, and Appendix~\ref{sec:errata_appendix} lists every correction by line. At that configuration we ran three full 30-event campaigns covering the entire ECN-BENCH catalog for each of three model families, on NVIDIA A100 GPUs during July 2026. Figure~\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the complete pipeline.

\subsection{Condition A Is a Shared Reference, Not a Per-Model Baseline}
\label{sec:condition_a}

Before presenting the aggregate statistics we must state a structural property of the campaign that constrains every ``lift'' figure in this paper. Condition A was executed once and its output reused verbatim for all three model campaigns. The $BS_A$ values are not merely equal in the mean: the full predicted probability vectors are byte-identical across Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B for all 30 events. Determinism (temperature $0$, seed $42$) cannot produce this, since three different model families do not emit identical distributions; the Condition A record was generated once and copied.

The consequence is that $Lift = BS_A - BS_B$ is \emph{not} a within-model contrast between a model's own direct elicitation and its own simulated swarm. It is the distance from each model's simulated forecast to a single fixed external reference. Two things follow. First, all cross-model differences in reported lift are differences in $BS_B$ alone and carry no information about the models' direct-elicitation ability. Second, Claim 1 as originally posed---``does the simulation step add lift beyond what \emph{the model} can do alone''---is not answered by this campaign for any model; answering it requires re-running Condition A per model. We treat this as the principal design defect of the July 2026 campaign and flag every affected quantity accordingly.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figB_susceptibility.png}
\caption{Susceptibility margin $BS_C - BS_B$ per model over the full 30-event set, with $95\%$ percentile bootstrap confidence intervals ($B=20{,}000$). Values above zero indicate the swarm integrates the relevant round-30 injection better than the length-matched null injection. The margin is positive for all three architectures; only Mistral-7B's interval excludes zero.}
\label{fig:susceptibility}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Cross-Model Statistical Inference}
\label{sec:cross_model_stats}

\confoundnote{No two campaigns in this table share a serving stack and a
quantization level, and no two share a model generation either: Llama-3.1-8B is
AWQ-INT4 under vLLM (Jul 2024), Mistral-7B is \emph{unquantised FP16} under vLLM
(Sep 2023), and Qwen2.5-7B is GGUF~Q4\_K\_M under Ollama (Sep 2024). Model
generation, numerical precision and inference server therefore vary together
across every row, and none is held constant (Section~\ref{sec:serving_note},
Table~\ref{tab:provenance}). Differences \emph{between} rows are consequently not
attributable to architecture and are reported as descriptive only. Every quantity
here also comes from the single Evaluator~J pass whose schema-enforcement defect
is documented in Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}; switching evaluator reverses
the sign of one model's lift and flips which models' susceptibility intervals
exclude zero (Section~\ref{sec:replication_implications}). Within-row,
within-model contrasts---principally the Condition~B versus Condition~C
susceptibility margin---are the only comparisons this design supports, and even
those are subject to the shared-reference defect of
Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}.}

Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_stats} presents the aggregate statistical summary for all three models, computed over the full $E=30$ event set. Bootstrap confidence intervals use $B=10{,}000$ replicates. The Diebold-Mariano test is applied one-sided ($H_1$: $\mathbb{E}[d] > 0$, i.e., With-Sim lower loss than No-Sim).

Achieved power computed for two-sided $\alpha=0.05$ at the measured effect size and $E=30$ events. Column abbreviations: $\Delta_{C-B} =$ BS$_C-$BS$_B$ (the susceptibility margin); Lift $=$ Lift$_{A \to B}$; DM $=$ Diebold--Mariano statistic; $p =$ its one-sided $p$-value; Pwr.\ $=$ achieved power.}

\midrule
\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B}  & vLLM/OpenRouter\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} & 0.3846 & 0.6850 [0.538, 0.834] & 0.5633 & $-0.122$ [$-0.232$, $-0.022$] & $-0.300$ & $-3.36$ & 1.00 & $-0.613$ & 91.9\%\textsuperscript{$\ddagger$} & 0.467 \\
\textbf{Mistral-7B}    & vLLM, \textbf{FP16} & 0.3846 & 0.5312 [0.429, 0.626] & 0.6462 & $\mathbf{+0.115}$ [$+0.009$, $+0.225$] & $-0.147$ & $-1.65$ & 0.95 & $-0.301$ & 8.8\% & 0.567 \\
\textbf{Qwen2.5-7B}    & Ollama, GGUF Q4 & 0.3846 & 0.4814 [0.372, 0.590] & 0.5345 & $+0.053$ [$-0.064$, $+0.172$] & $-0.097$ & $-1.18$ & 0.88 & $-0.215$ & 8.5\% & \textbf{0.733} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}%
}
\smallskip\\
\footnotesize $BS_A$ is again identical across all three rows: Condition A is a single No-Sim record shared byte-for-byte across the campaigns, and the corrected 2026-08-16 repair (\S\ref{sec:outage}) restored it for the 24 events whose Condition A the first, defective repair had overwritten. \textsuperscript{$\dagger$}Llama's 24 repaired events were re-simulated via OpenRouter (\texttt{meta-llama/llama-3.1-8b-instruct}), not the local AWQ-INT4 vLLM serving used for its other 6 events and for both other campaigns throughout; this is a within-row serving-stack change, on top of the pre-existing cross-row confound of \S\ref{sec:serving_note}. \emph{Instrument caveat.} Those 24 events did not exist in July, so no scoring by the July local DeepSeek-R1-14B evaluator is possible for them; this row's $BS_B$ and $BS_C$ come from an August OpenRouter \texttt{deepseek-r1} pass while its $BS_A$ and the Mistral/Qwen rows remain July-scored. Llama's lift and susceptibility are therefore reported on an instrument-consistent basis (all three conditions read by one evaluator) in Table~\ref{tab:cutoff_lift} and \S\ref{sec:cutoff}, and the spread across the three August evaluators is given there; that spread is wide, and no single number should be read without it. The negative Lift$_{A \to B}$ indicates the With-Sim mean Brier score \emph{exceeded} that reference on aggregate, for Llama and Mistral; Qwen2.5-7B's CI still spans zero. The susceptibility margin $BS_C - BS_B$ is positive (favouring the hypothesis) for Mistral and Qwen2.5-7B; for Llama it is $-0.122$ under this evaluator but $+0.093$ [$-0.069$, $+0.271$] under the instrument-consistent pairing and $+0.027$ under the third, so its sign is an evaluator effect rather than a property of the data (\S\ref{sec:cutoff}). All DM $p$-values are non-significant ($p > 0.10$). We report $d_z$ (the paired standardised mean difference, $\bar{d}/s_d$) rather than the pooled two-sample $d$: Conditions A and B are evaluated on the same 30 events, so the design is within-subjects. \textsuperscript{$\ddagger$}Llama's achieved-power figure was recomputed 2026-08-16 from a normal approximation to the paired $t$ ($\text{power} \approx \Phi(|\text{DM}| - z_{0.975})$) rather than the pipeline's original (unlocated) method, so it is not exactly comparable to Mistral's and Qwen's $8.5$--$8.8\%$, which were not recomputed. It is high here only because Llama's observed effect is large; observed power restates the observed $p$-value and says nothing about the design, which remains critically underpowered---$E \geq 92$ would still be required for 80\% power at $d=0.5$ (\S\ref{sec:power_limitation}).
\end{table*}

Three key patterns emerge from the full-campaign data:

\textbf{Finding 1 --- The Susceptibility Ordering Holds for Two Models and Is Evaluator-Dependent for the Third:} The With-Sim condition (B) yields a lower mean Brier score than the Null Control condition (C) for Mistral-7B ($0.531$ vs.\ $0.646$, margin $+0.115$) and Qwen2.5-7B ($0.481$ vs.\ $0.535$, margin $+0.053$), the qualitative prediction of the Information Susceptibility hypothesis (Claim 3). For Llama-3.1-8B the sign depends on which evaluator reads the transcripts: $-0.122$ [$-0.232$, $-0.022$] under the \texttt{deepseek-r1} pass tabulated above, $+0.093$ [$-0.069$, $+0.271$] under \texttt{deepseek-v4-flash-0731}, and $+0.027$ under \texttt{gpt-5.6-luna-pro}---two of the three centred on the hypothesis' side, one excluding zero against it, on \emph{identical} simulation data. Because $BS_C - BS_B$ is a contrast between two simulated conditions and never touches Condition A, this spread is a property of the measuring instrument, not of the campaign; we report it as such rather than selecting a sign. Figure~\ref{fig:susceptibility} shows the margins with bootstrap confidence intervals. Only Mistral-7B's interval excludes zero on the hypothesis' side ($[+0.009, +0.225]$); Qwen's spans zero. At $E=30$ events the design cannot separate these effects from no effect, and \S\ref{sec:power_limitation} quantifies how little power is available.

We note a correction to earlier drafts of this analysis, which reported a sign reversal for Qwen2.5-7B ($-0.044$) and attributed it to that model's near-dormant social channel. That figure was the \texttt{content\_susceptibility.delta.B\_minus\_C} field of the pipeline summary, which measures the mean \emph{yes-probability} difference between conditions, not the Brier margin; the two are distinct metrics and were inadvertently mixed across rows of the same table. On the Brier metric used for the other two models, Qwen2.5-7B's margin is $+0.053$ and in the same direction as the rest. The dormant-channel observation (723 social actions, 2.74/DB) remains factually correct and is discussed in Appendix~\ref{sec:interaction_realism}, but it is no longer needed to explain a reversal that does not exist.

\textbf{Finding 2 --- Bimodal Per-Event Distribution (Mistral/Qwen; Not Llama):} A subset of high-information-gain events (T2, C11, S9, C8, C13, C5, S3) shows large positive lift consistently for Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B, neither of which was affected by the outage or its repair (e.g.\ T2 lift $+1.26$ and $+1.275$). It does not hold for Llama-3.1-8B, whose per-event lifts were entirely re-derived from the corrected re-simulation of \S\ref{sec:outage}: twenty-four of the thirty events, including T2, C11, C8, C13, C5 and C15, now rest on transcripts produced on a different serving stack and read by a different evaluator than the other two campaigns. Per-event agreement across models is therefore no longer an apples-to-apples comparison for this campaign, and we report the cluster as a Mistral/Qwen pattern rather than a cross-model one. The only Llama events that remain directly comparable are the six the outage never touched (C3, S2--S6), which is too few to establish or refute a bimodal structure.

\textbf{Finding 3 --- Directional Accuracy Divergence (Qwen Best):} While Brier scores aggregate probabilistic calibration, directional accuracy (whether the model correctly identifies the winning outcome) reveals architectural differences. Qwen-7B achieves the highest directional accuracy in Condition B (73.3\%), matching the No-Sim baseline identically ($\Delta = 0.0$), indicating the simulation process does not degrade winner identification capacity. Mistral-7B shows significant degradation ($73.3\% \to 56.7\%$, $\Delta = -16.7\%$), confirming that over-deliberation at $R=60$ actively corrupts its directional forecasts. Llama-3.1-8B's Condition A baseline is again the shared $73.3\%$ after the corrected repair restored it; against that reference its With-Sim directional accuracy is $46.7\%$ ($\Delta = -26.7\%$) under the evaluator that scored its re-simulated events, the largest degradation of the three---but see the instrument caveat in Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_stats}'s note, since that evaluator also scores the other conditions of this campaign more harshly in absolute terms.

\label{sec:taxonomy_breakdown}

subject to the same three-way confound as

stack vary together across the three campaigns. Statements below of the form
``model $X$ excels on category $Y$'' therefore describe what this campaign
recorded; they do not isolate architecture, and with $n = 7$--$15$ events per
category they are additionally underpowered. The category counts are also small
enough that a single flat unit---of which Llama-3.1-8B has $8$ and Mistral-7B $7$
in Condition~B under this evaluator (Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat})---moves a
category mean materially.}

Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy_brier} reports mean Condition B Brier scores by taxonomy category and Figure~\ref{fig:taxonomy_breakdown} plots them alongside directional accuracy. The partition is the three-way prefix scheme of Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy}, and---as noted there---``Senate/Binary'' is a question-format label rather than a topical one, so the category contrasts below should be read as descriptive rather than as a claim about subject matter.

\subsection{Calibration Quality Analysis}
\label{sec:calibration_analysis}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.65\textwidth]{fig4_calibration_overlay.png}
\caption{Calibration Curves: Condition B (With-Sim), all three models, $N=300$, $R=60$. All models fall above the perfect calibration diagonal in the middle range $[0.25, 0.75]$, indicating systematic \emph{under-confidence} (actual hit rates exceed predicted probabilities). The high bracket $[0.75, 1.0]$ achieves near-perfect calibration (100\% hit rate) across all models. Llama-8B achieves closest calibration at $[0.25, 0.5]$ bracket.}
\label{fig:calibration_overlay}
\end{figure}

\confoundnote{The calibration curves below are per-model and are subject to the
same three-way provenance confound as
Sections~\ref{sec:cross_model_stats}--\ref{sec:taxonomy_breakdown}; differences
between the three curves do not isolate architecture. Two further cautions apply
specifically here. The bracket occupancies are small ($n = 2$--$15$ per cell), so
several ``$100\%$ hit rate'' entries rest on two or three events. And the
probabilities being bracketed are the Evaluator~J outputs: because that
instrument returns an exactly uniform vector on $20.6\%$ of simulated units
(Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat}), probability mass is displaced toward the
middle brackets by the extraction defect itself, which is a competing explanation
for the ``systematic under-confidence'' read below.}

Table~\ref{tab:calibration_quality} reports the probability calibration bracket statistics for Condition B across all three models, and Figure~\ref{fig:calibration_overlay} overlays the corresponding calibration curves. An ideal calibration corresponds to predicted probability $\approx$ empirical rate within each bracket.

\label{tab:calibration_quality}

\toprule
 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Mistral-7B}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Qwen2.5-7B}} \\
\cmidrule(r){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5} \cmidrule(l){6-7}
\textbf{Bracket} & \textbf{Pred.} & \textbf{Actual} & \textbf{Pred.} & \textbf{Actual} & \textbf{Pred.} & \textbf{Actual} \\
\midrule
{[0.00--0.25)} & 0.219 & 1.000 ($n=2$) & 0.200 & 1.000 ($n=2$) & --- & --- \\
{[0.25--0.50)} & 0.353 & 0.833 ($n=12$) & 0.344 & 0.615 ($n=13$) & 0.368 & 0.692 ($n=13$) \\
{[0.50--0.75)} & 0.631 & 0.846 ($n=13$) & 0.599 & 0.667 ($n=12$) & 0.643 & 0.733 ($n=15$) \\

All three models show \textbf{systematic under-confidence in the middle brackets}: predicted probabilities in the [0.25--0.50) range consistently underestimate outcome frequency (empirical rates 61.5\%--83.3\% vs.\ predicted 34.4\%--36.8\%). This under-confidence compression is a hallmark of LLM ensemble calibration under social deliberation: when agents aggregate beliefs through social interaction, probability mass accumulates in the moderate-certainty range ($0.3$--$0.7$), creating predictions that are too uncertain relative to the empirical hit rate. The high bracket [0.75--1.00] shows near-perfect calibration across all models ($100\%$ hit rate), indicating that events where the swarm achieves near-certainty are genuinely high-confidence outcomes.


\parttitle{I}{The Instrument Failure}

\section{Loss of Sharpness and Degenerate Consensus: The Central Finding}
\label{sec:degenerate}

Part~I opens with the phenomenon itself, characterised exactly as we first observed it: a mechanism by which forecast informativeness was eroded in this campaign, discovered while auditing the aggregate lift reversal reported at face value in Section~\ref{sec:cross_model_stats}. The two effects below---flat, non-informative forecasts and a milder, pervasive loss of sharpness among the remaining forecasts---account for a substantial part of that reversal and require no claim about the swarm's reasoning quality having deteriorated. This section attributes them, provisionally, to the deliberation; Sections~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}--\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} then take that attribution apart and relocate it to the instrument, which is the paper's actual contribution. We present the two in that order because it is the order in which the evidence arrived, and because the provisional reading is the one a reader of the aggregate would reach unaided.

\subsection{Flat Forecasts}
\label{sec:degenerate_flat}

We define a unit as \emph{flat} when every option receives probability within $0.005$ of $1/K$, i.e.\ the extracted swarm forecast is indistinguishable from a uniform guess. Across the simulated conditions, $37$ of $180$ units ($20.6\%$) are flat: Llama-3.1-8B returns $8/30$ flat under Condition B and $10/30$ under Condition C; Mistral-7B returns $7/30$ and $12/30$; Qwen2.5-7B returns none. Only $1/30$ Condition A units is flat, for each model. Those are the counts as first observed, on the July campaign files; Llama's twenty-four outage-affected events have since been re-simulated and re-scored (\S\ref{sec:outage}), which moves its counts to $7/30$ and $7/30$ and the pooled total to $33/180$ ($18.3\%$). Mistral's and Qwen's are untouched, and the argument of this section---that flat units are concentrated in the simulated conditions and inflate $BS_B$ and $BS_C$ by construction---holds identically on either version. A flat unit is not a forecast---it is an extraction failure, or a swarm that reached no usable consensus---but the Brier score charges it the full uniform loss $1 - 1/K$, which for a binary event is $0.5$. Because flat units are concentrated in Conditions B and C and nearly absent from Condition A, they push $BS_B$ and $BS_C$ upward relative to $BS_A$ by construction. This alone is enough to generate a negative aggregate lift without any claim about deliberation degrading reasoning.

\textbf{The failure is not universal.} Flat forecasts are entirely absent for Qwen2.5-7B (0/30 under both simulated conditions) and concentrated in Llama-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B. An earlier draft of this section attributed this split to the benchmarked swarm model's own serving backend (vLLM for Llama/Mistral vs.\ Ollama/GGUF for Qwen) and speculated that vLLM's guided decoding~\cite{vllm_guided} was responsible. A subsequent source-code audit (Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}) shows this attribution is not supported: the \texttt{probabilities} field is produced by the fixed evaluator (DeepSeek-R1-14B, Section~\ref{sec:confound}), not by the swarm model being benchmarked, and that evaluator's structured-output enforcement is disabled for an unrelated reason---its model identifier matches a \texttt{"reasoning model"} keyword filter in the serving client---uniformly across all three campaigns regardless of which swarm model is under test. Since that condition is constant across Llama, Mistral and Qwen, it cannot by itself explain why only two of the three degenerate. We withdraw the vLLM-versus-Ollama account and, in its place, note that the one variable we can confirm differs sharply by swarm model is the volume of \texttt{evidence\_text} the unconstrained evaluator must compress into a single distribution: up to $20{,}329$ characters for Llama-3.1-8B against a fraction of that for the near-dormant Qwen2.5-7B social channel (Appendix~\ref{sec:interaction_realism}). Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} takes this from a conjecture to a measurement. Within Mistral-7B---the one campaign in which evidence length varies without also varying whether deliberation occurred---the flat rate rises monotonically from $5\%$ to $25\%$ to $65\%$ across terciles of \texttt{evidence\_text} length, while the same long transcripts, re-read by four independent Evaluator~A passes and by a second architecture, come back sharp. That was our reading for most of this work: evidence volume as the trigger, the schema-disabled evaluator as the locus. Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} tests both by assignment rather than by observation and supports neither---shortening a transcript does not un-flatten it, and toggling enforcement within one model changes nothing---while confirming that the choice of evaluator dominates both. We state the volume relationship here as the association it is, and withdraw the causal reading in \S\ref{sec:truncation_grid}. The confirmed and unconfirmed parts of this account are treated in full in Sections~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping} and~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism}; what remains open is what it is about this instrument in particular---its distillation, its quantisation, its local serving configuration---that makes it vulnerable where its relatives are not.

\subsection{Loss of Sharpness}
\label{sec:degenerate_sharpness}

Even among non-flat units, simulation systematically flattens the distribution. Restricting to units that are not exactly uniform---so that the effect cannot be an artifact of the flat units of Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat}, which carry a modal probability of $1/K$ by construction---the mean probability assigned to the modal option falls from $0.664$ under Condition A to $0.563$, $0.536$ and $0.538$ under Condition B for Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B, and falls further still under the null-injection Condition C ($0.538$, $0.475$, $0.488$). Computed over all units, as the aggregate Brier comparison implicitly does, the corresponding Condition B means are $0.658 \to 0.514$, $0.485$ and $0.538$; the gap between the two rows is the contribution of the flat units, so the two mechanisms compound rather than substitute. Since the Brier score is a strictly proper scoring rule, it rewards sharpness conditional on calibration~\cite{gneiting2007}: a forecast that hedges from $0.85$ to $0.55$ on an outcome that occurs is penalised, even though the direction is unchanged. Directional accuracy, which is insensitive to sharpness, barely moves for Llama-3.1-8B ($0.733 \to 0.717$) and not at all for Qwen2.5-7B ($0.733 \to 0.733$); Mistral-7B's larger drop ($0.733 \to 0.567$) is confounded with that model's older generation and full-precision serving (Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}) and should not be read as a general property of sharpness loss. Where directional accuracy is preserved, the divergence from the Brier trend is the signature of a sharpness loss rather than a reasoning failure: the swarm still knows which outcome is more likely, but states that knowledge less confidently than direct elicitation does.

Figure~\ref{fig:degenerate} shows both effects. Together they mean the headline quantity $BS_A - BS_B$ conflates three distinct things: genuine forecasting skill, extraction reliability, and forecast sharpness---and the second and third, not the first, are what this campaign's aggregate lift mostly measures. We recommend that future campaigns report flat-unit rate as a first-class diagnostic, exclude flat units from calibration analysis (or impute them explicitly), and decompose the Brier score into reliability, resolution and uncertainty components so that sharpness effects are separable from skill effects.

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figE_degenerate_forecasts.png}
\caption{Two mechanical drivers of the aggregate lift reversal. \textbf{Panel A:} percentage of events answered with an exactly uniform probability vector, by model and condition. Flat forecasts are rare under direct elicitation ($3\%$) and common under simulation (up to $40\%$) for the two vLLM-served models, and the Brier score charges each one the full uniform loss. \textbf{Panel B:} mean probability placed on the modal option. Simulation reduces forecast sharpness for every model, which a proper scoring rule penalises independently of whether the predicted direction is correct.}
\label{fig:degenerate}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{Model Provenance and Serving Heterogeneity: A Three-Way Confound}
\label{sec:serving_note}

Earlier drafts described the campaign as comparing three AWQ-quantised models, with a single documented exception for Qwen2.5-7B. An audit of the four production Slurm scripts against the run manifests shows that this description is incorrect in two further respects, and that the comparison is confounded along three axes simultaneously. Because this materially affects how the cross-model results should be read, we set out the actual configuration in Table~\ref{tab:provenance} and label models by their true weights throughout this revision.

\begin{table*}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Actual model provenance and serving stack, recovered from the production Slurm scripts. The ``label'' column gives the identifier written into the run manifests and used in earlier drafts; it is a \texttt{--served-model-name} string and does not describe the weights.}
\label{tab:provenance}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\begin{tabular}{llllll}
\toprule
\textbf{Manifest label} & \textbf{Actual weights} & \textbf{Released} & \textbf{Precision} & \textbf{Server} & \textbf{Campaign} \\
\midrule
\texttt{llama-3.1-8b-awq}  & \texttt{Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct-AWQ-INT4} & Jul 2024 & AWQ INT4 & vLLM   & 30 events, local \\
\texttt{mistral-7b-awq}    & \texttt{Mistral-7B-Instruct-\textbf{v0.1}}     & \textbf{Sep 2023} & \textbf{FP16} & vLLM & 30 events, local \\
\texttt{qwen2.5:7b}        & \texttt{qwen2.5:7b} (Ollama)                  & Sep 2024 & GGUF Q4\_K\_M & Ollama & 30 events, local \\
