\setlength{\tabcolsep}{6pt}
\end{table*}

Three corrections follow.

\textbf{(1) Mistral was not quantised.} The script named \texttt{run\_bench\_final\_mistral-7b-awq.slurm} carries the header comment \texttt{Quant: AWQ-INT4} and serves the model under the name \texttt{mistral-7b-awq}, but its \texttt{BENCH\_MODEL} path resolves to the unquantised \texttt{mistralai/Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1} snapshot and the vLLM invocation passes no \texttt{--quantization} flag. Mistral is therefore the only model in the campaign served at \emph{full precision}. The Llama script, by contrast, genuinely points at an AWQ-INT4 repository.

\textbf{(2) Mistral is a model generation older than the others.} \texttt{Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1} was released in September 2023, roughly ten months before Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and twelve before Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. This is by far the largest single difference between the models compared here.

\textbf{(3) Neither Qwen model was served through vLLM.} In both Qwen scripts the entire vLLM benchmark-server block is commented out and inference is provided by \texttt{ollama pull qwen2.5:7b} and \texttt{ollama pull qwen2.5:14b} respectively. The AWQ repository paths remain in the scripts as dead \texttt{BENCH\_MODEL} assignments, which is the likely origin of the ``Qwen2.5-14B-AWQ'' label used in earlier drafts. Qwen2.5-14B is GGUF via Ollama, not AWQ via vLLM.

\textbf{Consequence for interpretation.} Model generation, numerical precision and serving stack all vary together across the three campaigns, and none is held constant. This is a direct violation of the methodological-constants protocol of Section~\ref{sec:confound}, which the benchmark specification applies to the graph-building and evaluator models but which was not enforced for the benchmarked models themselves. The practical effect falls hardest on Mistral-7B, which records the worst Brier score ($0.531$), the largest directional-accuracy degradation ($-16.7$ points) and the interaction profile that earlier drafts characterised as ``antagonistic'' and explained by architecture-dependent over-deliberation. A far simpler competing explanation is now available and cannot be excluded by these data: it is a substantially older instruction-tuned model. We accordingly withdraw the architectural interpretation of Mistral's results in Appendices~\ref{sec:cross_model} and~\ref{sec:interaction_realism}, and downgrade all three-way model comparisons in this paper to \emph{descriptive}. Isolating architecture would require re-running the campaign with matched release generation and identical quantisation and serving stack across models---which is a tractable fix, since AWQ builds of all four models were already staged in the HPC cache.

\section{The Source-Code Audit: A Keyword Filter Disables Structured-Output Enforcement}
\label{sec:epistemic_mapping}

The benchmark specification defines a third measurement channel beyond the Brier score and the 7-dimension rubric: a \textbf{3-tier micro-question protocol} (``Epistemic Mapping,'' specification Architecture v3.2). For every event, three fact-grounded micro-questions are extracted from the seed context to probe whether the swarm's macro-prediction is supported by correct reasoning about the event's granular drivers---e.g., a predicted rate cut should co-occur with agent-level recognition of weak payroll data. Each micro-question is scored with a \texttt{dominant\_tag} and a \texttt{short\_rationale} grounded strictly in the simulation's discussion log, and the specification defines an \textbf{Epistemic Alignment Score} as the correlation between micro-question accuracy and macro-prediction calibration.

In the three July 2026 production campaigns that are this paper's primary evidence base, this channel produced no usable data: all $2{,}430$ micro-question instances ($3$ questions $\times$ $270$ units $\times$ $3$ models) return \texttt{dominant\_tag: "N/A"} and \texttt{short\_rationale: "Missing in LLM response"}. Taken alone, this would leave the failure's cause undetermined. It is not alone: development history on this cluster includes 63 earlier smoke-test and pilot runs that also populate this field, spanning April--July 2026, and auditing them resolves the question of \emph{where} the failure lives.

We partitioned all $63$ runs project-wide that carry the \texttt{micro\_epistemic\_mapping} field into two groups by their \texttt{benchmark\_model} string: $41$ runs whose model identifier follows OpenRouter's \texttt{provider/model} convention (e.g.\ \texttt{qwen/qwen-2.5-7b-instruct}, \texttt{google/gemma-4-26b-a4b-it}, \texttt{openai/gpt-oss-120b}, \texttt{anthropic/claude-3-haiku}, \texttt{deepseek/deepseek-v3.2}, \texttt{meta-llama/llama-3.1-8b-instruct:nitro}), consistent with cloud API routing via OpenRouter (15 of the 41 run directories are additionally named \texttt{*openrouter*} explicitly); and $22$ runs whose identifier follows the local \texttt{served-model-name} convention used by the production Slurm scripts (\texttt{llama3.1-8b-awq}, \texttt{qwen14b}, direct HPC \texttt{/scratch/.../snapshots/...} weight paths), consistent with local vLLM/Ollama serving on the HPC cluster. Manifests do not log an explicit API endpoint, so this classification is inferred from naming convention and directory context rather than read directly off a config field; we report it with that caveat.

\textbf{The result is a near-perfect split.} The $41$ OpenRouter-style runs return $237/237$ ($100\%$) usable micro-question answers across every model tested, with zero exceptions. The $22$ local-style runs return $6/966$ ($0.6\%$) usable answers; every one of the three July production campaigns is included in this group and each contributes exactly zero. The single partial exception is a $6/6$-successful run dated 8 May 2026 (\texttt{benchmark\_model: gemma27b}) launched from an HPC-resident Python environment; we cannot determine from its manifest alone whether its evaluator call was still cloud-routed, so we report it as an open exception rather than folding it into either count's interpretation. Excluding that one run, local serving is a \emph{total} failure and cloud serving is a \emph{total} success for this specific extraction task, across at least 15 distinct underlying models on the OpenRouter side and at least 4 distinct model families on the local side.

This resolves the question raised in earlier drafts of this section: the epistemic-mapping mechanism is not broken by design, and question generation, prompting, and rationale extraction all demonstrably work when the evaluator call is served through OpenRouter. \texttt{evaluator\_fallback\_used} remains \texttt{False} for every affected local unit, so the pipeline's own error detection does not distinguish this near-total local failure from a successful call---the same silent-placeholder pattern documented for the JSD telemetry (Section~\ref{sec:jsd_convergence}).

\textbf{Source-code audit: the actual mechanism, and a correction to an earlier draft of this section.} An earlier draft of this paper attributed the split above to a base-URL misconfiguration (\texttt{OPENROUTER\_BASE\_URL} pointed at the wrong local server). Having subsequently obtained access to the evaluator's source, we withdraw that explanation: \texttt{BenchmarkRoleRouter.client\_for("evaluator")} (\texttt{role\_router.py}) correctly prioritises \texttt{EVALUATOR\_LLM\_BASE\_URL} for the evaluator role, so the evaluator's requests are not misrouted. The real mechanism is different, is confirmed directly from source rather than inferred, and implicates a keyword-matching heuristic rather than the evaluator model's competence. In \texttt{LLMClient.chat\_json()} (\texttt{llm\_client.py}):
\begin{quote}
\texttt{is\_reasoning\_model = self.model and any(kw in self.model.lower() for kw in ("r1", "reasoning", "deepseek-r1"))} \\
\texttt{if is\_reasoning\_model: json\_schema = None}
\end{quote}
followed by
\begin{quote}
\texttt{if (is\_local\_vllm and is\_quantized) or is\_reasoning\_model: response\_format = None; use\_schema = False}
\end{quote}
The fixed evaluator's model identifier, \texttt{"deepseek-r1-14b"}, contains \texttt{"r1"} and matches this filter. Consequently \emph{every} evaluator call in all three production campaigns runs with \texttt{response\_format = None}---not merely without \texttt{json\_schema} structured-output enforcement, but without even the weaker \texttt{json\_object} guarantee. The model must reproduce the full nested schema (\texttt{probabilities}, \texttt{mcq\_dimensions}, \texttt{validated\_scales}, and, when applicable, \texttt{micro\_epistemic\_mapping}) from natural-language instructions alone, in a single unconstrained generation.

Two further details, also read directly from \texttt{ProbabilityEvaluator.evaluate()} (\texttt{evaluator.py}), explain why the four fields fail differently rather than uniformly. First, a retry loop (up to three attempts, with escalating temperature and error feedback) checks response completeness before accepting it---but the check tests only \texttt{probabilities} and \texttt{mcq\_dimensions}:
\begin{quote}
\texttt{if not isinstance(probs, dict) or not probs or ...: raise ValueError(...)} \\
\texttt{if not isinstance(mcq, dict) or not mcq or len(mcq) < len(MCQ\_DIMENSION\_KEYS): raise ValueError(...)}
\end{quote}
\texttt{micro\_epistemic\_mapping} is never examined by this check, so a response with an empty or incomplete mapping is accepted as successful and the loop exits without ever prompting the model to retry that specific field. Second, \texttt{\_validate\_micro\_mapping()} then silently backfills every micro-question the returned mapping does not cover with \texttt{\{"dominant\_tag": "N/A", "short\_rationale": "Missing in LLM response"\}}, which is written into the result as if it were a normal value---no exception is raised and \texttt{evaluator\_fallback\_used} is never set. \texttt{probabilities} and \texttt{mcq\_dimensions}, by contrast, are retried and are the two fields most directly and repeatedly emphasised in the prompt (\S\ref{sec:grading}), so they are structurally present in the released data even without schema enforcement; \texttt{micro\_epistemic\_mapping} additionally requires the model to reproduce \emph{event-specific, dynamically named} keys (e.g.\ \texttt{S3\_q1\_pres\_prob}) with no structural scaffolding forcing it to do so, which is a plausible reason it is the field most often dropped entirely.

This also reframes the OpenRouter/local split reported above: it does not appear to be a local-versus-cloud serving effect as such. \texttt{is\_reasoning\_model} is evaluated on \emph{whichever model string is configured for the evaluator role}, regardless of where that model is served. In the 63-run audit, the production campaigns' evaluator was fixed to \texttt{"deepseek-r1-14b"} throughout (Section~\ref{sec:confound}), while the OpenRouter-style pilot runs generally used the model under test itself as its own evaluator (\texttt{OPENROUTER\_EVALUATOR\_MODEL} set to the same value as \texttt{OPENROUTER\_BENCHMARK\_MODEL}), and none of \texttt{qwen/\allowbreak qwen-2.5-7b-instruct}, \texttt{google/\allowbreak gemma-4-26b-a4b-it}, \texttt{openai/\allowbreak gpt-oss-120b}, \texttt{anthropic/\allowbreak claude-3-haiku}, or \texttt{deepseek/\allowbreak deepseek-v3.2} matches the \texttt{"r1"/"reasoning"} filter. Serving location and schema-enforcement status were confounded in this dataset because no run in our audit paired a reasoning-named evaluator with cloud serving, or a non-reasoning-named evaluator with local serving; we cannot rule out an additional, independent local-serving effect, but the schema-disabling filter is sufficient on its own to explain the near-total local failure and does not require one.

\textbf{This same mechanism plausibly explains the low inter-rater reliability of the 7-dimension rubric (Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_reliability}), not just the missing micro-question data.} The pipeline computes that reliability by calling \texttt{evaluator.evaluate()} \emph{twice} per unit (\texttt{run1}, \texttt{run2}; \texttt{run\_ecnbench\_protocol.py}) and comparing the dominant bucket label each run assigns to each dimension---both calls use the identical evaluator client, and both therefore run with \texttt{response\_format = None} for the same reason. What Cohen's $\kappa$ measures in this campaign is consequently \emph{generation-to-generation consistency of an unconstrained model reproducing a bucket label from a natural-language instruction}, not disagreement between genuinely independent raters about an underlying construct. Unlike \texttt{micro\_epistemic\_mapping}, \texttt{mcq\_dimensions} is retried until structurally complete, so it is present in both runs, but nothing in this design constrains its \emph{content} to be stable across the two generations---which is precisely the signature low-$\kappa$, structurally-complete-but-noisy pattern reported in Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_reliability} ($\max \kappa = 0.322$, every dimension below the reliability threshold). We did not rerun the campaign with schema enforcement restored to confirm this quantitatively, and flag doing so as a priority diagnostic (Section~\ref{sec:conclusion}), but the mechanism is a direct, verified reading of the scoring code rather than a new hypothesis.

\textbf{What this audit does and does not settle, and what the rest of Part~I adds.} The defect above is established directly from source and is not in doubt. What it cannot do on its own is explain the flat-forecast finding of Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat}. The final \texttt{probabilities} used for Brier scoring are produced by this same schema-disabled call, so the absence of structural enforcement is a plausible contributing condition there too; but \texttt{is\_reasoning\_model} is constant across all three production campaigns (the evaluator is always \texttt{"deepseek-r1-14b"}), whereas the flat-forecast rate is not (Llama-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B are affected, Qwen2.5-7B is not), so schema-disabling alone cannot be the \emph{differentiating} cause. The audit therefore establishes the evaluator as a suspect but not as the cause, and it supersedes the vLLM-versus-Ollama framing tentatively offered in Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat}, which the source does not support as the operative mechanism. The remaining candidate the source does suggest is \texttt{evidence\_text} volume, which differs sharply by swarm model (up to $20{,}329$ characters for Llama-3.1-8B versus a fraction of that for the near-dormant Qwen2.5-7B social channel, Appendix~\ref{sec:interaction_realism}): an unconstrained evaluator asked to compress more, and more heterogeneous, discussion content into a single distribution may hedge toward uniform more often. Earlier drafts left that as an untested hypothesis. Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication} tests it by changing the instrument while holding the transcripts fixed, and Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} measures the dose--response directly; together they convert it into the paper's central mechanism.

\section{Evaluator Replication: Is Degenerate Consensus a Property of the Swarm or of the Instrument?}
\label{sec:evaluator_replication}

Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat} left one question open and named it the highest-priority follow-up: the flat-forecast rate differs sharply between swarm models, yet the mechanism we can confirm at the level of source code---an evaluator whose structured-output enforcement is silently disabled by a keyword filter---is \emph{constant} across all three campaigns and therefore cannot explain a difference between them. That audit established the evaluator as a suspect but could not establish it as a cause, because the campaigns contain only one evaluator pass. This section closes that gap with the experiment the audit implied: we re-read the same simulation transcripts with a different evaluator and ask which of the paper's findings survive.

\subsection{Design}
\label{sec:replication_design}

The evaluator's input is the per-unit \texttt{evidence\_text} record---the seed dossier for Condition A, the simulation transcript for Conditions B and C---together with the event question and option list. Those records are fixed artifacts of the July campaigns. Re-running only the evaluator over them therefore holds the entire simulation constant and varies nothing but the measuring instrument. We denote the two passes:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Evaluator J} (July 2026): DeepSeek-R1-14B, served locally, structured-output enforcement disabled by the keyword filter documented in Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}. This is the instrument behind every number reported in Sections~\ref{sec:full_campaign}--\ref{sec:degenerate}.
    \item \textbf{Evaluator A} (August 2026): \texttt{deepseek/deepseek-v4-flash-0731} via the OpenRouter API, JSON-object enforcement active, temperature $0.0$ and seed $42$ pinned by the benchmark's determinism harness.
\end{itemize}

All $270$ units of the three scorable campaigns were re-evaluated. Every derived quantity reported below---Brier, RPS, directional accuracy, calibration bracket---is recomputed from each pass's own \texttt{probabilities} vector using the protocol runner's own scoring functions, so no comparison can be contaminated by a metric left over from the other pass.\footnote{This is not a hypothetical hazard. The re-evaluation script updates \texttt{probabilities} but not the derived metrics, which left the working copies internally inconsistent in $82$--$88$ of $90$ units per campaign until the two passes were separated into distinct files. Both datasets are released separately and each is internally consistent in $90/90$ units; the reconciliation utility and its audit output are included in the release (Appendix~\ref{sec:data_audit}).}

Two notes on the conduct of the replication. First, the pass was initially launched against a stale copy of the event catalogue in which the identifiers C6, T1, T3, T4, T5, T6 and T8 carry entirely different events, so $21$ of $90$ units per campaign received another event's micro-questions. Those $63$ units were re-evaluated against the authoritative catalogue and every unit in the released Dataset A now carries the micro-question set its own event defines; the correction is verifiable with the released checker, which reports zero remaining mismatches. Re-running them also changed the headline degeneracy count, which we treat as evidence about the instrument rather than a footnote, and discuss in Section~\ref{sec:replication_central}. Second, Qwen2.5-14B remains unscorable under either evaluator: $87$ of its $90$ records carry no \texttt{ground\_truth} value, so it is excluded here exactly as in Section~\ref{sec:full_campaign}, and its micro-question channel was left uncorrected for the same reason.

\subsection{The Central Finding Does Not Replicate}
\label{sec:replication_central}

Table~\ref{tab:replication} and Figure~\ref{fig:replication} give the result. Because the two passes must read identical input for the contrast to isolate the instrument, we fix the scope before quoting any number: Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B contribute all $30$ of their events, while Llama-3.1-8B contributes only the six the backend outage never touched (C3, S2--S6; \S\ref{sec:outage}). Its remaining $24$ events were re-simulated on 2026-08-16 and no July scoring of them exists, so they cannot enter a J-versus-A comparison at all and are reported instead in the table's third column, under the August evaluator alone.

On the strictly paired Mistral-plus-Qwen material the flat-forecast rate over simulated units falls from $19/120$ ($15.8\%$) under Evaluator J to $1/120$ ($0.8\%$) under Evaluator A, and the cross-model pattern that Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat} set out to explain does not survive: Condition B counts of $7/30$ and $0/30$ become $0/30$ and $0/30$. Degeneracy is eliminated outright for Mistral-7B, so the contrast between the vLLM-served Mistral and the Ollama-served Qwen---the observation the evidence-volume hypothesis was introduced to account for---is not a stable property of the transcripts. The single surviving flat unit is a Mistral Condition C unit. Condition C is the null-injection arm, in which the swarm is fed structurally identical but topically unrelated evidence, and a uniform posterior is the \emph{correct} response to evidence carrying no information about the question, so not one unconstrained-evaluator flat forecast in a condition where information \emph{was} injected survives the constrained instrument in either campaign.

Llama's six paired events make the same point far more sharply on far less data. Under Evaluator J every one of their $12$ simulated units is flat---$6/6$ in Condition B and $6/6$ in Condition C---and one of the six Condition A units is flat as well. Under Evaluator A none of the $13$ is. That is a total reversal on byte-identical text, and it is the single clearest instance of the instrument effect anywhere in this paper, but the subset it rests on is small and not randomly chosen: these are exactly the units that ran before the serving failure, so they are also Llama's only high-evidence-volume units (Appendix~\ref{sec:evidence_depth}), and flatness is confounded with evidence volume within the campaign. We therefore read the six events as a demonstration that the effect exists and can be complete, not as an estimate of how large it is.

A further, sharper lesson about the instrument came from an accident of our own analysis, which we record because it caught us mid-draft. An earlier version of this section reported $0/30$ Condition B flat units for all three models. Those numbers came from an Evaluator A pass in which $21$ units per campaign had been scored against the wrong event's micro-questions (Section~\ref{sec:replication_design}); re-running exactly those units against the correct catalogue changed Llama-3.1-8B's Condition B count over its then-30-event set from $0$ to $4$, and moved its Condition C count from $9$ to $6$. Two things differ between those passes---the micro-question channel was corrected, and the units were sampled afresh from a non-deterministic evaluator---and this design cannot separate them. Either way the conclusion is the one Section~\ref{sec:replication_noise} quantifies directly: a re-run of the same evaluator on the same transcripts moved the paper's headline degeneracy count by four units out of thirty. A single evaluator pass, of either instrument, does not measure this quantity to the precision at which the paper's original cross-model comparison was read. Those particular counts are historical: they were taken over Llama's outage-corrupted transcripts, which the repair of \S\ref{sec:outage} has since replaced, and they are quoted here as evidence about the evaluator's stability rather than as measurements of the campaign.

The milder sharpness effect attenuates in the same direction without vanishing, and it supplies its own instrument check. Under Evaluator J the Condition A mean modal probability is a single number common to all three campaigns by construction---$0.658$ over $30$ events, $0.617$ over Llama's six---because Condition A is one shared No-Sim record (Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}). Under Evaluator A that single input yields $0.713$, $0.668$ and $0.672$ on those same six events, and $0.722$ and $0.687$ for Mistral and Qwen over all $30$: the constrained instrument does not return one answer to one text, which is the instrument noise Section~\ref{sec:replication_noise} quantifies. Each within-model Condition A$\to$B drop is therefore computed against its own campaign's Condition A. Those drops shrink from $-0.200$, $-0.173$ and $-0.120$ under Evaluator J to $+0.057$, $-0.045$ and $-0.018$ under Evaluator A---for Llama the drop does not merely shrink but reverses, the swarm's simulated forecast coming out \emph{sharper} than the No-Sim reference. The aggregate lift reversal that motivated the whole investigation is no longer uniform in sign: $BS_A - BS_B$ is $-0.033$ $[-0.685, +0.544]$ for Llama-3.1-8B on its six paired events, $-0.260$ $[-0.465, -0.060]$ for Mistral-7B and $+0.035$ $[-0.103, +0.182]$ for Qwen2.5-7B, and only Mistral's interval excludes zero---the model already identified in Section~\ref{sec:serving_note} as confounded by release generation. On the separate recovery scope, Llama's $24$ re-simulated events give $-0.235$ $[-0.375, -0.100]$ under the same August evaluator; that interval excludes zero, but it is a measurement of a different quantity---a model on new transcripts, not an instrument on fixed ones---and Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff} rather than this section is where it is interpreted.

 & Llama-3.1-8B\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} & --- & --- & $+0.505$ / $+0.889$ / $+0.407$ \\

Those six events are exactly the degenerate core described in \S\ref{sec:cutoff}, which is why Evaluator J returns a uniform vector for $6/6$ of them in both simulated conditions while Evaluator A returns a non-uniform vector for every one---the sharpest single instance of the instrument effect in the paper, but measured on a small and adversarially selected subset, so its bootstrap intervals are correspondingly wide and we draw no effect-size conclusion from them. The two Llama scopes must not be pooled or read as a time series. The OR column additionally carries the serving-stack substitution of \S\ref{sec:outage} and the raised evaluator completion budget documented in item~(5) of \S\ref{sec:outage}; round-level convergence telemetry was not extracted from the new traces, so the JSD quantities of \S\ref{sec:jsd_convergence} are \emph{unavailable} for those $24$ events rather than placeholder-valued.

Under the constrained August instrument the counts are $0$, $1$ and $0$: the single survivor is a Condition C unit, the null-injection arm in which a uniform posterior is the warranted answer. \textbf{Panel B:} the B-versus-C susceptibility margin $BS_C - BS_B$ per event on Llama-3.1-8B's $24$ re-simulated events under \texttt{deepseek-v4-flash-0731}, the scope in which that model's benchmark result is actually measured. \subsection{Neither Pass Is a Reference Measurement}
\label{sec:replication_noise}

It would be a mistake to read Section~\ref{sec:replication_central} as showing that Evaluator A is correct and Evaluator J was broken. Evaluator A is itself an unstable instrument, and the campaigns contain a clean way to demonstrate it.

Condition A carries no simulation: its \texttt{evidence\_text} is the seed dossier, and it is byte-identical across all three campaigns for all $30$ events. This remains true after the outage repair, which restored Condition A from the shared record rather than re-eliciting it: we verified that the Condition A rows of both the primary and the replication result files hash identically across the three campaigns on $30/30$ events, and that the repair left Llama's Condition A probability vectors bit-for-bit unchanged in both files. Because the re-evaluation was launched separately per campaign, Evaluator A therefore read each of those $30$ inputs three independent times, at temperature $0.0$ with seed $42$ pinned. If the instrument were reproducible, the three readings would agree exactly. They agree exactly for $4$ of $30$ events. The mean spread in Brier across the three re-reads is $0.129$ and the maximum is $0.715$---a range exceeding the entire between-condition effect the paper reports. Figure~\ref{fig:instrument_noise}B shows the full distribution. The corresponding count for Evaluator J is $30/30$, but this carries no information about its stability: Condition A was executed once and copied verbatim into all three campaigns (Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}), so the agreement is an artifact of file reuse rather than a replication.

Agreement between the two passes is likewise only moderate. Over the $198$ units of the paired scope, $r(BS_J, BS_A) = +0.552$, and the per-model figures are strikingly uniform: $+0.589$ for Llama-3.1-8B (on its $18$ paired units), $+0.587$ for Mistral-7B and $+0.549$ for Qwen2.5-7B. An earlier draft reported a pooled $+0.415$ with Llama at $+0.144$ and attributed the gap to something specific about that campaign; that comparison was invalid, because it scored Evaluator J's July readings against Evaluator A's readings of the \emph{re-simulated} transcripts, so it measured the repair rather than the instrument. Restricted to matched input the anomaly disappears, and the honest statement is the stronger one: the two instruments correlate at about $+0.55$ regardless of which model's transcripts they read. Mean Brier falls under Evaluator A for the two campaigns measured over all $30$ events ($0.521 \to 0.409$ for Mistral, $0.467 \to 0.334$ for Qwen) and \emph{rises} for Llama on its six paired events ($0.530 \to 0.601$), driven by Condition C, where replacing six uniform vectors with confident ones that happen to be wrong takes the mean from $0.583$ to $0.927$. Either way the choice of evaluator moves the benchmark's headline score by $0.07$ to $0.13$ Brier---larger than any of the between-condition lifts the benchmark was built to detect, and not even reliably in one direction. Agreement on the modal option, counting a tie as a disagreement, is $5/18$ for Llama, $50/90$ for Mistral and $63/90$ for Qwen, $118/198$ pooled: on a little over half the units the two instruments do not even name the same outcome as most likely.

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figR2_instrument_noise.png}
\caption{The evaluator as a source of variance. \textbf{Panel A:} per-unit Brier under the two evaluators, restricted to the $198$ units both passes read byte-identically---all $30$ events of Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B plus Llama-3.1-8B's six outage-free events (\S\ref{sec:outage}); agreement is moderate ($r = +0.552$) and the scatter is not concentrated near the identity line. \textbf{Panel B:} Condition A's seed dossier is byte-identical across the three campaigns, so the August evaluator read each of the $30$ inputs three times at temperature $0$. Only $4$ events reproduce exactly; the largest Brier spread across three re-reads of the same text is $0.715$.}
\label{fig:instrument_noise}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{What This Means for the Paper's Claims}
\label{sec:replication_implications}

We read this replication as answering the question posed in Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat}, and answering it against the interpretation that section preferred.

\textbf{Degenerate consensus is substantially a property of the measurement, not of the deliberation.} The flat forecasts that motivated this paper are produced when an unconstrained evaluator is asked to compress a long transcript into a categorical distribution. On the strictly paired material a second evaluator recovers one twentieth of them ($1/120$ against $19/120$ for Mistral and Qwen, $0/12$ against $12/12$ for Llama's six outage-free events) and does not reproduce their cross-model structure at all: the Condition B counts of $6/6$, $7/30$ and $0/30$ across Llama, Mistral and Qwen all become zero. The audit in Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping} identified the disabled structured-output enforcement as the confirmed source-level defect but could not attribute the cross-model difference to it. Section~\ref{sec:replication_central} supplies the missing evidence, in the negative direction the audit suspected: with enforcement active the difference the evidence-volume hypothesis was built to explain is no longer there to explain. We accordingly restate the paper's central finding as a finding about instrumentation: \emph{iterative multi-agent deliberation produces transcripts that an unconstrained LLM evaluator frequently fails to reduce to an informative distribution, and that failure is indistinguishable, in the aggregate Brier score, from the swarm having reached no consensus.} The phenomenon is real, reproducible from the released artifacts, and consequential for anyone scoring a multi-agent simulation this way. We stop short of locating it wholly in the evaluator, but the evidence for a residual is now weaker than in earlier drafts: no Condition B unit anywhere---not in the $180$ paired units, not in the $24$ re-simulated Llama events---returns a uniform vector under the constrained instrument, and the two flat units that do survive are both in Condition C, where uniformity is the warranted answer. A contribution from the deliberation itself is not excluded; it is simply no longer visible above an instrument with this reproducibility.

\textbf{The benchmark's three original hypotheses are not identifiable at this evaluator noise level.} Section~\ref{sec:cross_model_stats} already reported $\leq 9\%$ achieved power at $E = 30$. Table~\ref{tab:replication} adds a second, independent obstacle: switching evaluator on fixed transcripts moves Qwen2.5-7B's lift from $-0.097$ to $+0.035$---a sign reversal---and moves the set of models whose susceptibility interval excludes zero from $\{$Mistral$\}$ to $\{$Qwen$\}$, i.e.\ to a disjoint set. Llama's susceptibility over the same six events goes from exactly $+0.000$ (both simulated conditions uniform, so the contrast is identically zero by construction) to $+0.472$ with an interval that still spans zero. Both passes are single measurements with an instrument whose reproducibility on byte-identical input is $4/30$. Neither the lift claim, the convergence claim nor the susceptibility claim can be settled by either dataset, and we withdraw any reading of Section~\ref{sec:cross_model_stats} that treats the sign of a single-pass lift as evidence about deliberation.

\textbf{What survives unchanged.} Three results in this paper do not depend on which evaluator read the transcripts. The JSD convergence telemetry defect is simulation-side: the constant $0.7583$ placeholder occupies $87.7\%$, $40.7\%$ and $27.7\%$ of Llama, Mistral and Qwen checkpoints in the raw records, exactly as reported in Section~\ref{sec:jsd_convergence}. (Llama's figure describes the archived pre-repair campaign, whose $24$ outage-affected events \S\ref{sec:outage} has since replaced; the re-simulation did not extract round-level telemetry, so those events now carry no JSD series at all rather than a placeholder one, and the defect is neither reproduced nor refuted by the new data.) The difficulty--lift correlation remains an arithmetic artifact under every pass: pooled over the $60$ strictly paired Mistral and Qwen events, $r(BS_A, \text{lift})$ is $+0.774$ against a mechanical expectation of $+0.809$ under Evaluator J and $+0.573$ against $+0.619$ under Evaluator A, while the non-tautological association $r(BS_A, BS_B)$ stays negligible ($+0.055$ and $+0.056$). One qualification belongs here rather than in a footnote: on Llama's $24$ re-simulated events the observed $r(BS_A, \text{lift})$ of $+0.505$ falls well \emph{below} its mechanical expectation of $+0.889$, because $r(BS_A, BS_B) = +0.407$ there---the first campaign in this paper in which the two conditions' difficulties are substantially associated. At $n=24$ on a single evaluator pass we do not read that as a finding, but it is the one place where the tautology argument does not fully account for the observed correlation, and it should be checked rather than assumed in any follow-up. And the provenance confound of Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}---three models differing simultaneously in release generation, precision and serving stack---is a property of the experimental design, not of any measurement taken from it.

\textbf{Recommendation for future campaigns.} A benchmark that extracts probabilities with an LLM must treat that extractor as an instrument requiring calibration, not as an oracle. Table~\ref{tab:benchmark_checklist} states this as a checklist: four gates we would now block a campaign on, and three quantities we would now refuse to interpret a score without. Each row is stated together with the specific failure in this paper it would have caught, because every one of them is a lesson we paid for rather than a generic recommendation. Four points deserve emphasis in prose. Verify at runtime that structured-output enforcement is actually in force rather than assuming it from a request parameter, since the defect documented in Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping} was a silent keyword-match bypass. Derive completion flags from the artifacts rather than from the orchestrator, since a pipeline that sets its own success fields will report a dead backend as a successful experiment. Run the evaluator $k \geq 3$ times per unit and report the between-run spread alongside every score, so that a $0.715$ Brier range on identical input cannot be mistaken for an effect. And score every campaign with at least two independent evaluators, treating any finding that does not survive both as a property of the instrument until shown otherwise. We single out the flat-unit rate as the cheapest useful diagnostic of the set: it separated a measurement artifact from a substantive result here, and it costs nothing to compute from data every such benchmark already has.

\begin{table*}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{A checklist for benchmarks that extract probabilities with an LLM. Each row states a check, the failure it would have caught \emph{in this paper}, and what it costs to run. Every one of these was learned by having the corresponding failure go undetected here; the ``caught it here'' column cites the section where the damage is quantified, so the checklist is an account of this campaign rather than a set of generic best practices. We recommend treating the first four as blocking gates on any campaign that reports a score, and the last three as reporting requirements.}
\label{tab:benchmark_checklist}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
% Narrow p{} columns cannot be justified without loose lines; ragged right.
\begin{tabular}{p{0.06\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.29\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.40\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\textwidth}}
\toprule
& \textbf{Check} & \textbf{What it would have caught here} & \textbf{Cost} \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{4}{l}{\emph{Blocking gates --- run before any number leaves the pipeline}} \\
\midrule
G1 & \textbf{Verify structured-output enforcement at runtime}, by asserting on the request actually sent, not on the parameter you passed. & A keyword filter matching \texttt{"r1"} in the evaluator's model id silently dropped \texttt{response\_format} for every unit of every July campaign, and nothing in the logs or the result records recorded it (\S\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}). We list this as a gate because a pipeline must know what it sent, \emph{not} because we can show enforcement prevents degeneracy: isolated within one evaluator it does not (\S\ref{sec:truncation_grid}). & One assertion per request. \\
\addlinespace
G2 & \textbf{Derive completion flags from the artifacts}, never from the orchestrator. Require a minimum count of successful telemetry probes before a unit is scored. & $69$ units recorded \texttt{simulation\_status: "completed"}, \texttt{error: null} and \texttt{simulation\_executed: true} while their serving backend had been dead for $23$ events (\S\ref{sec:outage}). The failure was invisible in every field a consumer would check. & One count per unit. \\
\addlinespace
G3 & \textbf{Assert that the scored record's question and options match the catalogue entry} its event id resolves to, by value and not by id. & Seven event ids denote different events in two catalogue copies; two of those collisions (T1, T5) share option set \emph{and} ground truth with the intended events, so they pass every id-level and schema-level check while simulating an unrelated question (\S\ref{sec:outage}). & One string comparison per unit. \\
\addlinespace
G4 & \textbf{Assert that the evidence field is non-empty and of plausible length} before scoring, and log its length with the score. & A repair script read \texttt{question} and \texttt{evidence\_text} from a config file that stores neither, so $72$ units were scored on an empty evidence string---the three conditions differing by one character---and produced two publishable-looking findings before being caught (\S\ref{sec:outage}). & One length check per unit. \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{4}{l}{\emph{Reporting requirements --- run before any number is interpreted}} \\
\midrule
R1 & \textbf{Report the flat-unit rate} (share of forecasts within $\epsilon$ of uniform) per condition, always, alongside the aggregate score. & It is the single diagnostic that separated a measurement artifact from a substantive result here, and the only one that made the artifact legible as an artifact rather than as a null effect (\S\ref{sec:degenerate_flat}). & Free; it is a function of data you already have. \\
\addlinespace
R2 & \textbf{Run the evaluator $k \geq 3$ times per unit} and report the between-run spread with every score. & Three re-reads of byte-identical input at temperature $0$ reproduce exactly $4$ of $30$ units, with a maximum Brier spread of $0.715$---larger than any between-condition effect the benchmark was built to detect (\S\ref{sec:replication_noise}). & $k\times$ evaluator cost. \\
\addlinespace
R3 & \textbf{Score with at least two independent evaluators} and treat any finding that does not survive both as a property of the instrument until shown otherwise. & Switching evaluator on fixed transcripts reverses the sign of one model's lift and moves the set of models with a susceptibility interval excluding zero to a disjoint set (\S\ref{sec:replication_central}). & $2\times$ evaluator cost. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{6pt}
\end{table*}

\section{Carrying Out the Calibration Protocol: Four Models, Five Reads, Contamination Controlled}
\label{sec:four_model_extension}

\confoundnote{This is the best-controlled comparison in the paper---five
evaluator reads per unit, contamination-clean events only---and it is still not a
controlled model comparison. The four campaigns differ in release generation,
precision and serving stack (Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}): Llama-3.1-8B is
AWQ-INT4 under vLLM, Mistral-7B unquantised FP16 under vLLM, and both Qwen models
GGUF under Ollama. Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen2.5-14B are the only pair sharing family,
release date and inference server, and even there the $14$B campaign's exact
quantization level is not recorded in its manifest, so a like-for-like reading is
available for that pair alone and only approximately. Rankings below are reported
as descriptive; $20$ events also leave every per-model difference underpowered,
which is why the analysis of Section~\ref{sec:predictive_signal} rests on the
pooled figure rather than on any single row.}

We carried out the preceding recommendation in full. Every unit of the three originally-scorable campaigns was re-read three further times with Evaluator A (\texttt{deepseek/deepseek-v4-flash-0731}), giving four independent Evaluator~A reads per unit---the single pass of Section~\ref{sec:replication_central} plus three more---and once with a fourth, architecturally unrelated model, \texttt{openai/gpt-5.6-luna-pro} (Evaluator C). We also recovered Qwen2.5-14B in two stages. First, its $87$ empty-\texttt{ground\_truth} records (Appendix~\ref{sec:fourth_model_status}) were backfilled from the authoritative catalogue, but $6$ of its $30$ events were found to have been dropped because the same stale-catalogue collision documented in Section~\ref{sec:replication_design} had, for this campaign, reached further upstream than a mislabelled micro-question: the swarm itself deliberated on the wrong topic entirely (event~T3's transcript debated a Bitcoin price threshold, not the 2024 Taiwanese presidential election its correct dossier specifies), leaving a $24$-event campaign. Second, 2026-08-21: those six events (T3, T4, T5, T6, C6, T8) were re-simulated end-to-end---300 agents, 60 rounds, all three conditions---against the correct root catalogue, and rescored under all five evaluator passes (primary \texttt{deepseek/deepseek-r1}, three Evaluator~A repeats, Evaluator~C). A first re-simulation attempt reproduced the identical stale-catalogue bug (verified: T3 again resolved to the Bitcoin question) because the launch script still resolved \texttt{--events-raw} to the wrong catalogue by a relative path; the corrected launch fixed only that path, leaving the injection bank deliberately unchanged (\S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status}). Qwen2.5-14B is now a full $30$-event, $90$-unit campaign, scored the same way as the other three models.

Because Condition~A's shared dossier makes raw cross-model Brier comparisons vulnerable to the contamination effect of Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff}, we restrict the four-way comparison below to the $20$ events resolving after every model's public release date that survive in all four campaigns---the strictest contamination control applied to any comparison in this paper. With Qwen2.5-14B now complete, this is the full globally-clean set of Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff} (T6, C6 and T8 are back in it; an earlier draft restricted this comparison to $17$ events because those three were still missing for Qwen2.5-14B). Per-unit Brier and directional accuracy are computed from the mean of the (up to four) Evaluator~A reads, not a single pass, following Section~\ref{sec:replication_noise}'s own diagnosis that a single read is not a reference measurement.

\centering
\caption{Four-model comparison on the $20$ events every campaign shares that resolve after every model's release date (no model could have memorised these outcomes). Lift and susceptibility use the per-unit mean Brier across all available Evaluator~A reads; accuracy uses the per-unit averaged probability vector. Flat rate is the fraction of individual Evaluator~A reads in Condition C returning an exactly uniform vector. Recomputed 2026-08-21 on the full $20$-event common set: Qwen2.5-14B's six wrong-topic exclusions (T3, T4, T5, T6, C6, T8) were re-simulated against the correct catalogue and rescored (\S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status}), restoring T6, C6 and T8 to the events common to all four campaigns; the set grows from $17$ to the full $20$.}
\label{tab:four_model_clean}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Lift $A\!\to\!B$} & \textbf{Dir.\ acc., B} & \textbf{Dir.\ acc., C} & \textbf{Susceptibility} & \textbf{Flat rate, Cond.\ C} \\
\midrule
Llama-3.1-8B    & $-0.114$          & $0.684$          & $0.500$          & $+0.059$          & $3.8\%$ \\
Mistral-7B      & $-0.044$          & $0.850$          & $0.650$          & $+0.070$          & $\mathbf{2.5\%}$ \\
Qwen2.5-7B      & $+0.004$          & $0.850$          & $0.550$          & $\mathbf{+0.173}$ & $2.5\%$ \\
Qwen2.5-14B     & $\mathbf{+0.069}$ & $\mathbf{0.900}$ & $\mathbf{0.800}$ & $+0.153$          & $5.0\%$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{6pt}
\end{table*}

\textbf{Contamination control changes which model looks worst, not just the aggregate sign.} Mistral-7B's lift on the full, uncontrolled $30$-event set, using the per-unit mean across all available Evaluator~A reads rather than the single pass of Section~\ref{sec:cross_model_stats}, is $-0.224$---the paper's clearest case of simulation harming a model. On the $17$ clean events it is $-0.020$, indistinguishable from the noise floor established in Section~\ref{sec:replication_noise}. Most of what looked like Mistral-specific harm from deliberation was memorisation in the shared Condition~A reference, consistent with Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff}'s finding for the aggregate, now shown to hold at the individual-model level as well. Llama-3.1-8B's numbers in this section were recomputed 2026-08-16 after the outage repair of \S\ref{sec:outage}; the reading that motivated this paragraph in earlier drafts does not survive it. Pre-repair, the 17 clean events showed Llama's lift as positive and larger than on the full set ($+0.092$), with $25.0\%$ of its Condition~C reads returning an exactly uniform vector---roughly an order of magnitude above every other model---read as a model-specific sharpness-loss pathology once contamination stopped masking it. Post-repair, both figures collapse: lift on the clean 17 is now $-0.016$ (indistinguishable from Mistral's $-0.020$, and like it spanning zero), and the Condition~C flat rate is $7.4\%$, in the same range as Qwen2.5-7B and no longer an outlier. The $25.0\%$ figure was concentrated in exactly the events later found to carry the unreachable-endpoint corruption (\S\ref{sec:outage}); once those events carry real evaluator input, the elevated flat rate disappears along with the lift advantage. We retract the sharpness-loss-pathology reading: it was evidence about the outage's evaluator input, not about Llama-3.1-8B as an architecture.

\textbf{Qwen2.5-14B leads on lift and both directional-accuracy columns, but is no longer the sharpest on flat rate once the comparison covers its previously-missing events.} It has the largest lift ($+0.069$) and the highest directional accuracy in both With-Sim ($0.900$) and Null ($0.800$) conditions---$5$ and $15$ points, respectively, above the next-best model on each. Its Condition~C flat rate, however, is $5.0\%$---the \emph{highest} of the four models, not the lowest as it appeared under the smaller $17$-event set (where it happened to have $0.0\%$ on the events it had). T6, C6 and T8, the three events that were missing before and are now included, contribute flat reads that were simply invisible to the earlier, narrower comparison; nothing about the recovered events' evidence or scoring is unusual (\S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status}), and there is nothing to indicate they are treated differently by the evaluator. Susceptibility keeps its ordering from the $17$-event set: Qwen2.5-7B still has the largest margin ($+0.173$, was $+0.194$), ahead of Qwen2.5-14B ($+0.153$, was $+0.157$). Qwen2.5-7B remains the most consistent low-risk choice on flat rate specifically: at most $2.5\%$ in every condition across both evaluators, at the cost of the lowest directional accuracy of the four models in Condition~C.

\textbf{Cross-evaluator agreement remains moderate at this scale.} Pooled over all conditions and all available units, Evaluator~A's per-unit mean Brier correlates with Evaluator~C's single read at $r = 0.696$ (Llama, resynced 2026-08-21 alongside the Qwen2.5-14B recovery below), $0.765$ (Mistral), $0.701$ (Qwen2.5-7B) and $0.706$ (Qwen2.5-14B, now over its full $90$ units rather than $72$)---all four in the ``moderate'' band, none near the agreement a reliable instrument should show. Evaluator~C returns a flat vector on \emph{zero} of the $120$ Condition-C reads pooled across all four models ($30$ each, Qwen2.5-14B now included at its full count rather than $24$), the strongest single piece of evidence in this paper that the flat-forecast phenomenon is an artifact of the specific evaluator rather than of deliberation: it is not merely reduced by a second read, as Section~\ref{sec:replication_central} showed, but absent under a differently-implemented one. This comes with a caveat symmetric to Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}'s finding for Evaluator~J: \texttt{openai/gpt-5.6-luna-pro} rejects this pipeline's strict JSON-schema request outright (\texttt{invalid\_json\_schema}, a malformed \texttt{required} array on \texttt{validated\_scales}) and falls back to unconstrained \texttt{json\_object} mode for every call, so Evaluator~C's zero flat-rate is achieved under the same weaker enforcement level as Evaluator~A, not a stronger one---an instrument-configuration bug independent of the one in Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}, caught by the same discipline of checking rather than assuming enforcement.

The underlying instrument-noise finding of Section~\ref{sec:replication_noise} also generalises beyond Condition~A: pooling all three conditions, the mean spread across the available Evaluator~A reads per unit---four for Llama, Mistral and Qwen2.5-7B, three for Qwen2.5-14B, which has no original single-pass Evaluator~A record to add as a fourth---is $0.330$ (Llama, resynced 2026-08-21), $0.375$ (Mistral), $0.280$ (Qwen2.5-7B) and $0.208$ (Qwen2.5-14B, now over $90$ units) Brier points, with $7.8$--$13.3\%$ of units reproducing to within $0.01$ across reads. This is larger than the $0.128$-mean/$0.715$-max figure reported there for Condition~A alone, because that figure benefited from Condition~A's dossier being short and largely unambiguous relative to full deliberation transcripts; the instrument is, if anything, less reliable on the transcripts that matter most for the paper's central claim.

\section{The Evaluator Decides; Evidence Volume and Schema Enforcement Do Not Survive a Controlled Test}
\label{sec:volume_mechanism}

Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat} raised evidence volume as the one swarm-model-specific variable that could account for the cross-model flat-forecast pattern, and left it unestablished. The per-unit \texttt{evidence\_text} lengths measured above, joined to the flat-unit flags of each scored pass, settle it. Two questions are separable and we take them in turn: whether volume predicts degeneracy \emph{within} a campaign, and whether volume is sufficient to produce it.

\textbf{Within a campaign, longer evidence predicts a flat forecast.} Pooling the $60$ simulated units of each affected campaign, flat units carry markedly more evidence than non-flat ones: median $19{,}016$ against $3{,}009$ characters for Llama-3.1-8B (Mann--Whitney $U=626$, $p = 6.5\times10^{-5}$) and $13{,}906$ against $12{,}074$ for Mistral-7B ($U=683$, $p = 3.1\times10^{-6}$). Qwen2.5-7B, whose units are the shortest of the three campaigns, has no flat units at all and so contributes no within-model contrast.

\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B cannot carry this argument, and we do not rest it on that campaign.} Its high-volume units are exactly its six pre-outage events: all $12$ of its healthy simulated units are flat, against $6$ of the $48$ units affected by the serving failure of Appendix~\ref{sec:outage}. Evidence volume is therefore perfectly confounded there with whether any deliberation occurred at all, and the $19{,}016$-versus-$3{,}009$ contrast is equally consistent with a deliberation account and with an extraction account.

\textbf{Mistral-7B is the clean test, and it shows a monotone dose--response.} That campaign suffered no outage: all $60$ of its simulated units carry a genuine multi-speaker transcript (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_depth}, $0/60$ units with $\leq 1$ speaker), so evidence length varies across units without varying whether deliberation happened. Splitting those units into terciles by \texttt{evidence\_text} length gives flat rates of $1/20$ ($5\%$), $5/20$ ($25\%$) and $13/20$ ($65\%$) from shortest to longest, over a range of only $10{,}192$ to $15{,}557$ characters (Figure~\ref{fig:volume_mechanism}A). A $50\%$ increase in input length moves the degeneracy rate by a factor of thirteen.

\textbf{Volume is nevertheless not sufficient: the same transcripts are not flat under a different instrument.} The decisive comparison holds the input fixed and varies only the evaluator. Llama-3.1-8B's $12$ high-volume units are $12/12$ flat under Evaluator J; under the four Evaluator~A reads of Section~\ref{sec:four_model_extension} they are $0/12$, $1/12$, $2/12$ and $0/12$, and under Evaluator~C, $0/12$. Mistral-7B's $20$ longest units are $13/20$ flat under Evaluator J, then $1/20$, $1/20$, $0/20$, $0/20$ and $0/20$. Figure~\ref{fig:volume_mechanism}B shows both. Nothing about the transcripts changes across those bars; only the model asked to compress them does.

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figI_volume_mechanism.png}
\caption{Evidence volume as the trigger for degenerate forecasts, and the evaluator as their locus. \textbf{Panel A:} flat-forecast rate by \texttt{evidence\_text} length tercile for Mistral-7B under Evaluator J. Mistral is the clean test because its campaign suffered no serving outage, so all $60$ simulated units carry a real multi-speaker transcript and length varies without a confound. \textbf{Panel B:} the units that drive Panel A---Llama-3.1-8B's $12$ high-volume units and Mistral-7B's $20$ longest---re-scored by every available pass. The transcripts are byte-identical across all six bars in each series; only the instrument differs.}
\label{fig:volume_mechanism}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{Does the failure transfer to other unconstrained evaluators?}
\label{sec:transfer_expt}

\textbf{And enforcement is not the whole of the instrument: the failure is specific to small evaluators, and reproduces on one of them.} The comparison above is suggestive but incomplete, because every pass it compares against---the four Evaluator~A reads and Evaluator~C---has structured-output enforcement \emph{on}. It therefore cannot separate two explanations: that enforcement is what prevents the collapse, or that this particular model is what causes it. We ran the missing arm. All $60$ of Mistral-7B's simulated units were re-scored by five further evaluators, each called with no \texttt{response\_format} field at all---the same unconstrained condition the July instrument ran under---on byte-identical transcripts. If unconstrained decoding over a long transcript were sufficient, all five should degenerate; if the July instrument were unique, none should.

Neither is what happens. Four of the five return \emph{no} flat units: \texttt{deepseek/deepseek-r1} $0/56$ (mean modal $0.667$), \texttt{deepseek/deepseek-r1-distill-llama-70b} $0/54$ ($0.675$), \texttt{qwen/qwen3-32b} $0/57$ ($0.577$) and \texttt{mistralai/ministral-14b-2512} $0/60$ ($0.557$). The fifth does: \texttt{qwen/qwen3-14b} returns an exactly uniform vector for $8/58$ units ($13.8\%$), against Evaluator~J's $19/60$ ($31.7\%$) on the same material. Pooled, the comparison evaluators give $8$ flat units in $285$ scored observations. Two controls matter here. First, prompt simplicity is not the explanation: running \texttt{deepseek/deepseek-r1} through the production evaluator class with the full rubric prompt verbatim and enforcement still disabled gives $0/60$, including $0/19$ on the units Evaluator~J flattened. Second, parameter count alone is not the explanation either, since \texttt{ministral-14b-2512} is the same size as the model that fails and does not fail. The two instruments that degenerate---DeepSeek-R1-14B and Qwen3-14B---are both \emph{small reasoning} models, and the within-family contrast is clean: Qwen3-14B gives $8/58$ where Qwen3-32B, same family and same prompt, gives $0/57$. With one model per cell we offer that as the pattern the data shows, not as an established mechanism.

\textbf{The two failing instruments fail on the same transcripts.} This is the part that turns a curiosity into evidence about the input. Of Qwen3-14B's $8$ flat units, $6$ fall inside the $19$-unit set Evaluator~J flattened, where chance would put $2.6$ (one-sided Fisher exact $p = 0.012$). Its flat units also carry more evidence than its sharp ones---median $13{,}817$ against $12{,}760$ characters---the same direction and nearly the same magnitude as Evaluator~J's own split ($13{,}906$ against $12{,}074$). Two independently trained models, from different families, agree above chance about which transcripts cannot be reduced to an informative distribution, and agree that the harder ones are the longer ones. That is difficult to explain as an idiosyncrasy of one deployment and easy to explain as a property of the input interacting with limited extraction capacity.

\textbf{What this narrows.} An earlier reading of this paper held that ``evidence volume is the trigger and the schema-disabled evaluator is the locus,'' which reads as though any schema-disabled evaluator would do. It would not: three of the five comparison evaluators are at least as capable as Evaluator~J, run in the same condition, and return nothing uniform at all. What separates the instruments that fail from those that do not is the model, and \S\ref{sec:truncation_grid} then shows by direct assignment that neither of the two factors we had credited---input length and enforcement---accounts for the difference. We could not re-run Evaluator~J itself, because the local endpoint no longer exists and no provider we have access to serves DeepSeek-R1-14B; that limits every causal statement in this section to a proxy. Figure~\ref{fig:transfer} shows the full comparison.

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figK_degeneracy_transfer.png}
\caption{Degenerate consensus across unconstrained evaluators. Every row scores all $60$ of Mistral-7B's simulated units---byte-identical transcripts---with no \texttt{response\_format} field, the identical condition the July instrument ran under. \textbf{Panel A:} flat-forecast rate with $95\%$ Wilson intervals. Four of the five comparison evaluators return nothing uniform; \texttt{qwen/qwen3-14b}, the smallest reasoning model tested, returns $8/58$. The \emph{pipeline rubric prompt} row uses the production evaluator class and its full prompt verbatim, ruling out prompt simplicity as the explanation. \textbf{Panel B:} the same rates restricted to the $19$ units Evaluator~J itself flattened. Qwen3-14B's failures concentrate there---$6$ of its $8$, against $2.6$ expected by chance---so the two failing instruments are failing on the same inputs rather than independently.}
\label{fig:transfer}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{The truncation grid, and what it does to this section's argument}
\label{sec:truncation_grid}

Everything above is observational in the variable that matters. The tercile result compares units that \emph{happen} to carry more evidence with units that happen to carry less, and the instrument contrast compares evaluators that differ in more than enforcement. We therefore ran the manipulation directly: a $2 \times 4$ grid crossing structured-output enforcement against four assigned evidence lengths, over all $60$ of Mistral-7B's simulated units, with the evaluator model, the prompt, the temperature and the transcript identical within each arm. Truncation retains both sections of the record at every level, keeping the log's final lines and uniformly subsampling the action lines, so the levels differ in how much of each section is shown rather than in which sections exist.

The evaluator is \texttt{qwen/qwen3-14b}, chosen because it is the only accessible model that reproduces the degeneracy at all (\S\ref{sec:transfer_expt}); \texttt{deepseek/deepseek-r1} is included as a negative control at three of the four levels. Evaluator J itself cannot be used: its local endpoint no longer exists and no provider we can reach serves DeepSeek-R1-14B. That is the central limitation of what follows, and it means the grid tests this section's mechanism on a proxy rather than on the instrument that produced the finding.

\textbf{Assigned evidence length does not increase degeneracy, and the sign is reversed.} Under \texttt{qwen3-14b} with enforcement off, the flat rate across the four levels is $7/59$ at $\approx\!1.8$k characters, $4/60$ at $\approx\!4.8$k, $0/60$ at $\approx\!7.8$k and $1/60$ untruncated. The \emph{shortest} condition is the worst one, and the contrast between the extremes is significant in the direction opposite to the hypothesis (two-sided Fisher exact $p = 0.032$). Excess sharpness, measured as the modal probability minus $1/K$ so that it is comparable across the mixed-$K$ catalogue, is likewise flat in length: $+0.171$, $+0.190$, $+0.203$, $+0.185$, with overlapping intervals throughout. The negative control behaves the same way and at a different level entirely: \texttt{deepseek-r1} gives $1/60$, $1/49$ and $0/60$ flat with excess sharpness near $+0.32$ at every length.

\textbf{Enforcement, isolated within one model, does nothing.} Pooled over all four lengths, \texttt{qwen3-14b} returns $12/239$ flat units with enforcement off and $11/239$ with it on (Fisher $p = 1.000$), at excess sharpness $+0.187$ and $+0.208$. This is the first test in this paper that varies enforcement \emph{alone}. Every earlier comparison---including the re-scoring of Section~\ref{sec:replication_central}---changed the evaluator model and the enforcement setting together, so none of them could attribute the effect to either.

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figL_truncation.png}
\caption{The $2 \times 4$ truncation grid over Mistral-7B's $60$ simulated units. Within an arm the evaluator, prompt, temperature and transcript are fixed; only how much of the transcript is shown varies. \textbf{Panel A:} flat-forecast rate with $95\%$ Wilson intervals. Assigning a shorter transcript does not reduce degeneracy---the shortest condition is the worst, and the $\approx\!2$k-versus-untruncated contrast is significant in the reverse direction ($p = 0.032$). \textbf{Panel B:} excess sharpness, the modal probability minus $1/K$, which is zero for a uniform vector at any $K$ and therefore comparable across this mixed-$K$ catalogue. It is flat in length for both evaluators, and the gap between them ($\approx\!+0.19$ for \texttt{qwen3-14b} against $\approx\!+0.32$ for \texttt{deepseek-r1}) is larger at every level than anything length or enforcement does.}
\label{fig:truncation}
\end{figure*}

\textbf{This is a negative result for two of this section's three claims, and we report it as one.} The dose--response of Figure~\ref{fig:volume_mechanism}A is real as an association within Evaluator J and does not reproduce as an effect when length is assigned. Two readings remain open and the data do not separate them: either the volume relationship is specific to Evaluator J, which we cannot re-run, or the tercile pattern was always driven by something that co-varies with length across units rather than by length itself---which is precisely the alternative this experiment was built to exclude, and it does not exclude it. The schema-enforcement claim fares no better under isolation. What survives, and is strengthened rather than weakened, is the claim this paper's title makes: \emph{which model reads the transcript} dominates. The gap between \texttt{deepseek-r1} and \texttt{qwen3-14b} in excess sharpness is roughly $0.13$ at every length, larger than anything either manipulation produces, and it is the same axis along which Evaluator~J differs from every evaluator that does not degenerate.

We leave the recommendation of Section~\ref{sec:replication_implications} in place but re-derive it. Verifying enforcement at runtime remains sound engineering---a pipeline should know what it sent---but on this evidence we cannot promise that enabling it will prevent degenerate extraction, and a benchmark that switches it on and declares the problem solved would be relying on something we tested and could not confirm. The check that does discriminate is the one we already recommend for a different reason: score with a second, stronger evaluator and compare.

\textbf{What this establishes.} Taking the observational and the experimental evidence together: degeneracy is a property of the extracting model, associated with input volume within the one instrument we could not re-run and not caused by it in the two we could. Long transcripts do not intrinsically elicit uniform forecasts---four independent reads and a second architecture recover sharp distributions from the very same text---and the instrument does not degenerate indiscriminately either, since its own flat rate on the same campaigns' short inputs is low. We had attributed the vulnerability to the schema-disabling defect of Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping} and its exercise to input length. Section~\ref{sec:truncation_grid} tests both in isolation and supports neither, so we withdraw the mechanism while keeping the localisation: the defect is real and worth fixing, but it is not what we can show makes the difference. This resolves the question Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_flat} left open in favour of the extraction account, and it does so without appeal to the cross-model comparison whose confounds Section~\ref{sec:serving_note} documents: the dose--response is measured within a single campaign, and the instrument contrast is measured on fixed inputs.

We previously noted an asymmetry here that the 2026-08-16 repair has since resolved rather than merely updated the numbers on. Before the repair, the residual flat units under Evaluator~A relocated to Llama's low-volume arm ($10$, $8$, $12$ and $12$ of $48$ across the four reads, against $0$--$2$ of $12$ high-volume): those $48$ were exactly the outage units, whose \texttt{evidence\_text} was a connection-error dump containing no information about the question (Appendix~\ref{sec:evidence_depth}), for which a uniform posterior is the defensible response. Post-repair, with real transcripts in place of the connection-error dump, the same $48$-unit arm's flat count falls to $3$, $2$, $2$ and $2$ of $48$---in the same low range as the high-volume arm, not elevated. This is consistent with, rather than contradicting, the extraction-channel account: the low-volume arm was never flat \emph{because} it was low-volume; it was flat because it was empty. Once it is no longer empty, it behaves like any other short-but-substantive input. The asymmetry was therefore itself a symptom of the outage, layered on top of the genuine volume/instrument interaction Mistral's clean test establishes; it is retracted as a separate finding.

\textbf{The remaining ambiguity is narrow, and we state it.} A dose--response within Mistral establishes that the amount of transcript shown drives Evaluator J's degeneracy; it does not establish \emph{why}, and it cannot separate raw character count from the properties that co-vary with it in this corpus---more speakers, more distinct claims, more conflicting positions to reconcile. The truncation grid we ran does not resolve this either, and we are explicit about why: showing less of a transcript necessarily shows fewer of its voices. Across our four levels the median number of distinct speakers visible in the representative-actions block falls from $44$ untruncated to $29$, $20$ and $9$, tracking the character count almost exactly. The manipulation therefore varies \emph{amount of deliberation shown} as a bundle, which is the same bundle the natural across-unit variation varies; what it buys is that the variation is now assigned rather than observed, so it cannot be explained by long transcripts differing in kind from short ones. Isolating character count alone would require padding with semantically null filler at a fixed speaker count---a different experiment, and one we have not run.

\parttitle{II}{What Survives the Audit}

\section{What the Benchmark Does Establish: Content-Sensitive Predictive Signal}
\label{sec:predictive_signal}

The preceding sections withdraw a great deal. It is worth stating precisely what survives, because one substantive claim does, and it is the claim the benchmark was built to test in its most defensible form. We evaluate it on the same footing as Table~\ref{tab:four_model_clean}: the $20$ contamination-clean events common to all four campaigns, scored from the per-unit mean of the available Evaluator~A reads rather than a single pass. Table~\ref{tab:predictive_signal} and Figure~\ref{fig:predictive_signal} report it.

\begin{table*}[!htbp]
\centering

Chance directional accuracy is $1/K$ per event, averaged; $K$ ranges from $2$ to $9$ across these events, so the null distribution is a Poisson binomial and the reported $p$ is exact and one-sided. $\Delta$ columns are Condition B minus Condition C, with $95\%$ percentile bootstrap intervals ($B=20{,}000$); positive means relevant evidence beats topically unrelated evidence. Recomputed 2026-08-21 on the full $20$-event common set (was $17$; \S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status}).}
\label{tab:predictive_signal}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%

Mistral-7B    & $0.850$ & $0.650$ & $0.326$ & $5.3\times10^{-7}$  & $+0.200$ $[-0.050, +0.450]$ & $+0.064$ $[-0.142, +0.261]$ \\
Qwen2.5-7B    & $0.850$ & $0.550$ & $0.326$ & $5.3\times10^{-7}$  & $+0.300$ $[+0.050, +0.550]$ & $+0.173$ $[+0.073, +0.271]$ \\
Qwen2.5-14B   & $\mathbf{0.900}$ & $0.800$ & $0.326$ & $3.1\times10^{-8}$ & $+0.100$ $[-0.100, +0.300]$ & $+0.143$ $[+0.060, +0.234]$ \\
\midrule
\textbf{Pooled} ($80$ units) & $\mathbf{0.819}$ & $0.625$ & $0.326$ & $\mathbf{3.7\times10^{-22}}$ & $\mathbf{+0.194}$ $[+0.075, +0.313]$ & $\mathbf{+0.112}$ $[+0.042, +0.182]$ \\

\smallskip\\
\footnotesize\textsuperscript{$\dagger$}Llama-3.1-8B's row is computed from the re-simulated transcripts of \S\ref{sec:outage}. Its per-model contribution to the surviving finding is the weakest of the four: both of its $\Delta$ intervals now span zero, and its Condition~B directional accuracy ($0.675$) is the lowest here, though still far above the $0.326$ chance level. The pooled result---the one this section's claim actually rests on---excludes zero on both $\Delta$ columns and is driven by all four campaigns rather than by this one. Directional accuracy credits a $k$-way tie at $1/k$; across the $80$ Condition~B units there are $65$ outright hits and a single two-way tie, giving $65.5/80 = 0.819$.
