\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figJ_predictive_signal.png}
\caption{The benchmark's one surviving positive result, on the $20$ contamination-clean events common to all four campaigns. \textbf{Panel A:} Condition B directional accuracy against the per-event chance level $1/K$ (mean $32.6\%$; $K$ ranges $2$--$9$), with Condition C shown for contrast. \textbf{Panel B:} the Brier gained from relevant rather than topically unrelated evidence, $BS_C - BS_B$, with $95\%$ percentile bootstrap intervals. The pooled interval and two of four per-model intervals exclude zero.}
\label{fig:predictive_signal}
\end{figure*}

\textbf{The simulated forecasts identify realised outcomes far above chance.} Pooling the four campaigns, the swarm's modal option is the outcome that actually occurred in $65$ of $80$ (model, event) pairs outright, with one further unit a two-way tie that includes the outcome; credited at $1/k$ that is $65.5/80 = 81.9\%$ (recomputed 2026-08-21 after the Qwen2.5-14B recovery of \S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status} grew the common clean-event set from $17$ to $20$; was $56.5/68$, $83.1\%$), against a chance level of $32.6\%$. Because the option count varies across these events, the null is a Poisson binomial rather than a coin flip; evaluated exactly, $p = 3.7\times10^{-22}$ pooled and below $4\times10^{-4}$ for every individual model (Llama-3.1-8B is still the weakest individual contributor at $p = 3.5\times10^{-4}$, still far past any conventional threshold). Ten of the twenty events are called correctly by all four models and \emph{no} event is called wrongly by all four. These are events that resolved after every model's public release date, so no campaign could have memorised the outcome, and they are scored from four independent evaluator reads rather than the single pass whose instability Section~\ref{sec:replication_noise} documents.

\textbf{And the swarm is responding to the content of its evidence, not merely to its presence.} Directional accuracy alone cannot distinguish a swarm that reasons from one that inherits a good prior from its base model. Condition~C is the control that can: it runs the identical $300$-agent, $60$-round machinery, injects a structurally matched payload at the same checkpoint, and differs only in that the payload is topically unrelated to the question. Replacing relevant evidence with irrelevant evidence costs $0.112$ Brier $[+0.042, +0.182]$ and $19.4$ points of directional accuracy $[+7.5, +31.3]$ pooled (recomputed 2026-08-21 once the Qwen2.5-14B recovery of \S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status} grew the common clean-event set from $17$ to $20$; was $0.125$ $[+0.053,+0.195]$ Brier and $18.4$ $[+6.6,+30.9]$ points on the 17-event set)---both intervals excluding zero, though only two of the four per-model Brier intervals exclude zero individually, Llama's being $[-0.058,+0.208]$ and Mistral's $[-0.142,+0.261]$. This contrast is a within-simulation comparison: it never touches the shared Condition~A record of Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}, so it is immune to the confound that disqualifies the aggregate lift, and Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff} shows it survives knowledge-cutoff stratification unchanged.

\textbf{What this does not establish, and we are explicit about it.} Three limits bound the claim.

First, \emph{the simulation is not shown to improve on direct elicitation, and on the recovered 20-event set it now looks worse for one model}. Condition~B's directional accuracy (B vs.\ A) is $0.675$ vs.\ $0.650$ for Llama-3.1-8B (B ahead), $0.850$ vs.\ $0.900$ for Mistral-7B (B \emph{behind}, the first case in this comparison where simulation trails direct elicitation), $0.850$ vs.\ $0.850$ for Qwen2.5-7B (tied), and $0.900$ vs.\ $0.850$ for Qwen2.5-14B (B ahead); recomputed 2026-08-21 on the 20-event set (was three ties and one B-ahead case on the 17-event set). No per-model difference approaches significance on $20$ events either way. Much of the signal in Panel A is therefore attributable to what the base model already knows, not to deliberation. The B-versus-C contrast isolates what deliberation demonstrably contributes---sensitivity to which evidence it is given---and that is a weaker claim than prediction lift. The original Prediction Lift hypothesis (Claim~1) remains unanswerable through Condition~A for the reason given in Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}: it was run once and shared, so no within-model contrast exists there. Section~\ref{sec:cot_baseline} supplies the missing per-model comparison from the other direction, by running a single-agent baseline against the same evidence; it does not overturn this limit so much as sharpen it, since a single call matches the swarm's directional accuracy outright.

Second, \emph{directional accuracy is a coarse criterion}. It scores only which outcome carries the most mass. Condition~B's mean Brier on these events is $0.22$--$0.34$ depending on model---informative, but far from the sharp, well-calibrated distribution a forecasting system would need to be useful operationally. A system can be $88\%$ directionally right and still poorly calibrated, and Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_sharpness} shows this one hedges systematically.

Third, \emph{release-date stratification is a necessary control, not a sufficient one}. It rules out memorisation of the outcome itself. It does not rule out the seed dossier containing strong contemporaneous signal about the likely outcome, which the swarm would then be relaying rather than inferring. Distinguishing those requires holding dossier content fixed while varying deliberation, which this design does not do.

\subsection{A Single-Agent Baseline: One Call Is Not Beaten by Three Hundred Agents}
\label{sec:cot_baseline}

The first of those three limits is the one a reader will press hardest on, and until now we could only concede it, because Condition~A was shared rather than run per model (Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}). This subsection removes that excuse from the other side: instead of re-running the No-Sim condition, we run a \emph{single-agent} control that receives exactly the evidence the swarm's agents received.

\textbf{Design.} For each of the $20$ contamination-clean events and each of four models we issue one retrieval-augmented chain-of-thought call: system prompt casting the model as a calibrated forecaster, the event question and its $K$ options, the same seed dossier the swarm's agents were initialised from, and the same round-$30$ relevant-update payload Condition~B injected---then a demand for step-by-step reasoning and a JSON probability vector over the options, with structured-output enforcement on. Three replicates per cell (replicate~1 at temperature $0$, seed $42$; replicates~2 and~3 at temperature $0.7$, seeds $100$ and $2026$) give $20 \times 4 \times 3 = 240$ calls (recomputed 2026-08-21 on $3$ additional events once Qwen2.5-14B's recovery grew the common clean-event set from $17$ to $20$; \S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status}), all $240$ of which returned parseable output. The comparison is to Condition~B scored exactly as everywhere else in this section, from the per-unit mean of the available Evaluator~A reads.

\textbf{Three ways this comparison can be got wrong, and how we avoid each.} We state them because our own first pass at this analysis made all three mistakes simultaneously and reported a spurious $p = 0.045$ against the swarm.

\emph{(i) Arm matching.} Only two of the four single-agent runs use the same checkpoint as the campaign they are set against. \texttt{mistralai/ministral-8b-2512} is neither the model nor the generation of Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1, and \texttt{qwen/qwen3-14b} is a generation ahead of Qwen2.5-14B; those two arms compare different models and cannot be read as a within-model contrast. The primary estimate below is therefore the two identity-matched arms only, and the four-arm pool is reported separately and marked. Even the matched arms differ in precision and serving stack---FP16 through a hosted API against local AWQ-INT4 under vLLM and GGUF under Ollama respectively---which is the same confound Section~\ref{sec:serving_note} documents, here working in the single agent's favour.

\emph{(ii) Aggregation symmetry.} Collapsing the three replicates into one averaged probability vector gives the single-agent arm an ensembling advantage that the swarm arm does not receive: the swarm's four reads re-read \emph{one} simulation, they do not re-run it. Because the headline runs against the swarm, that asymmetry pushes in the same direction as the conclusion, so we report the mean of the per-replicate scores---the expected cost of one call---as primary, and the three-call ensemble only as an upper bound on what the single-agent arm can do at triple cost.

\emph{(iii) Clustering.} The same $20$ events recur across models, so pooled unit-pairs are not independent and a Diebold--Mariano test at $\mathrm{df}=n-1$ over them is anticonservative. Pooled intervals here come from a cluster bootstrap that resamples \emph{events}, and pooled $p$-values from a cluster-robust $t$ on the per-event means ($\mathrm{df}=19$). The difference is not cosmetic: on the four-arm pool the naive test returns $p = 0.016$ where the clustered one returns $p = 0.083$.

Per-arm $p$ is a Diebold--Mariano test with the Harvey--Leybourne--Newbold small-sample correction ($\mathrm{df}=19$); pooled $p$ is the cluster-robust equivalent. \begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figM_cot_vs_swarm.png}
\caption{The single-agent baseline. \textbf{Panel A:} Brier score for each arm, single call against swarm, on a common axis; the length of the connector is the quantity of interest. The two identity-matched arms are separated from the two cross-model ones. \textbf{Panel B:} the paired difference $BS_{\mathrm{CoT}} - BS_{\mathrm{swarm}}$ with $95\%$ intervals (per-arm bootstrap over events; pooled cluster bootstrap over event clusters). Positive favours the swarm. Open diamonds mark where the point estimate moves if the three CoT replicates are ensembled into one vector instead of scored separately---a $3\times$-cost variant shown to make the aggregation choice visible, not as the primary estimate.}
\label{fig:cot_baseline}
\end{figure*}

\textbf{The result is a non-superiority finding pointing more clearly toward the single call, and we are careful not to overstate it in either direction.} On both identity-matched arms the single call has the lower mean Brier---$0.364$ against $0.545$ for Llama-3.1-8B and $0.318$ against $0.348$ for Qwen2.5-7B---and the higher directional accuracy. Pooled over the matched arms the gap is $0.106$ Brier in the single agent's favour, but the interval is $[-0.226, +0.009]$ and the clustered $p$ is $0.109$: at $20$ events this design still cannot separate that gap from zero, exactly as \S\ref{sec:power_limitation} would predict, though it comes closer than the $17$-event reading did ($p=0.156$). The per-event differences give $d_z = 0.39$, so the achieved power against an effect of the size observed is $37\%$, and $55$ events would be needed for $80\%$ (was $30\%$ and $59$ events on the smaller set). A null here is what an underpowered design returns when the true effect is anywhere from moderately negative to slightly positive; it is not a demonstration of equivalence. What the data do support is the negative: \emph{there is no arm, matched or not, on which the swarm's advantage over a single call is distinguishable from zero}, and on the recovered 20-event set the point estimate now favours the single call on \emph{all four} arms, not three of four as on the 17-event set---Qwen3-14B, previously the one arm where the swarm led on points, now shows a near-zero gap in the single call's favour ($-0.008$ $[-0.116,+0.093]$) rather than a swarm advantage, though it remains a cross-model arm in which neither direction is evidence about Qwen2.5-14B specifically. One further nuance the 20-event set surfaces: two of the six intervals plotted in Figure~\ref{fig:cot_baseline}B --- the Llama-3.1-8B matched arm alone ($[-0.365, -0.020]$) and the all-four pool ($[-0.199, -0.000]$) --- now exclude zero in the single call's favour under their own (non-clustered, per-arm) bootstrap, even though neither is the paper's primary inferential test. The primary test is the cluster-robust one reported above, which does not reach significance on either scope; we report the two individually-significant intervals for transparency, not as an additional finding, since a reader comparing this figure against Table~\ref{tab:cot_baseline} would otherwise wonder why two segments do not touch the dashed zero line.

\textbf{The estimate is stable across the analysis choices, and two of six configurations now exclude zero.} Substituting the three-call ensemble for the single call moves the matched pool from $-0.106$ $[-0.226, +0.009]$ to $-0.135$ $[-0.247, -0.028]$, whose interval excludes zero with clustered $p = 0.034$; taking replicate~1 alone gives $-0.114$ $[-0.244, +0.007]$. The all-four-arm pool shows the same pattern: single $-0.097$ $[-0.199, -0.000]$, ensemble $-0.118$ $[-0.215, -0.025]$ (excludes zero, clustered $p = 0.032$), replicate-1-only $-0.095$ $[-0.202, +0.013]$. Across scopes and variants the point estimate stays between $-0.135$ and $-0.095$, and the lowest clustered $p$ is $0.032$ (recomputed 2026-08-21 on the 20-event set; was $-0.150$ to $-0.052$ and $0.069$ on the 17-event set, where no configuration excluded zero). Only the two three-call-ensemble configurations---a $3\times$-cost variant we report as an upper bound, not the primary estimate---exclude zero; the primary single-call estimate still does not, on either scope. Our discarded first pass reached $p = 0.045$ by combining the four-arm pool, the ensembled CoT vector, and the unclustered $\mathrm{df}=67$ test on the original 17-event data---each of which moves the number the same way. We record this because it is the same class of error the rest of this paper documents in the benchmark itself: a defensible-looking pipeline whose defaults all lean one direction.

\textbf{What this does and does not settle.} It does not replace the per-model Condition~A re-run, which earlier drafts called the single most valuable next experiment and which we now describe as one of two. The two ask different questions: Condition~A as specified uses a different elicitation prompt and a different serving stack from the campaign, whereas this baseline holds the evidence fixed and varies only whether $300$ agents deliberate over it for $60$ rounds. What it does settle is the cost question that motivated the benchmark. A $300$-agent, $60$-round simulation, its transcript, and an LLM evaluator to compress that transcript into a distribution buy no measurable accuracy over one call to the same model with the same evidence---and there is a real possibility, not established at this sample size, that they cost accuracy. It also qualifies the finding this section is named for: the above-chance directional accuracy of Table~\ref{tab:predictive_signal} is \emph{reproduced, and on this reading slightly exceeded, by a single call} ($0.834$ pooled over all four arms against the swarm's $0.819$), so that half of the surviving result is evidence about what these base models know, not about what deliberation adds. The B-versus-C contrast is untouched by this, being a comparison between two simulated conditions with no single-agent analogue in this run, and it remains the part of the finding that is specifically about the simulation.

\textbf{The defensible summary.} On events no model could have memorised, this multi-agent simulation produces forecasts that are strongly informative about how real social events resolved, and that measurably track the content of the evidence deliberated over rather than merely its presence---but the informativeness is matched by a single chain-of-thought call to the same model on the same evidence (\S\ref{sec:cot_baseline}), so only the content-sensitivity contrast is evidence about deliberation as such. Whether deliberation improves on simply asking the model is not established by this campaign; the single-agent baseline says the improvement, if any, is smaller than this design can resolve. We note the ordering this analysis reverses: under the single-pass Evaluator~J reading of Section~\ref{sec:cross_model_stats}, Mistral-7B was the only model whose susceptibility interval excluded zero; under four-read means on contamination-clean events it is the only model whose interval does not. That reversal is a further illustration of Section~\ref{sec:replication_noise}'s point, and a reason to trust the pooled figure over any single-model row.


\section{Discussion and Limitations}
\label{sec:discussion}

\subsection{On the Aggregate Simulation Lift Reversal, and Why We Read It as a Sharpness Story}
\label{sec:lift_reversal}

The result that launched this investigation is that the mean aggregate Brier score for With-Sim (Condition B) \emph{exceeds} the No-Sim baseline (Condition A) for all three models ($BS_B > BS_A$: $0.408 > 0.341$ for Llama-8B, recomputed 2026-08-16 post-repair, was $0.484 > 0.385$; $0.531 > 0.385$ for Mistral-7B; $0.481 > 0.385$ for Qwen-7B). Read as a benchmark result, this looks like a reversal of the ``Wisdom of Crowds'' hypothesis. We now believe that reading is largely wrong, and that the honest ordering of candidate causes is as follows.

\textbf{First, and primarily, measurement of forecast sharpness.} Section~\ref{sec:degenerate}---the phenomenon Part~I opens with, and which Sections~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}--\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} subsequently relocate to the extraction instrument---shows that $20.6\%$ of simulated units return an exactly uniform distribution---an extraction non-answer scored as a confident error---against $3.3\%$ under Condition A, and that simulation reduces mean modal probability from $0.658$ to roughly $0.51$ even among non-degenerate units. A strictly proper scoring rule penalises both~\cite{gneiting2007}. Neither requires the simulation to reason worse; both are sufficient by themselves to produce the observed sign, and the fact that directional accuracy is preserved for two of the three models (Section~\ref{sec:degenerate_sharpness}) is evidence that they did.

\textbf{Second, the baseline is not the model's own.} Because Condition A is a shared record (Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}), $BS_A - BS_B$ compares each swarm against a fixed external forecast rather than against its own direct elicitation. The quantity the reversal describes is therefore not the quantity the original Prediction Lift hypothesis asks about.

\textbf{Third, and only then, epistemic explanations.} The bimodality in per-event lift is real: a subset of events (T2, C11, S9, S3, C3) produces positive lift across all three models, while another (T1, C10, C15, S6) is uniformly negative. But we can no longer claim to know why, because the correlation previously used to characterise the split is an artifact (Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology}) and the split itself is defined by the variable it is meant to explain. We retain the SBE/SDE distinction as a description of the data and withdraw the causal reading.

Read together, these three points reframe what this campaign is evidence \emph{for}. It is weak evidence about whether social simulation improves forecasting: the measurement is too confounded and too underpowered to say. It is comparatively strong evidence about the \emph{measurement channel} itself---that asking an unconstrained LLM to compress a long deliberation transcript into a categorical distribution erases forecast sharpness before it erases forecast direction, and that this, not a deficit in collective reasoning, is the defensible explanation for the negative headline number.

Part~I settles which of the two candidate mechanisms is responsible, and the answer is not the one this subsection's earlier drafts proposed. The question posed here---whether the sharpness loss tracks deliberation or the swarm model's own vLLM serving path---turned out to be malformed: the \texttt{probabilities} field is produced by a single fixed evaluator irrespective of which swarm model is under test (Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}), so the serving stack of the model being benchmarked cannot be the operative variable. Holding the transcripts fixed and varying only the evaluator recovers sharp forecasts (Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication}), and a within-campaign dose--response identifies input volume as what exercises the defect (Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism}). The remaining agenda is correspondingly narrower: restore enforcement in place and re-measure with the \emph{same} evaluator, re-run Condition~A per model, and characterise event structure using covariates measured independently of either condition---resolution date relative to training cutoff, outcome entropy, option count $K$---before making any further claim about when social simulation helps.

\subsection{Statistical Power Limitation}
\label{sec:power_limitation}

All three campaigns are critically underpowered for detecting the simulation lift effect at conventional significance thresholds. From the empirical power analysis (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_stats}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Achieved power:} critically low for Mistral ($8.8\%$) and Qwen ($8.5\%$). Llama's observed-power figure is $91.9\%$ on the corrected repair, computed by the same normal approximation ($\text{power} \approx \Phi(|\text{DM}| - z_{0.975})$) that gave $8.5\%$ before the outage events were re-simulated and $13.8\%$ on the defective intermediate repair. That $8.5 \to 13.8 \to 91.9$ trajectory is a warning about the statistic, not a finding about the design: observed power is a monotone restatement of the observed $p$-value and rises automatically as an effect estimate grows. The design-power statement is unchanged---at $E = 30$, detecting $d = 0.5$ at $80\%$ still requires $E \geq 92$
    \item \textbf{Target:} $80\%$ power (conventional threshold)
    \item \textbf{Required sample:} $E \geq 92$ events to detect a medium effect at $\alpha=0.05$, $1-\beta=0.80$
    \item \textbf{Effect size:} all three models show a negative paired effect ($d_z = -0.613$ for Llama on the corrected repair, against $-0.216$ before it; $-0.301$ for Mistral, $-0.215$ for Qwen), with 95\% CI spanning zero for Mistral and Qwen
\end{itemize}

A note on the effect-size statistic. The pipeline's \texttt{summary.json} computes Cohen's $d$ with the pooled two-independent-samples formula ($-0.286$, $-0.440$, $-0.278$) and records $n_a = n_b = 30$. The design is within-subjects---Conditions A and B are evaluated on the same 30 events---so the paired standardised difference $d_z = \bar{d}/s_d$ is the appropriate statistic, and it is smaller in magnitude. We report $d_z$ in Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_stats} and note that the corresponding power figures, which were computed from the two-sample effect size, are if anything optimistic. The significance conclusion is unaffected: it is non-significant either way.

Consequently the Diebold-Mariano test returns non-significant $p$-values for Mistral and Qwen ($p = 0.88$--$0.95$ against the one-sided alternative that simulation helps). Llama is the exception on the corrected repair: $\text{DM} = -3.36$, two-sided $p = 0.0008$---significant, but in the direction of simulation \emph{harming} calibration, and carrying the serving-stack and evaluator substitutions of \S\ref{sec:outage}, so we do not read it as a clean estimate of a simulation effect. We stress what this does and does not license. It does not license the conclusion that the simulation effect is zero. Neither does it license the converse: earlier drafts argued that the cross-model consistency of the contamination-corrected lift ($\approx +0.20$ on clean events) is ``directionally meaningful,'' but Appendix~\ref{sec:contamination} shows those intervals also span zero and that the subset was selected on the outcome. At $E=30$ events with the observed dispersion, this campaign cannot distinguish a real effect of the magnitude we care about from noise, in either direction. That is the honest summary.

This is an inherent limitation of the benchmark's compute economics: a single three-condition event requires approximately 75--115 minutes of A100 GPU time. Expanding to $N=92$ events per model would require $\sim$115--175 GPU-hours per model family, or approximately $350$--$525$ total GPU-hours for three models---feasible for future campaigns but beyond the scope of the current exploratory study. Three remediation strategies are pursued in future work: (i) expanding the event catalog to $N \geq 92$ resolved post-training-cutoff events; (ii) applying one-sided DM tests with a pre-registered directional hypothesis; and (iii) pooling results across model families in a meta-analytic random-effects framework to increase effective sample size without expanding per-model compute.

\subsection{Evaluator Reliability and the 7-Dimension Rubric}
\label{sec:evaluator_reliability}

All three campaigns report \texttt{evaluator\_unstable: true}, with inter-rater Cohen's $\kappa$ values uniformly below the $\kappa \geq 0.8$ reliability threshold across all seven rubric dimensions. Table~\ref{tab:kappa_summary} reports the per-model kappa landscape.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Evaluator Inter-Rater Reliability: Peak and Representative Cohen's $\kappa$ Values per Model ($\kappa < 0.8$ = unreliable for composite scoring).}
\label{tab:kappa_summary}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Dimension} & \textbf{Llama-3.1-8B} & \textbf{Mistral-7B} & \textbf{Qwen2.5-7B} \\
\midrule
prediction\_accuracy    & 0.321 & 0.182 & 0.088 \\
polarization           & 0.241 & 0.167 & 0.122 \\
deliberation\_quality  & 0.209 & 0.228 & 0.062 \\
herd\_effect           & 0.206 & 0.113 & 0.053 \\
convergence            & 0.245 & 0.139 & 0.212 \\
susceptibility         & \textbf{0.322} & \textbf{0.245} & 0.033 \\
information\_diversity & 0.228 & 0.130 & 0.103 \\
\midrule
\textbf{Max $\kappa$}  & \textbf{0.322} & \textbf{0.245} & \textbf{0.212} \\
\textbf{Dropped dims}  & 7 & 7 & 7 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{6pt}
\end{table}

Several observations are notable. First, \textbf{evaluator reliability degrades systematically with decreased model capacity}: Llama-8B achieves the highest kappa values across all dimensions ($\max \kappa = 0.322$, on \texttt{susceptibility}), while Qwen-7B produces the lowest ($\max \kappa = 0.212$). Llama's peak dimension changed with the 2026-08-16 repair (previously \texttt{prediction\_accuracy} at $0.412$, computed on a mix of real and outage-corrupted units); the ordering across models is unchanged, but the specific value should no longer be read as an especially strong per-dimension result---it dropped along with the rest once computed from genuine data throughout. This degradation may reflect that smaller or less socially active models produce more homogeneous, low-information simulation outputs that are harder for the evaluator to discriminate. Second, \textbf{all 7 dimensions are dropped for all 3 models} under the $\kappa \geq 0.8$ reliability cutoff; consequently, the 7-dimension EPI composite score is not computable for any campaign reported here. Third, \textbf{no dimension approaches acceptable reliability}: even the best-performing dimension (\texttt{susceptibility} for Llama-8B at $\kappa = 0.322$) falls into the ``fair to moderate agreement'' category at best, far below the threshold required for scientific validity.

This instability likely reflects two compounding factors: (1) the rubric MCQ labels (\texttt{very\_low}, \texttt{low}, \texttt{high}, \texttt{very\_high}) are too coarse to reliably discriminate across the simulation output space sampled by different events; and (2) the current evaluator architecture uses a fixed small model (DeepSeek-R1-14B) without per-event calibration or few-shot examples. A third factor, confirmed rather than merely suspected, is added in Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}: the two evaluator calls this reliability measure compares run with all structured-output enforcement disabled, because the evaluator's model identifier matches a keyword filter in the serving client that is unrelated to the rubric's design. What this section's $\kappa$ values measure is accordingly confounded with that defect and should be re-measured once it is fixed before any rubric redesign is evaluated. Future ECN-BENCH versions will redesign the rubric with finer-grained ordinal scales (e.g., 7-point Likert) and event-specific few-shot calibration prompts to achieve $\kappa \geq 0.6$ (substantial agreement) as a minimum reliability threshold for EPI computation.

\subsection{Convergence Dynamics: JSD Analysis and Monotonic Convergence}
\label{sec:jsd_convergence}

The mean inter-round Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) values and monotonic convergence counts from the convergence telemetry reveal qualitatively distinct opinion dynamics across architectures:

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Convergence Telemetry Summary and Integrity Audit (all 30 events, $R=60$, Conditions B and C pooled; 60 units and 600 checkpoint values per model).}
\label{tab:convergence_jsd}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Mean} & \textbf{Pipeline} & \textbf{Placeholder} & \textbf{Constant} & \textbf{Strictly} \\
 & \textbf{JSD} & \textbf{``monotonic''} & \textbf{values} & \textbf{series} & \textbf{decreasing} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ} & 0.734 & 48 & 526/600 (87.7\%) & 48/60 & 29 \\
\textbf{Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1}   & 0.637 & 0  & 244/600 (40.7\%) & 0/60  & \textbf{133} \\
\textbf{Qwen2.5-7B-GGUF}  & 0.628 & 0  & 166/600 (27.7\%) & 0/60  & \textbf{113} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\smallskip\\
\footnotesize ``Placeholder values'' counts checkpoint readings exactly equal to $0.758277$, the constant the telemetry emits when no probed distribution is available. ``Constant series'' counts units whose ten checkpoint values are all identical. ``Strictly decreasing'' counts round-to-round transitions with a JSD decrease exceeding the pipeline tolerance $\epsilon = 0.002$, out of 540 transitions per model.
\end{table}

Earlier drafts of this paper reported a striking finding from the first two columns of Table~\ref{tab:convergence_jsd}: that \emph{only} Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic JSD convergence (48 episodes) while Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B register zero, and interpreted this as a qualitative difference in opinion dynamics across architectures. A direct audit of the underlying \texttt{round\_jsd} arrays shows that this interpretation is exactly inverted, and we retract it.

The telemetry emits a fixed constant, $0.758277$, whenever a checkpoint has no probed belief distribution to compare. For Llama-3.1-8B this constant accounts for $87.7\%$ of all checkpoint readings, and $48$ of its $60$ units have a completely flat ten-point series. The pipeline's monotonicity test flags a series when JSD never increases; a constant series satisfies that condition vacuously. The reported ``monotonic count'' of $48$ is exactly the number of flat, no-data series. Llama-3.1-8B did not converge---it produced no usable convergence trace at all.

Appendix~\ref{sec:outage} closes this finding by supplying its cause. The reason Llama-3.1-8B in particular has no probed belief distribution to compare is that its serving backend became unreachable during the seventh event of the campaign: from that point every one of the $1{,}000$ telemetry probes per unit failed with \texttt{Connection error} and the \texttt{trace} tables contain zero \texttt{interview} rows, so there was nothing for the JSD checkpoint to read. The $46$ Condition B and C units of the 23 affected events, plus the two units of the transitional event S7, account for the $48$ flat series exactly. The placeholder rate and the outage are the same phenomenon measured through two different fields, which is why the symptom is confined to one model and is total rather than partial within it.

Conversely, Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B have no constant series and register $133$ and $113$ strictly-decreasing round transitions respectively, against Llama-3.1-8B's $29$. They score zero on the pipeline's monotonicity flag because their traces are \emph{sawtooth}: checkpoints at rounds 6, 18, 30 and 42 return the placeholder while the intervening checkpoints return real measurements, so any real decline is interrupted by an artificial rise. Figure~\ref{fig:jsd_integrity} shows both effects. Panel A gives the placeholder share per checkpoint; panel B shows the mean trajectories, in which Mistral-7B declines from $0.758$ to $0.549$ and Qwen2.5-7B to $0.575$ while Llama-3.1-8B remains pinned near the placeholder throughout.

The correct statement of the evidence is therefore: \textbf{Claim 2 (Convergence) is untested by this campaign.} The only two models that produced usable traces both show declining inter-round divergence on the measured checkpoints, which is directionally consistent with DeGroot-style belief updating, but the measurement is punctured often enough that we decline to fit an opinion-dynamics model to it. Repairing the telemetry---ensuring a probed distribution exists at every checkpoint, and replacing the vacuous monotonicity flag with a test that requires a minimum number of valid readings---is a precondition for any convergence claim in a future campaign. We flag this as the second major defect of the July 2026 campaign, alongside the shared Condition A of Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}.

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figA_jsd_integrity.png}
\caption{Convergence telemetry integrity audit, Conditions B and C pooled. \textbf{Panel A:} share of units emitting the placeholder constant $0.758277$ at each checkpoint. Every model is placeholder-only at rounds 6 and 30; Llama-3.1-8B remains so at every checkpoint for $80$--$100\%$ of units. \textbf{Panel B:} mean inter-round JSD trajectory under Condition B. The sawtooth in the Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B traces is the alternation between placeholder checkpoints and real measurements; Llama-3.1-8B's near-flat line at the placeholder value is the absence of measurement, not the absence of belief change.}
\label{fig:jsd_integrity}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{Consolidated Corrections}
\label{sec:errata_summary}

An audit of this kind is only as credible as its own bookkeeping, so we state plainly what this version changes relative to earlier drafts. Twenty-five claims were withdrawn, corrected, restricted or relocated. Table~\ref{tab:errata_summary} groups them by class of change and names the most consequential instance in each; the full row-by-row table, giving the basis for every individual change, is Table~\ref{tab:errata} in Appendix~\ref{sec:errata_appendix}.

Two patterns in that list are worth naming, because they are the reason the audit was necessary at all rather than incidental to it. First, \emph{every} one of the withdrawn quantitative claims was invisible in the pipeline's own summary files and surfaced only on re-derivation from per-unit records---a completion flag, a monotonic-convergence count and a correlation coefficient were each reported as normal values while measuring nothing. Second, the three most consequential corrections are not about the models under test but about the apparatus: what the evaluator was, whether its output was schema-enforced, and whether the serving backend was alive. A benchmark's least-audited component is its own instrumentation, and that is where this paper's errors concentrated.

\begin{table*}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Consolidated errata, condensed by class of change. The full row-by-row table, with the basis for each individual change, is Table~\ref{tab:errata} in Appendix~\ref{sec:errata_appendix}.}
\label{tab:errata_summary}
\small

\section{Conclusion and Future Work}
\label{sec:conclusion}

This paper set out to characterize \textbf{loss of sharpness and degenerate consensus}: the tendency of iterative multi-agent LLM deliberation to erase a forecast's informativeness while its direction survives. Using \textbf{ECN-BENCH}, a multi-agent social simulation testbed (the MiroFish-Offline pipeline), we found that $20.6\%$ of simulated forecasts collapsed to an exactly uniform, non-informative distribution and that the remaining forecasts were measurably flattened relative to direct elicitation---two mechanical effects sufficient on their own to produce an aggregate result that, taken at face value (Section~\ref{sec:full_campaign}), would have read as a straightforward failure of synthetic crowd intelligence.

\textbf{The audit then moved the finding one level down, and that relocation is the paper's contribution.} An earlier draft attributed the pattern to the swarm models' own decoding stacks, because it was concentrated in the two vLLM-served models and absent from the Ollama-served one. Reading the scoring code withdrew that account: the \texttt{probabilities} field is produced by a single fixed evaluator regardless of which swarm model is under test, and that evaluator runs with structured-output enforcement silently disabled by a keyword match on its own model name (Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping}). Re-scoring all $270$ transcripts with a schema-enforced instrument cut the flat rate to $6.1\%$ and dissolved the cross-model pattern entirely (Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication}); a dose--response measured within the one campaign free of that confound then showed degeneracy rising from $5\%$ to $65\%$ with input length, while the same transcripts came back sharp under five independent reads (Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism}). The phenomenon is real and reproducible from the released artifacts, but it is a property of an unconstrained LLM asked to compress a long transcript into a distribution---not of the deliberation being compressed. What generalises from this paper is therefore not a claim about multi-agent deliberation at all, but a requirement: \textbf{a benchmark that extracts probabilities with an LLM must calibrate that extractor as an instrument}, and flat-unit rate is the cheapest diagnostic that separates an extraction artifact from a result.

We have also documented the systems engineering, containerization, and NFS database lock patches necessary to deploy these simulations reliably on air-gapped HPC resources (Appendix~\ref{sec:architecture}), and the multi-criteria (not purely performance-optimal) selection of the $N=300$, $R=60$ production configuration (Appendix~\ref{sec:optimal_config}) at which the finding was measured, since it required a working large-scale pipeline to surface at all.

The supporting benchmark campaign (Appendices~\ref{sec:full_campaign_supporting} and~\ref{sec:results}) across three model families (Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1, Qwen2.5-7B-GGUF, executed July 2026) yields nine further findings, reported as context for the central result rather than as independently validated claims:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Bimodal event structure is real; its cause is not established.} A stable subset (T2, C11, S9, S3, C3) gains from simulation across all models and another (T1, C10, C15, S6) loses, but the $r=0.847$ correlation previously advanced to explain this is an arithmetic artifact of regressing a difference on its own minuend (Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology}); the non-tautological association $r(BS_A, BS_B)$ is only $+0.206$ across events ($+0.130$ over the $90$ individual units). We withdraw the Difficulty--Lift Predictive Rule.
    \item \textbf{The aggregate lift is partly a contamination effect in the shared reference rather than a simulation effect.} Stratifying on each event's resolution date against each model's release date (Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff}) localises the negative lift to events predating the model's release for Qwen2.5-7B ($-0.25$, interval excluding zero, against $-0.02$ post-release) and for Mistral. Because Condition A is the shared record, that pattern measures memorisation in the reference, not damage from simulation. It does not hold for Llama-3.1-8B, whose lift is negative in both strata and larger on the post-release events ($-0.24$, excluding zero) that it could not have memorised; that campaign is also the one carrying a serving-stack and evaluator substitution (\S\ref{sec:outage}), so we do not attribute the difference to the model. The date criterion agrees with the earlier $BS_A$-based proxy on only $18$--$21$ of $30$ events.
    \item \textbf{Susceptibility ordering holds in sign for two of three models on the single pass, and strengthens into the paper's one positive result when measured properly.} On the single-pass, full-30-event reading (recomputed 2026-08-16 for Llama post-repair, \S\ref{sec:outage}), $BS_C > BS_B$ for Mistral-7B ($+0.115$) and Qwen2.5-7B ($+0.053$); Llama-3.1-8B's sign depends on which of the three August evaluators reads its transcripts ($-0.122$, $+0.093$, $+0.027$), so we treat it as undetermined (\S\ref{sec:cutoff}). Only Mistral-7B's interval excludes zero. Re-measured on four-read means over the $20$ contamination-clean events common to all four campaigns (Section~\ref{sec:predictive_signal}), the pooled margin is $+0.112$ Brier $[+0.042, +0.182]$ and $+19.4$ points of directional accuracy $[+7.5, +31.3]$ (recomputed 2026-08-21 once Qwen2.5-14B's recovery grew the common clean-event set from $17$ to $20$; was $+0.125$ $[+0.053,+0.195]$ and $+18.4$ $[+6.6,+30.9]$), with two of four per-model Brier intervals excluding zero individually---and the model that carried the single-pass result, Mistral-7B, is one of the two that does not. Being a contrast between two simulated conditions, it never touches the shared Condition A record and survives knowledge-cutoff stratification unchanged. This is the benchmark's most trustworthy positive signal, and Section~\ref{sec:predictive_signal} promotes it from a supporting observation to a stated conclusion.
    \item \textbf{The original Convergence hypothesis is untested, and the previously reported result was inverted.} Section~\ref{sec:jsd_convergence} shows the pipeline's ``monotonic convergence'' count of 48 for Llama-3.1-8B in fact counts flat, no-data series ($87.7\%$ placeholder checkpoints, traced to the backend outage of Appendix~\ref{sec:outage}); Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B, reported as showing zero convergence, are the only two models with usable traces and both decline. No opinion-dynamics claim can be sustained until the telemetry is repaired.
    \item \textbf{Interaction volume does not predict calibration.} Llama-3.1-8B (27,087 actions) and Qwen2.5-7B (723) reach nearly identical Brier scores while the older, unquantised Mistral (9,364 actions) scores worst---correlational and confounded with model age, not architecture. Once memorised events are excluded, the three campaigns' Brier scores fall within $0.024$ of one another (Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff}), leaving no calibration difference to predict.
    \item \textbf{The campaign's run integrity was not uniform, and the artifacts said so.} Appendices~\ref{sec:persona_audit}--\ref{sec:evidence_depth} find the reference model's serving backend died during its seventh event, collapsing its credited $300$-agent persona pool to $11$ event-independent archetypes and extracting forecasts from connection-error logs on 24 of 30 events (23 fully dead plus the transitional seventh)---none of this visible in the result records, every one of which reported success. Completion flags written by the orchestrator are not evidence; the artifacts must be audited independently of them. These 24 events were re-simulated and re-scored 2026-08-16 (\S\ref{sec:outage}); the audit above documents the defect as originally discovered and is retained for that purpose, not as a description of the current data.
    \item \textbf{Evaluator reliability is insufficient for the 7-dimension rubric.} All seven dimensions fall below the reliability cutoff for all three models ($\max \kappa = 0.322$ for Llama post-repair, on \texttt{susceptibility}; was $0.412$ on \texttt{prediction\_accuracy}); the EPI composite is not computable. Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping} traces this to the same schema-disabling defect as item 8 below: both reliability-check calls run with structured-output enforcement off, so $\kappa$ here measures generation-to-generation noise in an unconstrained model, not disagreement between independent raters.
    \item \textbf{A third measurement channel, Epistemic Mapping, is confirmed non-functional locally, with a verified mechanism.} All $2{,}430$ micro-question instances in the production campaigns return ``Missing in LLM response,'' while $41$ OpenRouter-served pilot runs succeed $237/237$ times. Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping} traces this to \texttt{chat\_json()} disabling structured-output enforcement whenever the evaluator's model string matches a ``reasoning model'' keyword filter---which \texttt{"deepseek-r1-14b"} does, uniformly across all three campaigns---combined with a retry loop that never checks \texttt{micro\_epistemic\_mapping} for completeness before silently backfilling a placeholder. This implicates the evaluator's configuration, not its capability, and also invalidates the vLLM-versus-Ollama account offered in an earlier draft for the flat-forecast finding, since the same fixed evaluator produces \texttt{probabilities} regardless of which swarm model is under test.
    \item \textbf{The benchmark comparison itself is confounded on three axes and underpowered.} Condition A was shared rather than run per model, model generation/precision/serving stack vary jointly across campaigns, and achieved statistical power is $\leq 9\%$ at $E=30$ events. None of this weakens the sharpness-loss finding, which is measured at the $n=180$ per-unit level and does not depend on cross-model or cross-condition significance testing.
\end{enumerate}

Taken together, this paper is best understood as a phenomenon report enabled by, and extracted from, an instructive benchmark failure. The infrastructure works: 270 of 270 units completed, with no evaluator fallbacks, across three model families on an air-gapped cluster. The benchmark comparison built on top of that infrastructure does not yet support its original headline claims, for three reasons we consider generalisable to the field: a baseline that was shared rather than per-model, telemetry that failed silently by emitting a plausible constant instead of an error, and a headline correlation that was arithmetic rather than empirical. Each was invisible in the aggregate summary files and surfaced only on re-derivation from per-unit records.

Two things survived that audit, and both sharpened rather than weakened under it. The first is the sharpness-loss finding itself: a documentable tendency for the extraction channel to trade forecast informativeness for consensus, now localised to an interaction between long deliberation transcripts and one schema-disabled instrument (Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism}), and worth studying independently of whether the surrounding benchmark's other claims hold up. The second is substantive rather than methodological, and we state it as a positive result rather than as a caveat: \textbf{on events that resolved after every model's release date, this multi-agent simulation produces forecasts that are strongly informative about how real social events turned out}---the correct outcome carries the most probability mass in $82\%$ of (model, event) pairs against a $33\%$ chance baseline, $p = 3.7\times10^{-22}$---\textbf{and those forecasts demonstrably track the content of the evidence deliberated over}, degrading by $0.112$ Brier $[+0.042, +0.182]$ when the injected evidence is replaced with topically unrelated material (Section~\ref{sec:predictive_signal}). Multi-agent LLM simulation is therefore not merely producing plausible-looking text about social events; on this catalogue it carries real, content-dependent signal about their outcomes.

That signal is not, however, shown to come from the deliberation. A single-agent baseline run after the audit---one retrieval-augmented chain-of-thought call per event, given the same seed dossier and the same injected update the swarm's agents received---reproduces, and on the identity-matched pool slightly exceeds, the directional accuracy outright ($0.834$ against the swarm's $0.819$ pooled over all four arms) and carries the lower mean Brier on both arms where the single agent and the campaign use the same checkpoint, though at $20$ events no interval separates the two ($-0.106$ Brier $[-0.226, +0.009]$, clustered $p = 0.109$; Section~\ref{sec:cot_baseline}). The honest statement is a non-superiority one: three hundred agents deliberating for sixty rounds, plus a transcript and an evaluator to compress it, buy no measurable accuracy over a single call, and might cost some. The content-sensitivity result stands apart from this, being a contrast between two simulated conditions for which the single-agent run has no analogue---so what remains specifically attributable to deliberation is that it responds to which evidence it is given, not that it forecasts better than the model does alone. Converting a demonstrated signal into a demonstrated \emph{benefit} now requires two experiments rather than one: the per-model Condition A re-run, and a single-agent arm large enough to resolve a $0.11$ Brier difference, which at the observed variance ($d_z = 0.39$) means $E = 55$ events for $80\%$ power rather than the $20$ available here, at which the achieved power is $37\%$.

Beyond the central finding, this paper establishes an empirically validated \textbf{Sub-15B Exclusion Criterion} (Appendix~\ref{sec:small_model_limits}) for local vLLM HPC execution, rooted in three compounding failure modes: context-window overflow, VRAM saturation, and guided-decoding JSON schema incompatibility. Both Gemma 1.1 2B-IT and CodeGemma 7B-IT were confirmed unable to pass Stage 1 persona extraction, immediately raising \texttt{ValueError: Intelligent persona generation failed} and aborting.

\textbf{Future work} is ordered by what must be fixed before anything else is worth measuring.

\emph{Prerequisites, in order.} (i) \textbf{Partially discharged by Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication}.} The experiment this list previously named as highest-priority---restore structured-output enforcement and re-measure the flat-forecast rate---has now been run, by re-scoring all $270$ transcripts with a second evaluator whose enforcement is active rather than by patching the keyword filter in \texttt{chat\_json()}. The flat-forecast rate falls from $20.6\%$ to $6.1\%$ and Condition B degeneracy disappears entirely, which is why this paper now locates the phenomenon in the evaluator. Two parts of the original item remain open and should be done by fixing the defect in place---excluding \texttt{"deepseek-r1-14b"} from the \texttt{"reasoning model"} keyword filter, aliasing it to a served-model-name that does not match \texttt{"r1"}, or adding an explicit code path restoring \texttt{json\_object} enforcement for local reasoning-named evaluators---so that the \emph{same} evaluator can be compared with and without enforcement: whether enforcement raises rubric $\kappa$, and whether it eliminates the micro-question failure, are not answered by swapping to a different model. (ii) \textbf{Calibrate the evaluator as an instrument before drawing any further inference from it.} Section~\ref{sec:replication_noise} finds $4/30$ reproducibility on byte-identical input, with a mean Brier spread of $0.128$ and a maximum of $0.715$, so every future campaign should run the evaluator $k \geq 3$ times per unit and report the between-run spread, and should score with at least two independent evaluators. Any effect smaller than the instrument's own spread is not measurable by this design.

The remaining prerequisite items and the substantive testbed extensions (re-running Condition A per model, repairing the convergence telemetry, decomposing the Brier score, and eight further items) are testbed engineering rather than central-finding follow-ups; we list them in full in Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_future_work}. Recovering the Qwen2.5-14B campaign, listed there in earlier drafts, is no longer outstanding: Appendix~\ref{sec:fourth_model_status} backfills it and Section~\ref{sec:four_model_extension} scores it against the other three models.
