\section{Supporting Analysis and Run-Integrity Audit}
\label{sec:full_campaign_supporting}

This appendix collects the rest of the July 2026 campaign's findings---event-level structure, interaction logs, contamination analysis, and the run-integrity audit---together with the long-form logs behind them. It is context for the audit of Part~I, not a set of independently validated benchmark results: every quantity here carries the same caveats as the body (shared Condition~A reference, three-way provenance confound, low statistical power) and, where it derives from a score rather than from an artifact on disk, comes from the single Evaluator~J pass whose defect Section~\ref{sec:epistemic_mapping} documents. It is placed in an appendix for that reason, and because several of its subsections are reference logs rather than argument. The claims it withdraws or revises are consolidated in Appendix~\ref{sec:errata_appendix}.

\subsection{Simulation-Benefiting vs.~Simulation-Degrading Events: A Structural Analysis}
\label{sec:sbe_sde}

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig6_volatility_vs_lift.png}
\caption{Event Difficulty (BS$_A$) vs.~Simulation Lift (BS$_A$ - BS$_B$). Each point represents one of the 90 (event, model) pairs, with marker shape encoding model architecture and point color encoding taxonomy category. The apparent correlation ($r = 0.847$, the value as originally reported) is an arithmetic artifact: the vertical axis contains the horizontal axis as its minuend, and $r = +0.862$ is expected here even under an uninformative BS$_B$. This panel is the pre-repair rendering; on the corrected data of \S\ref{sec:outage} the same relationship is $r = +0.837$, which changes none of the argument. See Figure~\ref{fig:tautology} for the same data with the shared term removed ($r = +0.206$) and Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology} for the derivation. This figure is retained to document the relationship as originally reported; it should not be read as evidence that baseline difficulty predicts simulation benefit. Annotated events show the most extreme cases.}
\label{fig:volatility_lift}
\end{figure*}

A central empirical finding of this campaign is the existence of two structurally distinct event classes, which we formalize as \textbf{Simulation-Benefiting Events} (SBE) and \textbf{Simulation-Degrading Events} (SDE).

\subsubsection{SBE Structural Profile}

Figure~\ref{fig:volatility_lift} plots all 90 (event, model) pairs on the difficulty--lift plane from which the two clusters are read; as its caption and Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology} set out, the trend visible there is arithmetic, so the clusters below are a descriptive partition rather than evidence of a difficulty--benefit relationship.

The SBE cluster (T2, C11, S9, S3, C3, C6, S5, C1, S7; $n=9$) exhibits the following defining characteristics:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{High baseline uncertainty:} Mean $BS_A = 0.799$ (vs.~$0.188$ for SDE). These are events where the No-Sim direct elicitation is poorly calibrated, indicating the LLM has limited or contradictory prior knowledge.
    \item \textbf{Strong B$<$C gap:} Mean B-to-C gap $= +0.238$, indicating that social deliberation with relevant information (Cond B) substantially outperforms null noise injection (Cond C). Unlike the lift, this contrast shares no term between its two sides and is therefore not subject to the artifact of Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology}.
    \item \textbf{Cross-model consistency:} SBE events show positive lift across all 3 models in 5/9 cases ($55.6\%$): T2, C11, S9, C3 and S7. The remaining four (S3, S5, C1 with 2/3; C6 with 1/3) are majority- rather than unanimously-positive.
    \item \textbf{Diverse taxonomy:} SBE events span all three taxonomy categories (4 Social/Electoral, 4 Senate/Binary, 1 Technical/Economic), suggesting high-uncertainty events exist across domains.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{SDE Structural Profile}

The SDE cluster is the complement of the SBE set: T1, C10, C15, S6, T5, C2, C9, S2, C14, C12, C7, S4, T9, T4, T6, T8, C8, C5, C4, plus C13 and T3 ($n=21$; earlier drafts listed only 19 and left C13 and T3 unassigned, so that the two clusters summed to 28 rather than 30). It exhibits contrasting characteristics:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{High prior certainty:} Mean $BS_A = 0.207$ over the corrected $n=21$ set. These events already have correct directional forecasts under the shared Condition A reference.
    \item \textbf{Near-null B$<$C gap:} Mean B-to-C gap $= +0.015$, an order of magnitude below the SBE value of $+0.238$, indicating the swarm barely distinguishes real information from null noise on these events---the social simulation is operating near the susceptibility floor.
    \item \textbf{Cross-model coherence in degradation:} 0/21 SDE events show positive lift across all 3 models simultaneously.
    \item \textbf{Mechanism:} For SDE events, the injected information at $R=30$ conflicts with the swarm's already-formed high-certainty collective prior. Rather than updating productively, agents engage in polarization dynamics that \emph{amplify} the conflict, pushing probability mass away from the correct answer. This is analogous to the ``groupthink inversion'' phenomenon in human deliberation research~\cite{surowiecki2005}.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{The Difficulty--Lift Correlation Is an Arithmetic Artifact}
\label{sec:tautology}

The Pearson correlation between $BS_A$ (event difficulty at baseline) and mean cross-model lift is $r = +0.837$ ($r^2 = 0.700$) on the corrected data---it was $r = 0.847$ ($r^2 = 0.717$) before the repair of \S\ref{sec:outage}, and the argument below is insensitive to which value is used---and earlier drafts of this paper read it as evidence that \textbf{event uncertainty explains 71.7\% of the variance in simulation lift}. That reading is not defensible, and we retract it.

The quantity on the vertical axis is $Lift = BS_A - BS_B$, which contains the quantity on the horizontal axis as its minuend. Regressing a difference on one of its own terms produces a large positive correlation by construction, with no contribution from the data. If $BS_B$ were statistically independent of $BS_A$, the expected correlation would be
\begin{equation}
    r_{\text{mech}} = \frac{\sigma_A}{\sqrt{\sigma_A^2 + \sigma_B^2}}
      = \frac{0.380}{\sqrt{0.380^2 + 0.223^2}} = 0.862 ,
\end{equation}
using the observed dispersions $\sigma_A = 0.380$ and $\sigma_B = 0.223$. The observed $r = +0.837$ is thus \emph{below} the value expected under pure arithmetic coupling with an uninformative $BS_B$. The correlation carries no evidence for a difficulty--benefit relationship.

The non-tautological test is the association between baseline difficulty and simulated performance directly, $r(BS_A, BS_B)$, which shares no term between axes. That correlation is $\mathbf{+0.206}$ ($r^2 = 0.042$) on the $30$ event means, $+0.130$ ($r^2 = 0.017$) pooled over the $90$ individual units, and $+0.251$, $-0.078$, $+0.172$ for Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B individually. Llama's is the only one that moved with the repair of \S\ref{sec:outage} (from $+0.127$), and at $n=30$ on a single evaluator pass we do not read the change as informative; it is reported so that the appendix matches the released data. Figure~\ref{fig:tautology} plots both panels on the same data. \textbf{Baseline difficulty does not predict how well the swarm forecasts an event.} We therefore withdraw the ``Difficulty--Lift Predictive Rule'' proposed in earlier drafts, including its use as a screening heuristic for allocating simulation budget: on this evidence, a preliminary No-Sim elicitation tells a practitioner nothing about whether simulation will help.

We retain the SBE/SDE partition as a \emph{descriptive} split, because the per-event lift values genuinely are bimodal, but we emphasise that ``SBE'' is largely a relabelling of ``events on which the shared Condition A reference happened to score poorly.'' Since $BS_B$ is roughly constant across strata, selecting events by high $BS_A$ mechanically selects events with high $BS_A - BS_B$. Establishing a genuine structural determinant of simulation benefit requires a covariate measured independently of both conditions---for instance resolution date relative to training cutoff, outcome entropy, or option count---which we identify as the priority for the next campaign.

\subsubsection{Two Units Read End to End: What the Partition Looks Like Up Close}
\label{sec:case_studies}

The partition above is a statement about scores. This subsection reads the two extremes as documents. We take both from the Llama-3.1-8B campaign so that the contrast is internal to one model, one serving stack and one evaluator pass, and because that campaign is the only one whose evidence carries no telemetry-probe content (Table~\ref{tab:probe_share}); the same pair read on Qwen2.5-7B would confound the comparison with the extraction defect of \S\ref{sec:evidence_depth}. Probability vectors below are the mean of the four Evaluator~A reads.

\textbf{C11, the extreme SBE: ``Who will win the 2024 Nobel Peace Prize?'' (ground truth: Nihon Hidankyo).} Condition~A is not merely uncertain but wrong: it puts $0.47$ on \emph{Other} and only $0.12$ on the correct option, for $BS_A = 1.055$ on a five-option question. Condition~B moves the mode onto Nihon Hidankyo at $0.30$ and halves the loss to $BS_B = 0.609$. The transcript, however, does not contain a deliberation that arrives at that answer. Its sixty lines are generic advocacy---the United Nations as a model of cooperation, sustainable development, systemic injustice---and no agent names the eventual laureate on the merits. What the transcript does contain is the round-30 injection, which states that observers note ``an unusual thematic focus from Oslo on the legacy of atomic-bomb survivor advocacy'' and ``suggests a logically stronger probability for Hidankyo-aligned organizations.'' It also contains two lines spoken by an agent whose display name \emph{is} the ground-truth option, an instance of the label leak quantified in \S\ref{sec:evidence_depth}. The lift on this event is therefore best read as retrieval of an injected cue, assisted by a leaked label, rather than as an emergent forecast; and a single RAG+CoT call given the same dossier and the same injection scores $0.638$, which is not distinguishable from the swarm's $0.609$.

\textbf{S6, the extreme SDE: ``Which party wins the most seats in the 2024 Austrian legislative election?'' (ground truth: FP\"O).} Here Condition~A is very nearly right---$0.82$ on FP\"O, $BS_A = 0.053$---and the simulation destroys it: Condition~B places $0.71$ on \emph{Other} and $0.05$ on FP\"O, for $BS_B = 1.448$. S6 is one of the six events that preceded the serving outage, so unlike most of this campaign it carries a dense, fully logged deliberation: $251$ content actions from $185$ distinct agents across both platforms, $91{,}582$ characters. Reading it explains the collapse without recourse to polarization dynamics. The discussion is not about Austria. Agents converge, agreeably and at length, on youth environmental leadership, intergenerational sustainability curricula, frameworks for evaluating intersectional policy, and applications of AI to urban planning; the recurring rhetorical move is ``I completely agree.'' The single on-topic prediction in the sampled evidence forecasts a win for the Greens, which is not one of the four options. A sixty-line sample of that material contains no reason to prefer FP\"O, and the evaluator's mass drifts to the residual category. One CoT call on the same dossier keeps the correct answer at $BS = 0.097$.

\textbf{What the pair shows.} The two events are conventionally read as deliberation helping where the prior is weak and hurting where the prior is strong. The documents support a plainer mechanism: in both cases the evaluator is scoring the topical content of a sixty-line sample, and the sample's relationship to the question is what moves the score. C11 improves because the sample contains a directly relevant injected sentence; S6 collapses because a large volume of fluent, on-consensus, off-topic text displaces a correct prior. This also predicts the null we report elsewhere---that transcript volume does not correlate with lift ($r = +0.016$ over the forty matched-arm units)---since the extractor caps every sample at sixty lines regardless of how much was produced, so volume varies between logging regimes rather than between events. We offer this as an interpretation of two units, not as a tested mechanism; the corresponding experiment is the extractor ablation proposed in \S\ref{sec:evidence_depth}.

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figD_difficulty_tautology.png}
\caption{The difficulty--lift correlation is arithmetic, not empirical. \textbf{Panel A} reproduces the relationship the pipeline reported: mean simulation lift against baseline difficulty, $r = +0.837$. Because $Lift \equiv BS_A - BS_B$, a correlation of $r = +0.862$ is expected here even if $BS_B$ were statistically independent of $BS_A$; the observed value is below that null. \textbf{Panel B} plots the same 30 events with the shared term removed---baseline difficulty against With-Sim score---giving $r = +0.206$ ($r^2 = 0.042$). Marker colour encodes taxonomy category. Each point is the mean over the three model campaigns.}
\label{fig:tautology}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{Model Interaction Realism: A Critical Evaluation}
\label{sec:interaction_realism}

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig5_jsd_convergence.png}
\caption{\textbf{Superseded.} Round-by-round JSD profiles for selected high-lift (left) and low-lift (right) events using Llama-3.1-8B, Condition B, as generated for earlier drafts. This figure should be read only alongside Figure~\ref{fig:jsd_integrity} and Section~\ref{sec:jsd_convergence}: $87.7\%$ of Llama-3.1-8B's checkpoint readings are the constant placeholder $0.758277$, so the apparent differences between the two panels do not reflect measured belief dynamics. It is retained for transparency about what the pipeline emitted, not as evidence of convergence.}
\label{fig:jsd_convergence}
\end{figure*}

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig7_directional_matrix.png}
\caption{Directional Accuracy Matrix: Condition B. Each cell shows WIN/50-50/LOSS for the given (model, event) pair. Green = correct direction, orange = wrong direction, neutral = 50-50. Cross-model agreement is highest for SBE events (T2, C11, S9 show WIN across all three models), while SDE events show heterogeneous patterns. Qwen-7B achieves the most robust WIN preservation, losing only 7 events to LOSS in Condition B vs.~12 for Mistral-7B.}
\label{fig:directional_matrix}
\end{figure*}

A critical question for the validity of ECN-BENCH as a research benchmark is whether the agent interaction dynamics exhibit \textbf{realistic social behavior} analogous to human deliberation, or whether they represent superficial textual mimicry without genuine epistemic updating. The empirical data, qualitative analysis of simulation logs, and convergence telemetry permit the following structured assessment. Figure~\ref{fig:jsd_convergence} reproduces the round-by-round convergence profiles on which an earlier draft based this assessment; it is retained only as a record of what the pipeline emitted, and Section~\ref{sec:jsd_convergence} explains why its apparent structure is a placeholder artifact rather than measured belief dynamics.

\subsubsection{Evidence for Genuine Epistemic Interaction}

Several indicators are consistent with selective belief updating in the simulated network:

\textbf{(i) Belief trajectory divergence between Conditions B and C:} For SBE events (T2, S9, C11), the With-Sim and Null Control conditions produce substantially different final probability estimates despite starting from identical persona sets. The B-to-C gap of $+0.238$ for SBE events arises from the fact that the round-30 injection enters the telemetry-probe context differently under Conditions B and C, allowing agents in Condition B to condition on relevant signal while the Null Control introduces irrelevant noise.

\textbf{(ii) Declining JSD on the models with usable telemetry:} Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B---the only two models whose convergence telemetry is not dominated by placeholder values (Section~\ref{sec:jsd_convergence})---show mean inter-round divergence falling from $0.758$ to $0.549$ and $0.575$ respectively over the measured checkpoints, which is directionally consistent with DeGroot sequential belief updating. We attach no weight to the corresponding Llama-3.1-8B trace, which is $87.7\%$ placeholder. This indicator is accordingly weaker than earlier drafts claimed, and applies to the opposite pair of models.

\subsubsection{Action-Log Audit: Model-Dependent Social Interaction Density (Authoritative .db Counts)}

A direct audit of the authoritative SQLite \texttt{trace} tables across 1,380 database files (Tables~\ref{tab:action_density_30event}--\ref{tab:action_breakdown}) reveals a four-tier model-dependent social-action density. Two scope conditions on that audit must be read alongside it, and both are established in Appendix~\ref{sec:outage}. First, the 1,380 files span every campaign including the $N$- and $R$-sweeps, whereas the Brier scores of this paper come from the 30-event campaign alone, which contributes exactly 120 databases per model; restricted to those, the ranking below changes and Table~\ref{tab:action_30event_only} supersedes the counts quoted here for any statement about the scored campaign. Second, $96.7\%$ of Llama-3.1-8B's social actions in the 30-event campaign fall in the six events that preceded a serving-backend failure, so the four-tier ordering is a statement about that model's healthy runs and not about its campaign. We note that earlier drafts of this analysis relied on the \texttt{actions.jsonl} summary files, which systematically undercount organic actions due to a cursor-tracking bug during telemetry-probe rounds (organic actions written during probe rounds are read by the cursor but discarded, as only \texttt{INTERVIEW} actions are logged); the \texttt{.db} counts reported here supersede those earlier figures.

\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ---Richest social interaction.} Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ produces the richest social interaction: 27,087 total social actions (40.31/DB), including 8,079 posts, 5,421 comments, 5,993 follows, and 3,010 comment-likes. The high \texttt{follow} and \texttt{comment\_like} volumes indicate that Llama-8B agents build dense peer networks and engage deeply with peers' contributions---the strongest evidence of genuine social deliberation in the benchmark.

\textbf{Mistral-7B---Antagonistic/\allowbreak broadcast profile.} Mistral-7B is the second most interactive (9,364 actions, 35.47/DB) but exhibits a qualitatively different profile: high posting (4,476) and liking (1,946) but disproportionately high disliking (67 vs.\ Llama's 7) and low comment-liking (167 vs.\ Llama's 3,010). This antagonistic/\allowbreak broadcast-style pattern---agents express agreement or disagreement with posts but rarely engage in threaded comment-level discussion---is consistent with conformity and polarization dynamics rather than deliberative consensus-building.

\textbf{Qwen2.5-14B---Analysis-focused interaction.} Qwen2.5-14B (full 30-event campaign, 180 DBs) produces moderate, analysis-focused interaction (2,407 actions, 13.38/DB): 1,215 posts and 585 comments but only 11 follows. Agents write substantive contributions and reply to peers but build almost no social network---a ``lone analyst'' pattern distinct from Llama's network-building and Mistral's broadcast style.

\textbf{Qwen2.5-7B---Near-dormant.} Qwen2.5-7B is the only near-dormant model (723 actions, 2.74/DB), with minimal activity across all action types (592 posts, 108 comments, 16 likes, 1 follow). Under the deployed configuration, its social-interaction channel is effectively dormant.

\textbf{Mechanism implication.} The mechanism of measured Brier lift is therefore strongly model-dependent. For Qwen-7B, lift is attributable to diverse-persona ensemble aggregation plus mid-simulation injection---the social channel is dormant. For Qwen-14B, lift includes a modest analysis-focused contribution component (posts and comments without network-building). For Mistral-7B, lift reflects a mixture of persona-ensemble, antagonistic social interaction (high dislike, low comment-like), and injection effects. For Llama-8B, lift includes a genuine social-deliberation component: agents build peer networks (5,993 follows), engage in threaded discussion (5,421 comments, 3,010 comment-likes), and their posts enter peers' later telemetry-probe timelines. Claims of ``emergent crowd intelligence through social deliberation'' are best supported for Llama-8B; partially supported for Mistral and Qwen-14B; and not supported for Qwen-7B. These are statements about the observed action traces, not about the architectures, for the provenance reasons of Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}. We further restrict the Llama-8B half of that sentence: within the scored 30-event campaign it holds on six events and is false on the other twenty-three, where the swarm produced four social actions in total (Appendix~\ref{sec:outage}). The mechanism statement for Llama-8B should therefore be read as conditional on a working serving backend, which the campaign had for one fifth of its events.

\textbf{Correlational observation.} A four-model correlational observation emerges: interaction VOLUME alone does not predict Brier calibration. Llama-8B (most interaction, 40.31/DB) and Qwen-7B (least interaction, 2.74/DB) achieve nearly identical aggregate Brier scores ($BS_B$ = 0.484 vs.\ 0.481), while Mistral-7B (second-most interaction, 35.47/DB) has the worst Brier ($BS_B$ = 0.531). One candidate distinguishing factor is interaction \emph{quality}: Llama-8B's high \texttt{follow} + \texttt{comment\_like} co-occurs with good calibration, while Mistral's high \texttt{dislike} + low \texttt{comment\_like} co-occurs with poor calibration. We stress that this is a correlational observation over four models with no attempt at control, and that a competing explanation is available for the same pattern: the model with the antagonistic profile is also the oldest by roughly ten months and the only one served at full precision (Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}). Four points cannot separate interaction quality from model generation.

\subsubsection{Evidence for Behavioral Homogenization (Critical Limitation)}

The simulation logs also reveal systematic limitations:

\textbf{(i) Prior anchoring in SDE events:} For events with low $BS_A$ (e.g., C10, C12, C15), the baseline No-Sim evaluation already reflects a strong, confident prior probability. When the simulation begins, agents anchor their responses to this pre-existing LLM prior rather than engaging with the seed context analytically. At $R=30$, the injected intelligence is processed as a \emph{perturbation to a settled consensus} rather than new evidence for consideration, triggering social pressure dynamics that paradoxically increase miscalibration.

\textbf{(ii) Cross-model behavioral convergence (Directional Matrix, Figure~\ref{fig:directional_matrix}):} For SDE events, all three models show identical directional errors (LOSS) despite using different architectures, persona DNA, and serving stacks. This cross-architectural agreement on wrong answers indicates that the simulation is amplifying a shared LLM prior bias rather than discovering event-specific signal. True social deliberation would be expected to show more disagreement between architecturally distinct models on borderline cases.

\subsubsection{Topology and Echo-Chamber Risks}

Earlier drafts stated that the OASIS Twitter topology uses a power-law degree distribution with $\alpha \approx 2.3$. We withdraw that figure: it was not measured, and the run manifest for every campaign records \texttt{degree\_distribution\_descriptor: "oasis-default-unspecified"} with \texttt{clustering\_coefficient: null}. The graphs were built by the framework's default \texttt{generate\_twitter\_agent\_graph} and \texttt{generate\_reddit\_agent\_graph} constructors, whose realised degree distribution we did not instrument. The structural concern nevertheless stands in qualitative form: if the generated graph is heavy-tailed, a small number of highly-connected ``hub'' agents could dominate information flow, so that a position posted early by a hub reaches later agents through multiple social paths and produces \emph{correlated} rather than independent belief updates. However, the .db audit shows the social-action channel is substantial for Llama-8B (27,087 actions) and Mistral-7B (9,364) but near-dormant for Qwen-7B (723). For Llama-8B, the high follow count (5,993) creates dense network connectivity where echo-chamber effects could theoretically amplify hub opinions; for Mistral-7B, the high dislike count (67) suggests early polarization dynamics. For Qwen-7B and Qwen-14B (minimal follow: 1 and 11 respectively), topology effects are moot since agents do not build networks. Empirical validation of echo-chamber dynamics requires graph-level analysis of the follow network, which is pending.

\subsubsection{Implications for Benchmark Validity}

The action-log audit establishes an important, model-dependent scope condition: for Qwen-7B, ECN-BENCH primarily measures persona-ensemble probabilistic aggregation under mid-simulation injection (social channel dormant). For Qwen-14B, it additionally captures analysis-focused posting and commenting without network-building. For Mistral-7B, it captures a persona-ensemble + antagonistic-social + injection mixture. For Llama-8B, it captures genuine social deliberation with peer-network building and threaded engagement. This does not invalidate the measured Brier scores---it indicates that different mechanisms are in play across the campaigns. We say ``across campaigns'' rather than ``by architecture'' deliberately: the campaigns differ in model generation, precision and serving stack as well as architecture (Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}). We note that the difficulty--lift correlation invoked in earlier drafts to delimit where the persona ensemble adds value is an artifact (Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology}) and no longer supports that boundary; the SBE/SDE split remains descriptive only.

To strengthen the social-interaction channel and enable claims of deliberative emergence, we propose the following architectural modifications: (1) \textbf{Forcing a minimum posting rate} for Qwen-7B (near-dormant); (2) \textbf{Network-building incentives} (follow/reply requirements) for Qwen-14B (analysis without network); (3) \textbf{Anti-antagonism mechanisms} (reduce dislike, incentivize comment-level engagement) for Mistral (antagonistic/\allowbreak broadcast pattern), after first re-testing that model at matched generation and precision; (4) \textbf{Reply-to-peer requirements} and \textbf{information drip control} to extend Llama-8B's already-rich deliberation to all event types; (5) \textbf{Diversity penalties} on repeated content across all models.

\subsection{Temporal Data Leakage and Training Contamination: Reinterpreting Simulation Lift}
\label{sec:contamination}

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig9_contamination_analysis.png}
\caption{Temporal Data Leakage and Contamination Analysis. Panel A: Condition A Brier score per event colored by contamination likelihood (Red = Contaminated $BS_A < 0.15$, Yellow = Borderline $0.15 \le BS_A < 0.35$, Green = Likely Clean $BS_A \ge 0.35$). 11 of 30 events (36.7\%) fall below the contamination threshold; because Condition A is a single shared record (Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}), this classification is identical across models by construction rather than by agreement. Panel B: $BS_A$ vs.\ Lift scatter with contamination coloring---see Figure~\ref{fig:tautology} and Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology} for why the trend in this panel is arithmetic. Panel C: contamination-corrected lift, which moves from $\approx -0.10$ to $\approx +0.19$--$+0.20$; Figure~\ref{fig:lift_stratified} adds the confidence intervals, all of which span zero, and Appendix~\ref{sec:contamination} explains why thresholding on $BS_A$ makes this reversal partly self-fulfilling.}
\label{fig:contamination_analysis}
\end{figure*}

A crucial methodological insight arising from our empirical campaign is the presence of severe \textbf{temporal data leakage and pre-training contamination}. In benchmarking predictive multi-agent systems, a fundamental prerequisite is that the evaluation events represent genuine \emph{forward-looking uncertainty} relative to the model's knowledge cutoff. If an event outcome was already fixed and heavily represented in the pre-training text corpus or instruction tuning datasets of the LLM, the model does not perform probabilistic forecasting; instead, it performs static factual retrieval (memorized recall).

\subsubsection{Quantifying Contamination Severity}

Tables~\ref{tab:per_event_llama_8b}--\ref{tab:per_event_qwen_7b} reveal that for $11$ of the $30$ events ($36.7\%$), the Condition A baseline Brier score is near zero ($BS_A < 0.15$): C10, C12, C14, C15, C2, C4, S2, S6, T1, T4 and T9. For seven of these, the score is essentially zero ($BS_A \le 0.045$; e.g.\ C12: $0.0005$, C10: $0.0036$, T9: $0.0000$).

We must correct a claim made in earlier drafts, which reported that ``the exact same 11 events are classified as contaminated across all three independently trained model families'' and treated this unanimity as corroborating evidence. That agreement is vacuous. As established in Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}, Condition A was executed once and copied to all three campaigns, so the three models share a single $BS_A$ vector by construction and could not possibly have disagreed. The unanimity is an artifact of the campaign design and carries no evidential weight whatsoever. What remains is the weaker but still meaningful observation that a single direct-elicitation pass answers 11 of 30 events almost perfectly.

That pattern is consistent with these events (such as S2: ``US Senate Control 2024'', C10: ``2024 UK Prime Minister'', T9: ``OpenAI AGI 2025'') having resolved before the training cutoff or having had an overwhelming, unambiguous consensus in pre-training data---in which case Condition A is performing memorised recall rather than forecasting. It is also consistent with the events simply being easy. Distinguishing the two requires the external, date-based test described below; the present data cannot separate them, and we do not claim it does.

\subsubsection{The Simulation vs. Memorization Conflict}

This contamination explains the apparent aggregate "simulation degradation" observed in Section~\ref{sec:cross_model_stats}. When $N=300$ agents engage in social deliberation for $R=60$ rounds on an event where the LLM already possesses $99\%+$ memorized certainty (Condition A $BS_A \approx 0.00$):
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Social interaction forces agents to generate diverse roleplay commentary, explore alternative scenarios, and express nuanced probabilities.
    \item This deliberation process inevitably shifts the swarm's collective probability distribution away from the single deterministic point mass ($1.0$ or $0.0$) toward a moderate distribution (e.g., $0.70$ / $0.30$).
    \item When evaluated against the binary ground truth, this spread of probability mass increases the Brier score from $0.00$ to $0.40$--$0.60$, resulting in a large negative simulation lift ($\Delta \approx -0.40$ to $-0.93$; see Event T1, C15, S6).
\end{enumerate}

Thus, on contaminated events, \textbf{the social simulation is competing against static memorized recall}. Any social deliberation that introduces epistemic uncertainty relative to perfect memorization is mathematically penalized by the Brier metric.

\subsubsection{Baseline Score as a Possible Contamination Proxy}

Earlier drafts advanced this as a reinterpretation of the correlation between $BS_A$ and lift. That correlation is an artifact (Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology}) and cannot support any interpretation, so the argument below rests instead on the unanimous cross-model agreement on which events are near-perfectly answered at baseline, which is a fact about $BS_A$ alone and does not involve the lift. On that basis we propose---as a hypothesis rather than a demonstrated result---that \textbf{$BS_A$ may serve as a proxy for the degree of training data contamination}.
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Low $BS_A$ ($< 0.15$):} High contamination. The model recalls the answer. Social simulation disrupts static recall, producing negative lift.
    \item \textbf{High $BS_A$ ($\ge 0.35$):} Genuine prediction task (uncontaminated). The model faces true uncertainty. Social deliberation synthesizes disparate signals, producing strong positive lift.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Contamination-Corrected Lift Reversal}

To evaluate the true efficacy of multi-agent social simulation for genuine prediction, we isolate the subset of \textbf{13 Likely Clean Events} ($BS_A \ge 0.35$, where models exhibit genuine baseline uncertainty with mean $BS_A = 0.7408$).

As illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:contamination_analysis} (Panel C) and Figure~\ref{fig:lift_stratified}, when contaminated events are excluded:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ:} $BS_A = 0.7408 \to BS_B = 0.5397$. Mean Lift $= +0.2010$, 95\% bootstrap CI $[-0.020, +0.460]$ (from $-0.0993$ on the full dataset).
    \item \textbf{Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1:} $BS_A = 0.7408 \to BS_B = 0.5397$. Mean Lift $= +0.2010$, 95\% CI $[-0.025, +0.457]$ (from $-0.1466$).
    \item \textbf{Qwen2.5-7B-GGUF:} $BS_A = 0.7408 \to BS_B = 0.5569$. Mean Lift $= +0.1838$, 95\% CI $[-0.063, +0.447]$ (from $-0.0968$).
\end{itemize}
(The Llama-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B clean-subset means are identical to four decimal places. We verified this is a numerical coincidence: their per-event Condition B scores differ on 12 of the 13 clean events and happen to sum to the same total, $7.0167$. It is not a duplicated row.)

Three qualifications must be attached to this reversal, and together they prevent us from claiming that Claim 1 is confirmed.

\textbf{First, the subset is selected on the dependent variable.} The ``clean'' set is defined by $BS_A \geq 0.35$, and the outcome being averaged is $BS_A - BS_B$. Since $BS_B$ is close to constant across strata (Figure~\ref{fig:lift_stratified}), conditioning on a high $BS_A$ mechanically selects events with a high $BS_A - BS_B$. This is the same arithmetic coupling identified in Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology}, now operating through a selection rule rather than a regression. A positive mean lift on this subset is close to guaranteed and is therefore weak evidence for the hypothesis.

\textbf{Second, none of the three intervals excludes zero.} At $n=13$ events the bootstrap CIs all span zero comfortably, so even taken at face value the reversal is not statistically distinguishable from no effect.

\textbf{Third, the contamination criterion is itself derived from the model's own output} rather than from an external fact about the event. Classifying an event as contaminated because the model scored well on it makes contamination unfalsifiable: any event the model gets right is reclassified out of the sample. A defensible contamination test must use an event-level covariate that is independent of performance---resolution date relative to the model's training cutoff being the obvious candidate, since it is documented for all three model families and requires no access to model outputs. Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff} carries out that test and supersedes the $BS_A$ threshold used in this subsubsection; the reversal survives the exogenous criterion, but its interpretation changes.

We therefore state the finding as follows: \emph{on the subset of events where the shared No-Sim reference is poorly calibrated, simulation reduces Brier loss by $\approx 0.19$--$0.20$ on average across all three models; this is directionally consistent with the Wisdom of Crowds hypothesis, is not statistically significant at $n=13$, and is substantially inflated by selection on the outcome.} The mechanism of whatever lift is real remains model-dependent, as set out in Appendix~\ref{sec:interaction_realism}.

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figC_lift_stratified.png}
\caption{Simulation lift by event stratum, with $95\%$ percentile bootstrap confidence intervals ($B=20{,}000$). The sign of the aggregate lift flips from negative to positive as the event set is progressively restricted by the contamination threshold---but the threshold is applied to $BS_A$, which is the minuend of the lift being plotted, so the flip is partly a selection artifact (Appendix~\ref{sec:tautology}). Every interval, in every stratum, includes zero.}
\label{fig:lift_stratified}
\end{figure*}

\subsubsection{Knowledge-Cutoff Stratification: the Negative Lift Lives Entirely in Pre-Release Events}
\label{sec:cutoff}

The two preceding subsubsections classify events as contaminated by thresholding the shared baseline at $BS_A < 0.15$, and state plainly that stratifying on a measured score makes the resulting lift reversal partly self-fulfilling. We now replace that proxy with a criterion exogenous to every measured quantity: an event's resolution date against each model's public release date. A model released before an event resolved cannot have memorised its outcome, whatever its documented data cutoff; release date is therefore a hard upper bound on possible leakage and, unlike $BS_A$, it is fixed before any forecast is made. We use resolution date rather than the dossier's information cutoff because what a model may have memorised is the \emph{outcome}, which becomes public at resolution. Resolution dates come from \texttt{data/events\_raw.json}; the bounds are 2023-09-27 (Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1), 2024-07-23 (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, whose documented pretraining cutoff is earlier still, December 2023) and 2024-09-19 (Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen2.5-14B).

Ten of the thirty events resolved before at least one model's release (C2, C3, C4, C7, C10, C12, T1, T3, T4, T5); the remaining twenty postdate every model's release and form a \emph{globally clean} subset (C1, C5, C6, C8, C9, C11, C13, C14, C15, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6, S7, S9, T2, T6, T8, T9). Table~\ref{tab:cutoff_lift} and Figure~\ref{fig:cutoff} give the stratified result.

\label{fig:cutoff}

Four conclusions follow, and the first is the load-bearing one.

\textbf{(1) The contamination reading survives for Qwen2.5-7B but not for Llama-3.1-8B, whose negative lift now sits in the post-release stratum.} This subsubsection was originally built on Llama's contrast between $-0.323$ over its nine pre-release events and $-0.003$ over the twenty-one post-release ones. An intermediate, defective repair on 2026-08-16 appeared to inverse that contrast; that inversion was an artifact and has been withdrawn---the defect and its mechanism are documented in \S\ref{sec:outage}, and the corrected data reported here comes from re-simulating and re-scoring those events properly. On the corrected data both strata carry the same sign: post-release lift is $-0.242$ $[-0.436, -0.062]$, excluding zero, and pre-release lift is $-0.085$ $[-0.400, +0.271]$, spanning it. The stratified contrast that motivated the contamination reading is therefore absent for Llama: its negative lift is not localised to events it could have memorised, and is if anything larger on the events it could not. For Qwen2.5-7B the original contrast is unchanged and still holds: $-0.253$ (excludes zero, pre-release) against $-0.019$ (post-release), as does Mistral's single pre-release event. We read this as a difference between models on valid data rather than as an anomaly: the memorisation account explains Qwen's aggregate lift and does not explain Llama's, and with $E=30$ and the evaluator spread quantified in Table~\ref{tab:cutoff_lift} the design cannot adjudicate why. What the corrected data does remove is any need to invoke an unexplained mechanism: the earlier ``inversion'' was a scoring defect, not a property of the campaign.

\textbf{(2) On events every model had to genuinely forecast, the three campaigns are no longer indistinguishable---Llama is the outlier.} Over the globally clean twenty events, $BS_B$ is $0.548$ (Llama, corrected repair, instrument-consistent pairing), $0.473$ (Mistral) and $0.497$ (Qwen2.5-7B), and lift is $-0.211$ $[-0.405, -0.034]$ (Llama, excluding zero), $+0.005$ $[-0.190, +0.223]$ and $-0.019$ $[-0.208, +0.195]$. The cross-model $BS_B$ spread is $0.075$ here, and Llama is the worst-performing campaign on the clean subset rather than statistically tied with the other two. Two confounds sit on top of this comparison and neither can be removed: Llama's twenty-four re-simulated events ran on a different serving stack from the other campaigns (\S\ref{sec:outage}), and they are scored by a different evaluator than the Mistral and Qwen rows because the July instrument cannot be applied to simulations that did not exist in July. This complicates the decision of Section~\ref{sec:serving_note} to downgrade all three-way model comparisons to descriptive: it is still the right call given the provenance confound, but it can no longer be justified by claiming there is nothing left to explain once memorised events are removed---for Llama there now is, and the design cannot say whether the cause is the model, the serving stack, or the judge.

\textbf{(3) Mistral's susceptibility margin survives; Llama's sign is an evaluator effect, not a data effect.} Over the globally clean subset, $BS_C - BS_B$ is $+0.172$ $[+0.068, +0.291]$ for Mistral-7B (unchanged) and $+0.084$ $[-0.055, +0.226]$ for Qwen2.5-7B. For Llama-3.1-8B the same twenty events give $+0.085$ $[-0.096, +0.283]$ under \texttt{deepseek-v4-flash-0731}, $+0.024$ $[-0.147, +0.197]$ under \texttt{gpt-5.6-luna-pro}, and $-0.153$ $[-0.245, -0.069]$ under \texttt{deepseek-r1}---three readings of one set of transcripts, two centred on the hypothesis' side and one excluding zero against it. An earlier version of this paper reported the negative value alone as a clean-data reversal that the paper could not explain. That reading is withdrawn on two grounds. First, the $-0.198$ figure it rested on came from the defective repair of \S\ref{sec:outage}, in which every unit was scored against an empty evidence field, so Conditions B and C differed only by a letter in the prompt; on the corrected simulations that value does not reproduce. Second, what remains is a disagreement among evaluators applied to identical data, and the three judges differ widely in absolute severity as well (mean Brier $0.32$, $0.48$ and $0.63$), with pairwise directional agreement of only $48$--$79\%$. Susceptibility is a contrast between two simulated conditions and never touches Condition A, so it is insulated from the Condition-A defect---but it is fully exposed to instrument variance, which at $E=30$ is large enough to determine the sign. We therefore report the spread rather than a point estimate, and treat Llama's susceptibility as undetermined by this design.

\textbf{(4) The $BS_A < 0.15$ proxy and the date criterion agree on only $18$--$21$ of $30$ events, and the disagreements are diagnosable.} The proxy flags eleven events (C2, C4, C10, C12, C14, C15, S2, S6, T1, T4, T9). Four of its flags cannot be memorisation: S2 and S6 resolved in November and September 2024, after every model's release---though S6, resolving 2024-09-29, clears the Qwen2.5 bound by only ten days and is the one event whose classification a release-date criterion holds weakly---and C12 and T9 are simply easy given their sanitized dossiers. C12's context is frozen at ``Eve of Finals Game 5'' with Boston leading the series $3$--$1$, and T9 asks whether OpenAI announced AGI in 2025, where the base-rate answer \texttt{NO} requires no knowledge of the outcome. Conversely the proxy misses C3, C7, T3 and T5, which resolved before every relevant release. The two criteria fail in opposite directions: a low $BS_A$ conflates memorisation with an easy dossier, while the date criterion establishes only the \emph{possibility} of memorisation. We report the date criterion as primary because only it is safe to stratify on, and we recommend that any forecasting benchmark built on resolved markets publish the resolution date of every event next to each model's cutoff, so that this stratification is available to readers by construction.

\textbf{The five events the outage never touched are all degenerate.} Intersecting this stratification with the run-integrity audit of Appendix~\ref{sec:outage} isolates the units that were simultaneously post-release and served by a healthy backend in the \emph{original} July campaign: five events (S2, S3, S4, S5, S6), ten simulated units. Every one of those ten returns an exactly uniform probability vector---$\{0.5, 0.5\}$ for the four binary events and $\{0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25\}$ for S6---under Condition B and under Condition C alike, giving $BS_B = BS_C = 0.550$ against $BS_A = 0.344$. Within those five, degenerate consensus is not a tendency, it is the entire outcome. Since the corrected repair the reference campaign is no longer confined to them---the other twenty-four events now carry valid re-simulated transcripts---but they remain the only Llama events whose data was produced end-to-end on the original local serving stack, and so the only ones free of the substitution confound.

\textbf{A separate design defect affects Condition B on seven events, in every campaign.} The step-30 injection bank is keyed by event identifier and was authored against the stale catalogue described in \S\ref{sec:outage}. For the seven identifiers where the two catalogues disagree (C6, T1, T3, T4, T5, T6, T8), Condition B's ``relevant update'' is therefore topically unrelated to the question the simulation actually ran: the 2025 Papal Conclave event receives a core-PCE inflation bulletin, the Taiwanese presidential election receives a Bitcoin treasury announcement, the Irish general election receives an OpenAI product note. This is not a consequence of the outage or of either repair---it is present in the original Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B campaigns as well, both of which simulated the correct questions under the incorrect injections. On those seven events Condition B is thus effectively a second null control, and the data behave accordingly: $BS_C - BS_B$ is $-0.023$ $[-0.181, +0.126]$ on the seven versus $+0.157$ $[+0.034, +0.287]$ on the other twenty-three for Mistral-7B, and $-0.187$ versus $+0.126$ for Qwen2.5-7B. The susceptibility effect this paper reports exists only where the injection is on-topic. We leave the injections unchanged rather than re-authoring them for the re-simulated Llama events, which would have given one campaign better stimuli than the others, and record the seven events as a known limitation of the instrument.

\subsubsection{Normalized Brier Skill Score (BSS) Evaluation}

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig10_bss_comparison.png}
\caption{Normalized Brier Skill Score (BSS) Comparison across the 19 Filtered Events ($BS_A \ge 0.15$). $BSS = 1.0$ indicates perfect forecasting, $BSS = 0.0$ indicates uniform random guessing. Across all three model architectures, Condition A direct elicitation yields marginal skill over random chance ($BSS_A = +0.0924$), whereas Condition B social simulation elevates forecasting skill to $BSS_B \approx +0.2264 \to +0.2476$ (a net $+0.134 \to +0.155$ skill gain).}
\label{fig:bss_comparison}
\end{figure*}

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig11_bss_by_format.png}
\caption{BSS Skill Lift by Question Format: Boolean ($K=2$, $n=7$) vs.\ MCQ ($K > 2$, $n=12$). Boolean questions exhibit dramatic simulation skill gains ($\Delta BSS = +0.158 \to +0.261$). MCQ questions maintain positive forecasting skill over random guessing ($BSS_B \approx +0.17 \to +0.25$), though probability dispersion across multiple options reduces net lift compared to binary choices.}
\label{fig:bss_by_format}
\end{figure*}

To evaluate forecasting capability without option-count distortion, we convert raw Brier scores into the normalized \textbf{Brier Skill Score (BSS)}. On the 19 filtered events:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ:} Baseline skill $BSS_A = +0.0924 \to$ Condition B $BSS_B = \mathbf{+0.2264}$ (Skill Lift $= \mathbf{+0.1340}$).
    \item \textbf{Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1:} Baseline skill $BSS_A = +0.0924 \to$ Condition B $BSS_B = \mathbf{+0.2476}$ (Skill Lift $= \mathbf{+0.1552}$).
    \item \textbf{Qwen2.5-7B-GGUF:} Baseline skill $BSS_A = +0.0924 \to$ Condition B $BSS_B = \mathbf{+0.2309}$ (Skill Lift $= \mathbf{+0.1385}$).
\end{itemize}

Figure~\ref{fig:bss_comparison} illustrates this BSS elevation. Direct single-pass elicitation (Condition A) achieves less than $10\%$ skill above uniform random guessing ($BSS_A = +0.0924$). In contrast, multi-agent social interaction (Condition B) more than doubles forecasting skill, elevating $BSS_B$ to $+0.2264 \to +0.2476$.

\subsubsection{Question Format Analysis: Boolean vs.\ MCQ}

Decomposing the 19 filtered events into \textbf{Boolean} ($K=2$, $n=7$) and \textbf{Multiple Choice (MCQ)} ($K > 2$, $n=12$) reveals significant structural differences in swarm performance (Figure~\ref{fig:bss_by_format}):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Boolean Questions ($K=2$):} Social simulation produces dramatic skill gains across all models. BSS increases from $BSS_A = -0.1329$ (sub-random baseline) to $BSS_B = +0.1829 \to +0.3886$. Mistral-7B achieves the highest Boolean skill lift ($\Delta BSS = +0.5214$). Binary consensus formation enables swarms to efficiently concentrate probability mass onto the realized outcome.
    \item \textbf{MCQ Questions ($K > 2$):} For multi-option questions (up to $K=9$), Condition B maintains solid skill above random guessing ($BSS_B = +0.1654 \to +0.2517$). However, agent deliberation causes \emph{probability dispersion} across secondary choices, moderating net skill lift relative to Condition A ($\Delta BSS = -0.058 \to +0.028$). Crucially, Condition B consistently outperforms Null Control Condition C across all MCQ events (Susceptibility Margin $= +0.061 \to +0.193$), confirming that the swarm actively integrates real-world signal over noise even under multi-option dispersion.
\end{itemize}

This finding establishes an essential methodological mandate for future agentic benchmark design: benchmarks evaluating predictive multi-agent systems must implement strict temporal filtering or post-cutoff event generation to prevent memorized recall from corrupting crowd intelligence evaluation.

\subsection{Reproducibility and Data Audit}
\label{sec:data_audit}

All aggregate statistics reported in this paper (per-model mean $BS_A$/$BS_B$/$BS_C$, directional accuracy, Diebold-Mariano statistics, the 11/6/13 contaminated/borderline/clean event split, and the Brier Skill Score values) are reproduced from the programmatic \texttt{summary.json} of the three completed campaigns, in which all 270 (model, event, condition) units are recorded as having completed with no errors and no evaluator fallbacks. Appendix~\ref{sec:outage} shows that those recorded flags are not evidence of a successful run: 69 of the 270 executed against an unreachable model endpoint and are flagged clean regardless. For this revision we additionally recomputed every reported quantity independently from the raw \texttt{event\_results.json} records; the script that does so and that generates Figures~\ref{fig:susceptibility}, \ref{fig:tautology}, \ref{fig:jsd_integrity}, \ref{fig:degenerate} and \ref{fig:lift_stratified} is released with the code, as is the run-integrity script behind Figures~\ref{fig:outage}, \ref{fig:evidence_depth} and \ref{fig:cutoff}, so that each figure can be traced to the per-unit records.

That recomputation surfaced three discrepancies with earlier drafts, which we record here in full.

\textbf{(1) Per-event table errors.} A full cell-by-cell comparison---not the spot-check described in earlier drafts---found \textbf{18 incorrect cells}, all in the Mistral-7B table and all confined to its nine alphabetically-first C-prefixed events (C1, C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15, C2, C3). Errors reach $0.93$ Brier (C2: reported $0.2000$, actual $1.1338$), not the ``$\approx 0.18$ maximum'' previously stated. The Llama-3.1-8B and Qwen2.5-7B tables match the raw records exactly. Because the aggregate means were always computed programmatically, no mean, test statistic or conclusion in the paper depended on the erroneous cells; Table~\ref{tab:per_event_mistral_7b} has been regenerated from source.

\textbf{(2) Metric conflation in the susceptibility column.} The $\Delta_{B-C}$ value previously reported for Qwen2.5-7B ($-0.044$) was read from \texttt{content\_susceptibility.delta.B\_minus\_C} (a mean yes-probability difference) while the other two rows were read from the Brier margin, producing a spurious sign reversal. Corrected in Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_stats} and Section~\ref{sec:cross_model_stats}.

\textbf{(3) Condition A provenance.} Condition A is shared across models, and earlier drafts justified this as an intended consequence of deterministic decoding (temperature~0, seed~42). That justification is incorrect: determinism makes a \emph{single} model reproducible, but cannot make three different model families emit byte-identical probability vectors on 30 events, which is what the records show. Condition A was generated once and reused. Section~\ref{sec:condition_a} treats this as a design defect and traces its consequences. The replication of Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication} turns this defect to diagnostic use: because the Condition A input is byte-identical across campaigns, re-evaluating each campaign separately constitutes a triplicate re-read of the same text, which is how the instrument's reproducibility ($5/30$) was measured.

\subsubsection{Two Evaluator Passes, Released Separately}
\label{sec:data_audit_two_passes}

The replication of Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication} adds a second scored pass over the same simulation artifacts, and the two must not be mixed. Each campaign directory therefore carries both, each internally consistent in $90/90$ units:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{event\_results.json} --- Dataset J, the July pass (DeepSeek-R1-14B). Reproduces that campaign's \texttt{summary.json} exactly, and is the basis of every number in Sections~\ref{sec:full_campaign}--\ref{sec:degenerate}.
    \item \texttt{event\_results\_evaluatorB\_openrouter.json} --- Dataset A, the August pass (\texttt{deepseek-v4-flash-0731}), with all probability-derived metrics recomputed from that pass's own vectors.
\end{itemize}

We record the hazard that produced this arrangement, since it is a general one. The re-evaluation utility overwrites \texttt{probabilities}, \texttt{mcq\_dimensions}, \texttt{validated\_scales} and \texttt{micro\_epistemic\_mapping}, but does \emph{not} recompute \texttt{brier}, \texttt{rps}, \texttt{directional\_accuracy} or the \texttt{calibration\_*} fields, which are written once by the protocol runner. Running it in place therefore produced files in which the stored Brier described a probability vector the record no longer contained---in $82$, $86$ and $87$ of $90$ units for Llama, Mistral and Qwen respectively---while remaining superficially well-formed and passing the pipeline's own completeness checks. Any analysis reading \texttt{brier} from those files would have silently mixed two instruments. The reconciliation utility (\texttt{separate\_evaluator\_datasets.py}) rebuilds both datasets from the preserved snapshots, reports the consistency count for each, and retains the inconsistent intermediate file for audit rather than deleting it. We recommend that any benchmark storing derived metrics alongside the predictions they derive from either recompute them atomically with the prediction or validate their consistency on load; a stored metric that cannot be regenerated from its own record is not auditable.

A second, repaired hazard is recorded here for completeness, because the shape of it generalises. Dataset A was first generated against a stale copy of the event catalogue shipped under \texttt{ECN-HPC-DEPLOY/data/}, in which the identifiers C6, T1, T3, T4, T5, T6 and T8 carry different events; the \texttt{micro\_epistemic\_mapping} field of the corresponding $21$ units per campaign therefore answered micro-questions belonging to the wrong event, while the probability vectors---built from each record's own question, evidence and option list---remained correct. Nothing about the affected files was malformed: two catalogues with overlapping identifiers and disjoint contents produce records that validate cleanly and are wrong only by cross-reference. It was caught by comparing each unit's stored micro-question ids against the catalogue, which is now a released check (\texttt{find\_contaminated\_micro\_events.py}); the $63$ affected units were re-evaluated against the authoritative \texttt{data/events\_raw.json}, the checker reports zero remaining mismatches, and the catalogue path is corrected in the released scripts. The generalisable lesson is that an event catalogue keyed by short identifiers needs a content hash or version recorded in each run manifest; identifier collision across catalogue versions is otherwise undetectable from the outputs alone.

\subsubsection{Persona Pool Audit: the 4-Axis DNA Is Not Realised as Specified}
\label{sec:persona_audit}

Appendices~\ref{sec:optimal_bounds} and~\ref{sec:cross_model} justify $N=300$ partly on cognitive-diversity grounds, stating that at that population each of the four persona-DNA axes (Worldview, Motivation, Style, Biases) is represented by $\geq 75$ distinct agents. That claim was never measured. We measure it here by counting the persona records the pipeline actually wrote: all $270$ \texttt{reddit\_profiles.json} files, $300$ agents each, $81{,}000$ agent records in total. Table~\ref{tab:persona_audit} reports the result, and it does not support the claim for two of the three campaigns.

\begin{table*}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Persona pool as written to disk, per campaign. ``Distinct personas'' counts unique persona strings among the 300 agents of one unit; the boilerplate bio is the event-independent string \texttt{"[profession] based in [country], engaging on emerging developments."}; template compliance is the fraction of agents whose persona string emits all four labelled axes. Persona sets are fixed per event: Conditions A, B and C of the same event share one pool, so the per-condition figures are identical.}
\label{tab:persona_audit}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Campaign} & \textbf{Distinct personas / 300} & \textbf{min / median / max} & \textbf{Boilerplate bio} & \textbf{4-axis compliance} & \textbf{Units $\leq$ 20 distinct} \\
\midrule
Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1 & $295.6$ & $263 / 298 / 300$ & $0.7\%$  & $97.7\%$ & $0/90$ \\
Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ         & $71.6$  & $11 / 11 / 271$    & $79.0\%$ & $16.2\%$ & $69/90$ \\
Qwen2.5-7B-GGUF          & $38.0$  & $19 / 40 / 57$     & $92.0\%$ & $1.7\%$  & $9/90$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}%
}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{6pt}
\end{table*}

Three observations follow.

\textbf{(1) Only Mistral realises the specified design.} Its pools carry a median of $298$ distinct personas out of $300$ and emit the four labelled axes in $97.7\%$ of agent records. Whatever else is confounded about that campaign (Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}), its Stage~1 persona extraction worked.

\textbf{(2) Qwen2.5-7B generates roughly 38 event-specific stakeholders and fills the remaining $\sim$260 agent slots with a boilerplate template.} Its distinct-persona count never exceeds $57$. It also does not follow the four-axis template: it writes \texttt{- Biased against [X]} in place of \texttt{- Biases: [X]}, so a strict parse of the Biases axis succeeds on $1.7\%$ of its agents. The persona ensemble that Appendix~\ref{sec:interaction_realism} identifies as this model's operative mechanism is therefore an ensemble of about $38$ archetypes, not $300$.

\textbf{(3) In 23 of 30 events, the Llama-3.1-8B pool is a static fallback library of 11 archetypes, identical across events.} Those 69 units decompose as $5 \times 45$ agents on five generic worldview archetypes (\emph{grassroots activist}, \emph{techno-optimist}, \emph{skeptical contrarian}, \emph{traditionalist moralist}, \emph{globalist bureaucrat}), $30$ agents on \texttt{"Worldview: Pragmatic and data-driven"}, and $5 \times 9$ agents on \texttt{"Pragmatic institutionalist; filters truth through [profession] experience"}. Not one of the 11 refers to the event under forecast. The remaining $21$ units carry $270$--$271$ distinct, event-specific personas. The split is not random and not event-driven; Appendix~\ref{sec:outage} identifies its cause.

The generalisable point is that persona diversity is an \emph{output} of the model under test---Stage~1 extraction is an LLM call---and therefore has to be measured per campaign rather than assumed from $N$. A benchmark that scales agent population as its primary diversity knob should report realised distinct-persona counts alongside $N$, because the two came apart here by a factor of $27$.

\subsubsection{A Mid-Campaign Backend Outage Voids 23 of the 30 Llama-3.1-8B Events}
\label{sec:outage}

\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figF_llama_outage.png}
\caption{The Llama-3.1-8B campaign's Condition B units in wall-clock execution order. The serving backend fails during the seventh event (S7) and never recovers: realised persona diversity drops from $271$ to the 11-archetype fallback, social actions in the authoritative \texttt{.db} trace drop from $448$--$539$ per unit to exactly $4$, every one of the $1{,}000$ telemetry probes fails with \texttt{Connection error}, and the \texttt{interview} rows through which the forecast is extracted fall to zero. All 23 post-break events record byte-identical failure counts, which is the signature of a dead endpoint rather than of noise. Every one of these units is nevertheless recorded as \texttt{simulation\_status: "completed"} with \texttt{error: null}.}
\label{fig:outage}
\end{figure*}

Ordering the Llama-3.1-8B units by wall-clock start time reveals a single, sharp regression, shown in Figure~\ref{fig:outage} and summarised in Table~\ref{tab:outage}. The first six events (S2, S3, S4, S5, C3, S6, in execution order) ran normally. During the seventh (S7) the vLLM endpoint serving the swarm became unreachable. For the remaining 23 events every unit records exactly $1{,}000$ \texttt{Telemetry probe failed \ldots Connection error} warnings, exactly $340$ \texttt{Model error} lines, exactly $4$ social actions across both platform databases, zero \texttt{interview} rows, and a $2.93$\,MB log---the same numbers, unit after unit.

\centering

Social actions and \texttt{interview} rows are counted from the authoritative SQLite \texttt{trace} tables; failure counts from \texttt{simulation.log}. The \texttt{interview} action is the telemetry probe that extracts each agent's belief state, i.e.\ the channel the Condition B and C forecasts are read from.}
\label{tab:outage}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{llccccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Phase} & \textbf{Events} & \textbf{Distinct personas} & \textbf{Social actions/unit} & \textbf{Probe failures/unit} & \textbf{\texttt{interview} rows} & \textbf{Log size} \\
\midrule
Healthy & S2, S3, S4, S5, C3, S6 (6) & $270$--$271$ & $448$--$539$ & $0$       & $12{,}000$ (all) & $0.25$\,MB \\
Failing & S7 (1)                     & $271$        & $14$         & $1{,}000$ & $0$              & $2.88$\,MB \\
Dead    & S9 $\to$ C15 (23)          & $11$         & $4$          & $1{,}000$ & $0$              & $2.93$\,MB \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}%
}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{6pt}
\end{table*}

Three consequences bear directly on results reported earlier in this paper.

\textbf{(1) It is the cause of the JSD placeholder already reported.} Section~\ref{sec:jsd_convergence} records that $87.7\%$ of Llama-3.1-8B's round-level Jensen--Shannon-divergence readings are the constant $0.758277$ and withdraws the convergence result on that basis. The outage explains why: with no successful telemetry probe there is no belief distribution to diverge, so the field retains its initialised value. The symptom and its cause are the same event.

\textbf{(2) The interaction-density ranking of Appendix~\ref{sec:interaction_realism} is stated for the un-repaired campaign and has not been recomputed.} The figures quoted there ($27{,}087$ actions for Llama at $40.31$/DB, $9{,}364$ for Mistral, $723$ for Qwen-7B) aggregate $672$, $264$ and $264$ database files across all campaigns including the $N$- and $R$-sweeps, none of which were touched by the 2026-08-16 repair; that comparison is left as reported and should be treated as describing the pre-repair state until the sweep databases are re-scanned. Table~\ref{tab:action_30event_only}, restricted to the $120$ databases of the 30-event campaign that produces every Brier score in this paper, \emph{has} been rescanned against the post-repair \texttt{.db} files, and the ranking it showed pre-repair no longer holds: Llama now leads Mistral in every social-action category (e.g.\ $9{,}074$ vs.\ $3{,}545$ \texttt{create\_post}), and \texttt{interview} probes are tied across all three models at $60{,}000$ (previously $12{,}000$ for Llama alone, since only its $12$ healthy units contributed a full telemetry read). This inverts the previous within-campaign picture, in which Mistral posted more than twice as often as Llama and $96.7\%$ of Llama's recorded actions came from just the six pre-outage events---that asymmetry was a direct readout of the outage itself, not a property of the model, and dissolves once the affected events carry real telemetry.

\label{tab:action_30event_only}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}

\textbf{Campaign} & \texttt{create\_post} & \texttt{create\_comment} & \texttt{follow} & \texttt{like\_post} & \texttt{like\_comment} & \texttt{interview} \\
\midrule
Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ         & $\mathbf{9{,}074}$ & $\mathbf{6{,}454}$ & $\mathbf{5{,}046}$ & $\mathbf{4{,}046}$ & $\mathbf{2{,}044}$ & $60{,}000$ \\
Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1 & $3{,}545$ & $1{,}648$ & $576$ & $1{,}618$ & $139$ & $60{,}000$ \\

\textbf{(3) The failure is invisible in the result records.} Every one of the 69 affected units carries \texttt{simulation\_status: "completed"}, \texttt{full\_simulation\_completed: true}, \texttt{simulation\_executed: true}, \texttt{error: null}, \texttt{strict\_contract: true}, \texttt{evaluator\_fallback\_used: false} and \texttt{evaluator\_reliability\_status: "complete"}. The statement made in earlier drafts---that all $270$ units completed ``with no errors and no evaluator fallbacks''---is true of those fields and false of the runs, and is corrected wherever it appears in this revision. The generalisable lesson is that a pipeline whose completion flags are set by the orchestrator rather than derived from the artifacts will report a dead backend as a successful experiment; the auditable form of the same check is to require a minimum count of successful telemetry probes per unit before a forecast is scored, which would have failed loudly on all 69.

\subsubsection{What the Evaluator Actually Read}
\label{sec:evidence_depth}

Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication} treats the evaluator as an instrument and measures its reproducibility. This subsection measures its \emph{input}. Each scored record carries the verbatim text the evaluator was given, in \texttt{evidence\_text}: a tail of the simulation log followed by a block headed \texttt{Representative Social Media Actions (Sample)}. Parsing that block gives, per unit, how many action lines the evaluator saw, from how many distinct agents, and how many distinct utterances they contained. Table~\ref{tab:evidence_depth} reports the distribution and Figure~\ref{fig:evidence_depth} plots every unit.

\begin{table*}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Deliberation content supplied to the evaluator, over the $60$ simulated (Condition B and C) units of each campaign. ``Distinct utterances'' counts unique message texts among the action lines shown.}
\label{tab:evidence_depth}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
