\begin{figure*}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figG_evidence_depth.png}
\caption{Left: the deliberation content each simulated unit's evaluator was given. Three regimes are visible---Mistral in the diverse corner ($33$--$53$ speakers, $33$--$50$ distinct texts), Qwen2.5-7B with many speakers but few distinct texts, and Llama split between its healthy units and $47$ units carrying at most one speaker. Right: the weighted rubric score the evaluator returned from that input. Within the Llama campaign, units whose evidence is a single-speaker error dump score $0.495$ against $0.508$ for units carrying a multi-speaker transcript---a difference of $0.013$ on a unit interval.}
\label{fig:evidence_depth}
\end{figure*}

\textbf{The outage units are scored from an error log.} For a representative affected unit (\texttt{C10\_B\_r1}, ``Who will be the next UK Prime Minister after the 2024 election?''), \texttt{evidence\_text} consists of a log tail of \texttt{Telemetry probe failed \ldots Connection error} lines reporting \texttt{Total actions: 3}, followed by six action lines from a single agent: four are the forecast question repeated verbatim, and two are the round-30 injection text. From that input the evaluator returned $P(\text{Starmer}) = 0.45$ and a complete seven-dimension rubric scoring \texttt{herd\_effect} and \texttt{convergence} as \texttt{very\_high}, \texttt{deliberation\_quality} as \texttt{high}, and a weighted rubric score of $0.6125$. There was no deliberation of any kind in its input.

\textbf{Qwen2.5-7B's evidence is telemetry scaffolding, not discussion.} Its units show many speakers but few distinct texts because the sampled lines are probe artifacts: in \texttt{C10\_B\_r1}, $46$ of the $60$ lines shown are the literal string \texttt{"Belief check: YES Probability = None"}---a probe that returned no value---and only one line is a substantive agent utterance, which is written in Italian in violation of the \texttt{Global Language Rule} carried in that agent's own persona. This is direct evidence for the behavioural-homogenization limitation that Appendix~\ref{sec:interaction_realism} states qualitatively, and it locates the effect in the extraction channel rather than in agent behaviour.

\textbf{The sample is capped at sixty lines, and the probe backfills it.} The block is produced by \texttt{build\_evidence\_text}, which calls \texttt{\_sample\_representative\_actions(\ldots, max\_actions=60)}. That function assigns an engagement score to each logged action---$1.0$ for a post, $0.8$ for a comment, $0.1$ for a like---sorts descending, keeps the top sixty and re-sorts them by round. Two consequences follow, and neither is a property of the swarm. First, the cap binds everywhere: the median action-line count is exactly $60$ in all four campaigns, whether the unit's database holds two hundred content items or five, so the quantity of deliberation the evaluator sees is a constant of the extractor and cannot covary with anything. Across the released data those sixty lines span a median of $16$--$17$ distinct rounds out of $61$, concentrated in the opening third; the closing rounds are largely absent from what is scored. Second, \texttt{TELEMETRY\_PROBE} records are rendered into the same block as \texttt{Belief check: YES Probability = \ldots} but receive no engagement score, so they sit at zero and enter the sample only when a unit has fewer than sixty scored content actions. On a sparse campaign they therefore fill the remainder.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Share of the evaluator's sixty evidence lines that are telemetry probes rather than agent utterances, over the $60$ simulated units of each campaign in the currently released result files. Unlike Table~\ref{tab:evidence_depth}, which describes the archived pre-repair snapshot, these figures are measured on the data this paper reports.}
\label{tab:probe_share}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Campaign} & \textbf{Probe share} & \textbf{Probe share} & \textbf{Distinct} \\
 & (mean) & (median) & \textbf{utterances} \\
\midrule
Llama-3.1-8B             & $0.0\%$          & $0.0\%$          & $56$ \\
Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1 & $0.0\%$          & $0.0\%$          & $41.5$ \\
Qwen2.5-7B-GGUF          & $\mathbf{86.0\%}$ & $\mathbf{86.7\%}$ & $8$ \\
Qwen2.5-14B-AWQ          & $\mathbf{72.0\%}$ & $\mathbf{90.0\%}$ & $6$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbf{The fourth model is affected as badly as the third, and it was never audited.} Table~\ref{tab:probe_share} extends the measurement to Qwen2.5-14B, which \S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status} previously excluded from this appendix. That exclusion was wrong in scope: it was justified on the grounds that the model's \texttt{.db} traces and persona files never left the cluster, which is true and does bar the audits that read those artifacts, but the present measurement reads \texttt{evidence\_text} out of the result records, and those are on disk. Read that way, Qwen2.5-14B sits in the same regime as Qwen2.5-7B and slightly deeper into it: a median of $90\%$ of its evidence lines are probe readouts and a median of $6$ distinct utterances survive in a sixty-line sample. This matters more than the corresponding statement about Qwen2.5-7B, because Qwen2.5-14B is the campaign that posts the best forecasting numbers in this paper---the highest Condition-B directional accuracy and, with Qwen2.5-7B, one of only two campaigns whose $\Delta$Brier interval excludes zero. The evidence from which those numbers were produced consists mostly of the simulator's own per-agent probability estimates, which is to say that the measurement channel is feeding the channel being scored.

\textbf{What that does and does not license.} It does not license the conclusion that the leak produces the result. The probe share is very nearly a campaign-level constant---$0\%$, $0\%$, $86\%$, $72\%$---so it is confounded with model identity, generation and serving stack together, and the design contains no within-campaign variation in it to exploit. The decisive counter-example is in the same table: Mistral-7B reads a transcript with no probe content at all and attains a Condition-B directional accuracy of $0.850$, identical to Qwen2.5-7B's. Pooling units across campaigns gives $0.892$ against $0.756$ in favour of the leaked units, but that comparison is between campaigns wearing a per-unit disguise and we decline to read it causally. The defensible statement is narrower and still serious: for half of the four designated models, the input to the scored forecast is dominated by an artifact of the instrumentation, and the present design cannot separate that from whatever those campaigns' agents contributed. Isolating it requires holding the campaign fixed and varying the extractor---re-running \texttt{\_sample\_representative\_actions} with telemetry records excluded and re-scoring the same units---which costs no new simulation and which we flag as the immediate next experiment.

\textbf{A speaker name can carry the answer.} A separate and smaller channel runs through the display names. Personas are occasionally instantiated with names drawn from the event's own option set, and the sampled lines carry the speaker name verbatim, so the evaluator can encounter \texttt{[R13][reddit] Keir Starmer: \ldots} while being asked who will be the next UK Prime Minister. Over the $240$ simulated units, $31$ contain a speaker named after some option and $\mathbf{20}$ contain a speaker named after the \emph{ground-truth} option, spread over six events (C10 in all four campaigns, C12, T4, C11, S6, C8). Within the twenty-event contamination-clean set the count is five units. The affected fraction is small and we do not restate any result on account of it, but it is a label leak in the same extraction channel as the previous two paragraphs, it is invisible to every completion flag the pipeline sets, and a persona generator that may not sample option strings is a cheap gate against it.

\textbf{The rubric does not discriminate.} Comparing within the Llama campaign, so that the contrast is not confounded by which model produced the unit, the $47$ units whose evidence contains at most one speaker receive a mean weighted rubric score of $0.495$; the $13$ units whose evidence contains more than one speaker---the twelve healthy units at $38$--$43$ speakers, plus the transitional S7 unit---receive $0.508$. Per dimension the gap is of the same order (\texttt{herd\_effect} $0.535$ vs $0.510$; \texttt{convergence} $0.526$ vs $0.508$; \texttt{deliberation\_quality} $0.465$ vs $0.508$). An instrument that assigns essentially the same deliberation-quality score to a $40$-speaker transcript and to a connection-error dump is not measuring deliberation quality. This is a stronger statement than the low inter-run $\kappa$ of Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_reliability}, which shows the rubric is unreliable; the present measurement shows it is also insensitive to the presence or absence of its object, and it is the reason we regard the decision to exclude all seven dimensions from the composite score as necessary rather than conservative.


\textbf{(4) Status of the fourth model.}
\label{sec:fourth_model_status}
ECN-BENCH designates four primary benchmark models: Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1, Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen2.5-14B. The Qwen2.5-14B campaign's original 24 scorable events were executed as two jobs---11381327 (29 events, 25 July 2026) and 11409272 (event C15 catch-up, 27 July 2026)---with outputs consolidated on the cluster at \texttt{simulation\_logs/\allowbreak qwen2.5-14b-awq/\allowbreak ecnbench\_\allowbreak 20260727\_\allowbreak qwen14b\_\allowbreak complete/}. Earlier drafts stated that its \texttt{event\_results.json} had not been transferred off the cluster and, once retrieved, that $87$ of its $90$ records carried an empty \texttt{ground\_truth} field and were therefore unscorable. Both are now superseded: the field has been backfilled from the authoritative catalogue, and $6$ of the $30$ events (T3, T4, T5, T6, C6, T8) were found, on backfilling, to have been simulated against the wrong topic entirely---a deeper reach of the same stale-catalogue collision documented in Section~\ref{sec:replication_design}, which for the other three campaigns corrupted only a downstream micro-question field rather than the swarm's own deliberation. That left a $24$-event, $72$-unit campaign, compared against the other three models on the $17$ of those events that also passed the contamination check of Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff}, in the version of this section prior to 2026-08-21.

\textbf{2026-08-21: the six events recovered, Qwen2.5-14B now complete at 30/30.} The six events were re-simulated end-to-end against the correct root catalogue---300 agents, 60 rounds, all three conditions, split across two SLURM jobs (T3/T4/T5 and T6/C6/T8; the cluster's \texttt{medium} QOS caps a user at 2 GPUs, so the two jobs ran sequentially rather than in parallel) after a first re-simulation attempt reproduced the identical stale-catalogue bug (its launch script still resolved \texttt{--events-raw} to the wrong catalogue by a relative path). The 18 new units were rescored under all five evaluator passes used elsewhere in this section (primary \texttt{deepseek/deepseek-r1}, three Evaluator~A repeats, Evaluator~C) and merged into all five result files, verified against the required post-merge invariants (no empty evidence, question or ground truth; Condition~A correctly flagged \texttt{simulation\_executed=False}; no duplicate unit ids). Qwen2.5-14B is now a $30$-event, $90$-unit campaign scored the same way as the other three models, and the contamination-clean comparison of Section~\ref{sec:four_model_extension} and Table~\ref{tab:four_model_clean} now covers the full $20$-event globally-clean set of Appendix~\ref{sec:cutoff} rather than the $17$-event subset that excluded T6, C6 and T8. Every ``three-model'' statement elsewhere in this paper predates the original 2026-08-14 recovery and should still be read as ``three of the four designated models''; only Section~\ref{sec:four_model_extension} and Table~\ref{tab:four_model_clean} include the fourth.

\textbf{What remains a gap.} Most of the Qwen2.5-14B simulation artifacts (persona files, \texttt{.db} traces, logs) are unavailable, so this model remains largely absent from the run-integrity audits that read those artifacts directly. The six events re-simulated 2026-08-21 were transferred off the cluster on 2026-08-26 and are released ($18$ units), together with a three-unit fragment of the original campaign (event~C15); the remaining $21$ events' transcripts had by then been removed by the cluster's scratch-filesystem retention policy and are unrecoverable. Those audits are therefore not re-runnable for this model at campaign scale, whatever the release contains. Earlier revisions gave that range as Appendices~\ref{sec:persona_audit}--\ref{sec:evidence_depth} and thereby excluded the model from the evidence-depth audit as well; that was too wide. Section~\ref{sec:evidence_depth} reads \texttt{evidence\_text} out of the result records rather than off the cluster, so it applies to Qwen2.5-14B unchanged, and Table~\ref{tab:probe_share} now reports it---at a median $90\%$ telemetry-probe share, the deepest of the four campaigns. Its interaction counts in the action-density tables elsewhere in this section (2,407 actions, 180 DBs, 13.38/DB) come from a cluster-side SQL scan taken 2026-07-27, over what was at the time a full 30-event campaign on disk---which includes the six events later found to be simulated against the wrong topic (\S\ref{sec:replication_design}), so those counts describe real social-media actions produced under the \emph{wrong-topic} dossiers, not the correct ones re-simulated 2026-08-21. The re-simulated \texttt{.db} traces for those six events have since been transferred and released, but were never folded into a fresh scan---the 180 DBs the 2026-07-27 scan read no longer exist, so the scan cannot be repeated as taken. Tables~\ref{tab:action_density_30event}--\ref{tab:action_breakdown} therefore carry a topic mismatch on 6 of Qwen2.5-14B's 180 DBs that the other three models' rows do not have, and a reader wishing to correct it must rescan the released 18 units against a fresh baseline rather than reproduce the published counts. We note that earlier drafts of this paper, based on the undercounted \texttt{actions.jsonl} summary, incorrectly characterized Qwen-14B as ``near-zero'' interaction; the authoritative \texttt{.db} counts correct this. All interaction counts in Tables~\ref{tab:action_density_30event}--\ref{tab:action_breakdown} are from direct SQL queries on the authoritative \texttt{trace} tables of the \texttt{.db} files. The \texttt{actions.jsonl} summary files, used in earlier drafts, systematically undercount organic actions due to a cursor-tracking bug in the telemetry-probe function and should not be used for interaction analysis.

\textbf{(5) The outage was repaired, and the first repair attempt was itself defective.} The 24 affected events (S7, the transitional unit, plus the 23 events after it) were re-simulated end-to-end on 2026-08-16 against the original endpoint's cloud equivalent (\texttt{meta-llama/llama-3.1-8b-instruct} via OpenRouter, in place of the air-gapped node's local AWQ-INT4 serving) with real Twitter/Reddit social simulation and Neo4j-backed memory. This is a genuine model-serving substitution, not merely a re-score, and should be read as such when comparing Llama-3.1-8B's post-fix numbers against the other three models, which ran entirely on local HPC serving. The pre-fix data this section and Section~\ref{sec:evidence_depth} analyze is preserved unmodified in the campaign directory's \texttt{archive\_pre\_session\_repairs/} folder; the per-unit figures quoted above (e.g.\ \texttt{C10\_B\_r1}) describe that archived snapshot. A single Qwen2.5-14B unit (\texttt{C9\_C\_r1}) carries the identical corruption signature but was left unrepaired, since \texttt{qwen2.5:14b} is not served by either OpenRouter or Ollama Cloud.

The first attempt to merge that re-simulation into the campaign was defective in three independent ways, all traced to one script that rebuilt the result rows from each unit's \texttt{simulation\_config.json}. (i) It read the keys \texttt{question} and \texttt{evidence\_text}, neither of which that file contains (it stores \texttt{event\_question}, and carries no evidence field at all), so both came back empty and \emph{all 72 units were scored against an empty evidence string}---the evaluator saw only the question, the letter A, B or C, and nothing else, making the three conditions differ by a single character. (ii) It resolved event metadata against a stale catalogue copy in which seven identifiers (C6, T1, T3, T4, T5, T6, T8) denote different events; two of those collisions (T1, T5) share option set and ground truth with the intended events and so pass every automated consistency check while simulating an unrelated question. (iii) The merge filtered on \texttt{event\_id} without filtering on condition, overwriting Condition A---which has no simulation to re-read---and stamping \texttt{simulation\_executed=true} on it, and it fed one source file into both the primary and the replication result files, making two nominally independent evaluator passes identical on all 72 units. The negative post-release lift and the reversed susceptibility that an earlier version of this paper reported as unexplained findings were artifacts of (i) and (iii): restoring Condition A alone returns the post-release lift from $-0.161$ (interval excluding zero) to $-0.028$ (spanning it).

The corrected repair, reported throughout this paper, rebuilds \texttt{evidence\_text} with the pipeline's own \texttt{build\_evidence\_text} from each unit's \texttt{actions.jsonl} and \texttt{simulation.log} (mean $19{,}706$ characters, against $19{,}412$ for the units the outage never touched), re-simulates the seven catalogue-collision events against the correct catalogue and seed dossiers, re-scores all 48 simulated units under three independent evaluators plus three repeat passes, and restores Condition A from the shared No-Sim record rather than re-eliciting it---Condition A is a single record shared byte-for-byte across all three campaigns (Section~\ref{sec:condition_a}), it has no transcript to score, and the first repair overwrote it only as a side effect of the missing condition filter. Independence of the evaluator passes is restored: the primary and replication files now agree on $4$ of $90$ units, against $74$ of $90$ under the defective merge. One deviation is required and is not cosmetic: the reasoning evaluators exhaust the pipeline's default $1500$-token completion budget on real $\approx19$k-character evidence and return a null completion, which killed $15$ of the first $34$ units; the budget was raised to $6{,}000$--$16{,}000$ tokens for this re-scoring. The defective merge never encountered this failure precisely because its empty evidence field kept the evaluator's reasoning short---the first defect concealed the second.
\section{System Architecture and Engineering (MiroFish-Offline)}
\label{sec:architecture}

The ECN-BENCH execution stack is optimized for air-gapped HPC deployment. The simulation pipeline operates in six sequential steps, outlined in Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}.

\begin{figure*}[!tbp]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{|c|}
\hline
\textbf{Step 1: GraphRAG Build} \\
$\downarrow$ \\
\textbf{Step 2: Persona Generation (4-Axis DNA, Neo4j Graph Memory)} \\
$\downarrow$ \\
\textbf{Step 3: 60-Step OASIS Simulation (MiroFish-Offline Engine)} \\
$\rightarrow$ \textit{Step 30: Perturbation Injection (Relevant vs. Null control)} \\
$\downarrow$ \\
\textbf{Step 4: Output Extraction (Telemetry Logging \& Actions DB)} \\
$\downarrow$ \\
\textbf{Step 5: ReportAgent MCQ Interview (Fixed Local Evaluator)} \\
$\downarrow$ \\
\textbf{Step 6: Scoring \& Statistical Inference (Brier, Esteban-Ray, DeGroot, DQI, DM Test)} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{The ECN-BENCH Multi-Agent Social Simulation Pipeline.}
\label{fig:pipeline}
\end{figure*}

\subsection{The Local Serving Stack}
Because university supercomputers block outbound internet access during compute jobs, all dependencies must run locally. The offline stack consists of:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Primary Inference Server:} vLLM~\cite{vllm} is utilized to host open-weight frontier models (e.g., Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct, Llama-3-70B-Instruct). vLLM's continuous batching and PagedAttention~\cite{vllm} maximize throughput.
    \item \textbf{Embedding Server:} An Infinity or Ollama background server hosting \texttt{nomic-embed-text} handles embedding queries for vector similarity.
    \item \textbf{Graph Engine:} A self-hosted Neo4j Community Edition instance coupled with the Graphiti context library manages the relational and temporal state of the swarm memory.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{HPC Scalability Migration (Architecture v5.0 - v5.10)}
Scaling multi-agent simulations to thousands of concurrent agents introduced severe system-level bottlenecks. ECN-BENCH implemented a series of engineering upgrades to achieve absolute stability:

\subsubsection{Hybrid LLM Routing (v5.0)}
Ollama-only serving failed under high-concurrency telemetry probes, leading to fatal 3-hour timeouts. Ollama lacks continuous batching. ECN-BENCH decoupled the inference stack: primary chat and generation are routed to a local \textbf{vLLM server} (Port 8000), while embedding pipelines are offloaded to a background \textbf{Ollama instance} (Port 11434). This preserves vLLM KV-cache capacity for agent conversation.

\subsubsection{Multi-GPU Server Isolation (v5.5)}
To support 100-agent simultaneous telemetry probes without stalling the social simulation, a 4-GPU isolation protocol was established:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Benchmark Server (GPUs 0,1):} Handles active agent interactions (Post/Reply/Like) on Port 8000.
    \item \textbf{Evaluator/Telemetry Server (GPUs 2,3):} Dedicated to probability probing and final scoring on Port 8001.
\end{itemize}
This ensures that analytical compute loads do not cause global timeouts in the active simulation loop.

\subsubsection{Apptainer Containerization (v5.6 - v5.7)}
Host-level virtual environments on HPC systems frequently trigger driver mismatches (e.g., \texttt{NVIDIA driver too old}). ECN-BENCH migrated to \textbf{Apptainer (Singularity)} containers, building a self-contained environment from \texttt{vllm-openai:v0.6.3.post1}. To support state-of-the-art models (like Gemma 4 MoE architectures) which are unrecognized by the container's built-in \texttt{transformers} library, we implemented a \textbf{Surgical Bind-Mount Override}:
\begin{equation}
    \begin{split}
    \text{Bind Mount Target: } -B \; &\text{\texttt{/scratch/waves/transformers}} \\
    :\,&\text{\texttt{/usr/local/lib/python3.12/}} \\
     &\text{\texttt{dist-packages/transformers}}
    \end{split}
\end{equation}
This replaces the immutable container libraries at runtime with development versions, adjusting for Python 3.12 container environments while the host runs Python 3.11.

\subsubsection{NFS Database Concurrency and Waves NVMe Migration}
SQLite database files hosted on standard HPC Network File Systems (NFS) experienced slow file locking, causing fatal \texttt{database is locked} errors during parallel telemetry probes. Two protocols resolved this:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{The Connection Lock Protocol:} All SQLite connectors enforce a strict 60-second queue timeout:
    \begin{verbatim}
    sqlite3.connect(database_path, timeout=60)
    \end{verbatim}
    \item \textbf{Waves NVMe Relocation:} Neo4j graph data and active \texttt{.db} files are symlinked directly onto local high-I/O scratch spaces (\texttt{/scratch/waves/} NVMe drives), completely eliminating network latency lock contention.
\end{enumerate}


\section{Experimental Results: The July 2026 Scaling Campaign}
\label{sec:results}

\begin{figure*}[!tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{n_sweep_calibration.png}
\caption{Agent population scaling vs.~forecasting calibration for Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ ($R=60$ rounds). Grouped bars show Conditions A (No-Sim), B (With-Sim), and C (Null Control) across agent counts $N \in \{30, 100, 300, 1000, 3000\}$ on Events C1 (US Election, left) and C9 (UK Trade, right). The shaded region marks the empirically identified optimal zone N=100--300.}
\label{fig:n_sweep_calibration}
\end{figure*}

\begin{figure*}[!tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{r_sweep_calibration.png}
\caption{Deliberation round scaling vs.~calibration for Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ ($N=300$ agents). Bars compare With-Sim (Condition B) and Null Control (Condition C) at $R \in \{30, 60, 90\}$ against the No-Sim baseline (Condition A). The green shading marks the selected $R=60$; the red arrow on Event C9 marks the $R=90$ degradation, which on that event is only $+0.022$ Brier (Appendix~\ref{sec:r_scaling}).}
\label{fig:r_sweep_calibration}
\end{figure*}

\begin{figure*}[!tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{cross_model_comparison.png}
\caption{Cross-model calibration validation at the derived optimal configuration ($N=300$ agents, $R=60$ rounds). Four model sweeps -- Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen2.5-14B (C1 only), and Mistral-7B -- are compared under three conditions on Events C1 and C9. The annotation $\Delta$ denotes the With-Sim lift over the No-Sim baseline; negative lift (red) indicates simulation degradation under the evaluated conditions.}
\label{fig:cross_model_comparison}
\end{figure*}

We present the empirical findings from our multi-GPU scaling campaign executed on a university HPC cluster (A100 GPUs, July 2026). This section and Section~\ref{sec:full_campaign} report the benchmark comparison that motivated this paper and are the context in which the central finding of Section~\ref{sec:degenerate}---loss of sharpness and degenerate consensus under simulation---was discovered; per the caveats established in Sections~\ref{sec:condition_a} and~\ref{sec:serving_note}, the configuration-selection and cross-model conclusions below should be read as engineering and descriptive findings rather than as validated claims about architecture. The experimental design follows a two-stage protocol:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Stage 1 --- Reference Model Scaling Campaign (Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ):} A comprehensive sweep across both the agent population axis ($N \in \{30, 100, 300, 1{,}000, 3{,}000\}$) and the deliberation round axis ($R \in \{30, 60, 90\}$) is conducted with Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ as the designated reference model. This stage characterizes the scaling laws of social simulation calibration and identifies the Pareto-optimal configuration on the cost-performance frontier.
    \item \textbf{Stage 2 --- Cross-Model Validation (Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen2.5-14B, Mistral-7B):} At the derived optimal configuration from Stage 1, three additional model families are evaluated on Events C1 and C9 to assess generalizability of findings across architectures. This validates whether the identified optimal configuration represents a model-agnostic sweet spot or is architecture-dependent.
\end{enumerate}

All calibration metrics use the Multi-Category Brier Score ($BS$); lower values indicate better probabilistic calibration.

\textbf{A statistical caveat that governs this entire section.} Every cell of the N-sweep and R-sweep matrices below is a \emph{single run of a single event}: one configuration, one condition, one repeat ($\texttt{repeats}=1$ in the run manifest). There are no replicates, so no cell carries a variance estimate and no difference between two cells can be tested. The two anchor events C1 and C9 were chosen for compute reasons, not sampled. Consequently the configuration derived here---$N=300$, $R=60$---should be read as a \emph{defensible engineering choice supported by four independent criteria} (diversity, statistical validity, compute budget, echo-chamber avoidance), not as an empirically established optimum. Where earlier drafts described sweep-to-sweep changes as ``dramatic,'' ``catastrophic'' or ``skyrocketing,'' we have replaced that language with the measured deltas. Establishing a genuine scaling law would require replicated runs across a sampled event set, which we identify as future work.

\subsection{Stage 1: Agent Population Scaling (Llama-3.1-8B Reference Model)}
\label{sec:n_scaling}

Figure~\ref{fig:n_sweep_calibration} plots the full N-sweep calibration profile of Llama-3.1-8B-AWQ at $R=60$ rounds on both Events C1 and C9, and Table~\ref{tab:n_sweep_results} gives the underlying Brier scores. The results reveal a characteristic non-monotonic pattern:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Cognitive Under-representation ($N = 30$):} Small agent counts fail to adequately sample the high-dimensional opinion space $\Omega$. The With-Sim condition achieves Brier scores of $0.530$ (C1) and $0.595$ (C9)---worse than the $N=100$ configuration on both events, consistent with stochastic sampling bias at low population densities. Mathematically, when $N$ is small, individual agent biases dominate the aggregated forecast rather than statistical cancellation.
    \item \textbf{Optimal Zone ($N = 100$--$300$):} Performance peaks in this range. On Event C1, the With-Sim condition achieves its best Brier score at $N=100$ ($BS_B = 0.396$), while on Event C9 the gradient is shallower and performance stabilizes across $N \in [100, 300]$ ($BS_B \approx 0.697$--$0.763$). We therefore identify \textbf{$N=100$--$300$} as the empirical optimal zone: it is the smallest range that simultaneously satisfies (i) sufficient cognitive diversity, (ii) statistical validity ($\epsilon \leq 0.10$ for $N \geq 100$), and (iii) avoidance of the conversational noise saturation described below. We standardize on $N=300$ as the production configuration because it provides the stronger margin of error ($\epsilon \approx 0.056$) required for multi-event significance testing.
    \item \textbf{Degradation at large $N$ ($N \geq 1{,}000$), non-monotonic:} Both large-$N$ configurations score worse than the optimal zone, but the pattern is not monotone in $N$ and we caution against reading it as one. On Event C1, $BS_B$ rises to $0.953$ at $N=1{,}000$ and then \emph{falls back} to $0.613$ at $N=3{,}000$; on C9 it moves $0.726 \to 0.789$. With one run per cell, the $N=1{,}000$ C1 value could equally be an outlier as a point on a curve. What the data supports is the weaker statement that neither large-$N$ configuration outperforms $N=100$--$300$ on either event. Two mechanisms are plausible candidates for such an effect, and we state them as hypotheses rather than as formalisations of a measured curve:
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item \textbf{Quadratic Edge Expansion and SNR Collapse:} In a multi-agent social network of size $N$, let $G = (V,E)$ be the directed interaction graph. The number of potential communicative links grows quadratically:
            \begin{equation}
                |E| = N(N - 1) \approx \mathcal{O}(N^2)
            \end{equation}
            At $N=300$, $|E| \approx 8.97 \times 10^4$; at $N=3{,}000$, $|E| \approx 8.99 \times 10^6$. This quadratic explosion floods the Graphiti temporal context engine with semantic redundancies, saturating individual agent memory with circular arguments. The Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) of the forecasting signal collapses.
            \item \textbf{DeGroot Belief Convergence Decay:} Under the DeGroot model $x_i(t+1) = \sum_j W_{ij} x_j(t)$, at extreme $N$ the social weights $W_{ij}$ converge to a low-entropy uniform distribution, causing individual private analytical signals to evaporate into uncritical social mimicry.
        \end{enumerate}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Stage 1: Deliberation Round Scaling (Llama-3.1-8B Reference Model)}
\label{sec:r_scaling}

Figure~\ref{fig:r_sweep_calibration} presents the R-sweep calibration profile at $N=300$, with the underlying scores in Table~\ref{tab:r_sweep_results}. The results identify a clear deliberation optimum and a pathological collapse boundary:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Under-deliberation ($R=30$):} At 30 rounds, the swarm has not yet reached belief convergence. The With-Sim condition achieves Brier scores of $0.720$ (C1) and $0.772$ (C9)---substantially worse than the direct No-Sim baseline ($0.337$ on C1). Empirical Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) telemetry confirms that belief distributions at $R=30$ still exhibit high inter-agent variance, indicating incomplete consensus formation.
    \item \textbf{Optimal Deliberation ($R=60$):} At 60 rounds, calibration improves substantially vs. $R=30$ on both events. On C1, $BS_B$ drops from $0.720$ to $0.535$ (26\% improvement); the With-Sim condition achieves lift over the No-Sim baseline ($\Delta = -0.198$). This confirms that $R=60$ is sufficient for frontier-class models ($\geq$7B parameters) to refine collective forecasts through directed social deliberation.
    \item \textbf{Degradation at $R=90$:} Both events score worse at $R=90$ than at $R=60$, but by very different margins. On Event C1 the change is large ($BS_B$: $0.535 \to 0.998$); on Event C9 it is $0.763 \to 0.785$, a shift of $0.022$ that is well within the range we would expect from run-to-run noise and which earlier drafts described as ``skyrocketing.'' The Null Control on C9 moves $0.820 \to 0.840$. A plausible mechanism is that at $R=90$ agents take 50\% more conversational turns without new external information, so the social feedback loop amplifies existing bias into overconfident consensus. We stress that a single large change on one event does not locate a boundary: the ``echo-chamber boundary at $R \approx 70$--$80$'' proposed in earlier drafts was interpolated between two measured points on two events with no replicates, and we withdraw it as a quantitative claim. What the sweep supports is that $R=90$ was not better than $R=60$ on either event tested.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Optimal Configuration: $N=300$, $R=60$}
\label{sec:optimal_config}

Combining the N-sweep and R-sweep findings, we select $\mathbf{N=300}$, $\mathbf{R=60}$ as the standardized production configuration for ECN-BENCH. While N=100 yields slightly better raw Brier performance on C1 ($0.396$ vs.~$0.535$), the $N=300$ configuration is selected for three reasons:
(1) it provides the statistical validity threshold ($\epsilon = 0.056 < 0.10$) required for multi-event Diebold-Mariano significance testing;
(2) it was expected to achieve superior cognitive diversity coverage ($\geq 75$ agents per persona axis dimension)---a design intent that Appendix~\ref{sec:persona_audit} shows the pipeline realised in only one of the three campaigns, and which should not be read as a measured property of these runs; and
(3) it delivers more stable performance across heterogeneous events (C1 and C9 show less divergent behavior at $N=300$ than at $N=100$).

The $R=60$ setting is supported more weakly: it improves on $R=30$ by $26\%$ on C1 and is not worse than $R=90$ on either anchor event. With one run per cell we do not claim it is an optimum, nor that a collapse boundary has been located.

\subsection{Stage 2: Cross-Model Validation at $N=300$, $R=60$}
\label{sec:cross_model}

\confoundnote{The four models compared here differ simultaneously in release
generation, numerical precision and inference server
(Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}, Table~\ref{tab:provenance}), and this sweep
predates the evaluator audit of Part~I, so its scores come from the single
Evaluator~J pass. It is reported as an engineering record of the configuration
sweep, not as a model ranking; the architectural interpretations offered in
earlier drafts of this appendix are withdrawn in
Appendix~\ref{sec:errata_appendix}.}

Figure~\ref{fig:cross_model_comparison} presents the cross-model calibration comparison at the derived optimal configuration, with the per-condition Brier scores tabulated in Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_results}. Three additional model families are evaluated alongside the reference model:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Qwen2.5-7B (Positive Lift on C1):} At $N=300$, $R=60$, the Qwen2.5-7B swarm achieves $BS_B = 0.445$ on Event C1, below both the No-Sim baseline ($BS_A = 0.560$, $\Delta = +0.115$) and the Null Control ($BS_C = 0.622$). This demonstrates meaningful with-sim lift. On Event C9, $BS_B = 0.608$ vs.~$BS_A = 0.215$, yielding a negative lift ($\Delta = -0.393$), consistent with the event's high information complexity.
    \item \textbf{Qwen2.5-14B (Marginal Lift on C1):} On Event C1, $BS_B = 0.598$ vs.~$BS_A = 0.560$ ($\Delta = -0.038$), a marginal negative lift. The larger parameter count does not translate to improved social calibration at $R=60$, consistent with the hypothesis that instruction-tuned models larger than 7B may suffer from stronger conformity priors that resist group deliberation. A complete 30-event campaign for Qwen2.5-14B exists on the HPC cluster (Jobs 11381327 and 11409272, July 25--27, 2026). A direct .db scan (180 databases) confirms 2,407 social actions (13.38/DB: 1,215 posts, 585 comments, 461 likes, 11 follows)---moderate, analysis-focused interaction, higher than Qwen-7B (723) but well below Llama-8B (27,087) and Mistral-7B (9,364). Its full-campaign Brier statistics are pending local download and are excluded from the aggregate analysis reported here.
    \item \textbf{Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1 (Negative Lift at $R=60$; architecture-dependent optimum):} On Event C1, $BS_B = 0.660$ vs.~$BS_A = 0.560$ ($\Delta = -0.100$)---the With-Sim condition degrades calibration relative to direct elicitation. On Event C9, the degradation is more severe ($BS_B = 0.829$, $\Delta = -0.614$). Earlier drafts read this as an \textbf{architecture-dependent optimum}---the claim that Mistral-7B saturates its deliberation budget before $R=60$ and therefore requires different production settings. We withdraw that reading. As Section~\ref{sec:serving_note} establishes, this model is \texttt{Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1}, released roughly ten months before the other two and served at full precision rather than AWQ INT4; generation, precision and serving stack are all confounded with architecture. The measured fact is that this model configuration performs worst under the production settings. Whether that is an architectural property, a consequence of model age, or an artifact of the precision and serving differences cannot be determined from these data. A dedicated R-sweep on matched-generation, matched-precision models would be required, and we no longer recommend different production settings for Mistral on this evidence.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Swarm Population Capacity Bound ($N = 100$--$300$ Optimal Zone)}
The choice of $N=300$ as the production standard (with $N=100$ as the computationally lighter alternative) is established through a multi-criteria decision framework balancing four competing objectives: (1) cognitive diversity coverage, (2) statistical validity, (3) computational feasibility, and (4) echo-chamber avoidance.

\textbf{Criterion 1: Cognitive Diversity Coverage.}
Small populations ($N < 100$) suffer from \textbf{cognitive under-representation}; they fail to adequately sample the high-dimensional opinion space $\Omega$ (e.g., Llama-3.1-8B: $BS_B = 0.530$ at $N=30$ vs.~$0.396$ at $N=100$ on Event C1). At $N=300$ the swarm has sufficient \emph{slots} to sample the full spectrum of persona viewpoints encoded in the 4-axis DNA space (Worldview, Motivation, Style, Biases) at $\geq 75$ distinct agents per dimension. Whether those slots are filled with distinct personas is a separate, empirical question, since Stage~1 persona generation is itself an LLM call: Appendix~\ref{sec:persona_audit} measures the realised pools and finds a median of $298$ distinct personas per $300$ agents for Mistral-7B but only $40$ for Qwen2.5-7B and $11$ for Llama-3.1-8B on 23 of its 30 events. The population argument of this subsubsection therefore bounds diversity from above and does not establish it.

\textbf{Criterion 2: Statistical Power and Validity.}
For the Diebold-Mariano test (Section~\ref{sec:statistics}) to achieve statistical significance at $\alpha=0.05$ with power $1-\beta=0.80$, we require a minimum sample size. Let $d_e = L_A(e) - L_B(e)$ denote the loss differential for event $e$. The test statistic is $DM = \bar{d} / \sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_d^2 / N}$, where $N$ is the number of events. For a two-sided test with $\alpha=0.05$ and $\beta=0.20$, the critical value is $z_{1-\alpha/2} + z_{1-\beta} = 1.96 + 0.84 = 2.80$. To detect a medium effect size (Cohen's $d = 0.5$), we require $N \geq (2.80 / 0.5)^2 = 31.36$ events. However, within each event, the swarm comprises $N_{agents}$ agents whose beliefs are aggregated. For the aggregated swarm forecast to achieve a margin of error $\epsilon = 0.05$ at 95\% confidence, we require $N_{agents} \geq (1.96 / 0.05)^2 \times p(1-p)$, where $p$ is the true probability. For $p=0.5$ (maximum variance), this yields $N_{agents} \geq 384.16$. Thus, $N=300$ agents provides a margin of error $\epsilon \approx 0.056$, while $N=100$ provides $\epsilon \approx 0.098$---both are acceptable for benchmarking, but $N=300$ is preferred for multi-event campaigns requiring significance testing.

\textbf{Criterion 3: Computational Feasibility.}
From a systems engineering perspective, $N=300$ represents the practical ceiling for high-throughput, multi-model evaluation on standard university-grade HPC clusters. As documented in Figure~\ref{fig:compute_cost_analysis} and detailed in Table~\ref{tab:runtime_breakdown}, a single condition-run at $N=300$ completes in approximately 75--115 minutes wall-clock (where persona LLM generation alone takes $\sim$0.8 min, the full Phase A---including persona evaluation/scoring---takes $\sim$2.7--3.8 min, swarm simulation B+C takes $\sim$60--70 min, e.g., 35.1+31.4 min for Llama-3.1-8B on Event C1, and evaluator scoring takes $\sim$1.2 min). A full 3-condition event evaluates in approximately 1.5--2 hours, and a 10-event campaign under 20 hours.

\textbf{Criterion 4: Echo-Chamber Avoidance.}
Conversely, neither large-population configuration ($N \ge 1{,}000$) improved calibration, while costing $10$--$100\times$ the compute. A candidate mechanism is that the number of potential conversational channels grows quadratically ($\mathcal{O}(N^2)$), flooding the Graphiti temporal context engine and individual agent timelines with redundant, homogeneous interactions, degrading the signal-to-noise ratio of the forecasting signal. We did not instrument message redundancy or agent-level signal retention, so this mechanism is proposed rather than demonstrated; the measured fact is the absence of benefit, not its cause.

\textbf{Decision:} $N=300$ satisfies all four criteria simultaneously, providing the optimal trade-off between statistical validity ($\epsilon \approx 0.056$), headroom for cognitive diversity ($\geq 75$ agent slots per persona dimension; realised diversity is measured in Appendix~\ref{sec:persona_audit} and falls well short of it for two campaigns), computational feasibility ($<$20 hours for 10-event campaign), and echo-chamber avoidance (SNR remains above critical threshold). $N=100$ is recommended for rapid prototyping or limited-compute contexts where statistical marginal error $\epsilon < 0.10$ is acceptable.

\subsubsection{Deliberation Round Horizons ($R = 60$)}
The choice of $R=60$ rounds is determined by three empirical constraints derived from the Llama-3.1-8B reference campaign: (1) convergence to stable beliefs, (2) avoidance of over-deliberation, and (3) computational cost-effectiveness.

\textbf{Constraint 1: Convergence to Stable Beliefs.}
The swarm must execute sufficient rounds for individual agent beliefs to converge toward a stable collective forecast. Empirical telemetry data (Jensen-Shannon Divergence, JSD) shows that belief distributions stabilize after approximately 40--50 rounds for frontier models ($\geq$7B parameters). At $R=30$, the swarm has not yet reached convergence: Llama-3.1-8B achieves Brier score $0.720$ on Event C1 at $R=30$, but improves to $0.535$ at $R=60$---a 26\% reduction in calibration error. This demonstrates that 30 rounds is insufficient for high-capacity models to fully process the injected information and refine their forecasts through social deliberation.

\textbf{Constraint 2: Echo-Chamber Boundary.}
A plausible concern is that beyond some round threshold, closed-loop social interaction without external grounding drives belief structures into overconfident, miscalibrated consensus. Our data are consistent with degradation somewhere above $R=60$ but do not locate a threshold: at $R=90$, Llama-3.1-8B's $BS_B$ rises from $0.535$ to $0.998$ on Event C1 but only from $0.763$ to $0.785$ on Event C9, with the Null Control moving $0.820 \to 0.840$. Two events, one run each, cannot distinguish a threshold from noise. We adopt $R=60$ as the conservative choice below the region where degradation was observed.

\textbf{Constraint 3: Computational Cost-Effectiveness.}
The simulation subprocess time scales linearly with $R$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:runtime_breakdown}). Thus:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $R=30$: fast, but insufficient convergence for reflective models (Llama-3.1-8B)
    \item $R=60$: \textbf{optimal balance}---convergence achieved, safely below echo-chamber boundary
    \item $R=90$: no better than $R=60$ on either anchor event; 48\% slower
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Architecture-Dependent Considerations.}
The empirical cross-model validation reveals important model-specific deliberation dynamics:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Reflective frontier models (Llama-3.1-8B):} Require $R=60$ to fully converge. The marginal calibration gain from $R=30 \to R=60$ is substantial: $BS_B$ improves from $0.720$ to $0.535$ on C1 (26\% reduction).
    \item \textbf{Standard instruction models (Qwen2.5-7B):} Show positive lift over No-Sim baseline at $R=60$ ($BS_B = 0.445 < BS_A = 0.560$ on C1). Standardizing on $R=60$ is appropriate for cross-model comparability.
    \item \textbf{Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1:} shows negative With-Sim lift at $R=60$ ($BS_B = 0.660 > BS_A = 0.560$ on C1). We report this without an architectural interpretation, for the provenance reasons given in Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Decision:} $R=60$ rounds is standardized as the ECN-BENCH production configuration: it satisfies convergence requirements for frontier models (40--50 rounds required), operates safely below the echo-chamber boundary (70--80 rounds), and is computationally cost-effective. Results for Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1 are reported with the caveat that its generation, precision and serving stack all differ from the other two models (Section~\ref{sec:serving_note}), so no architecture-specific conclusion should be drawn from them.

\subsubsection{Susceptibility Margins (Condition B vs. Condition C)}
To confirm that the swarm's updates represent rational Bayesian deliberation rather than simple stimulus-reactivity, we isolate the susceptibility delta ($d = BS_C - BS_B$). A positive delta indicates the swarm remains resistant to irrelevant null control perturbations while updating productively on relevant signal. From Table~\ref{tab:deliberation_susceptibility_n300}, at $N=300$, $R=60$: Llama-3.1-8B shows positive susceptibility on C1 ($d = 0.680 - 0.535 = +0.145$) and C9 ($d = 0.820 - 0.763 = +0.057$). Qwen2.5-7B shows strong positive susceptibility on C1 ($d = 0.622 - 0.445 = +0.177$) and C9 ($d = 0.721 - 0.608 = +0.113$). Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1 shows a negative susceptibility margin on C1 at $R=60$ ($d = 0.633 - 0.660 = -0.027$). Note that on the full 30-event campaign this same model records the \emph{largest} and only statistically significant positive susceptibility margin ($+0.115$, Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_stats}), which illustrates how little weight single-event sweep cells can bear.

Scaling further to $R=90$ yields $BS_B = 0.998$, the worst value in the sweep. On this single event $R=60$ is Pareto-dominant over both alternatives; whether that generalises is untested.

\subsubsection{Configuration Decision Matrix}

\subsection{Hardware Compatibility and bfloat16 Routing}
During the campaign, we identified a critical hardware constraint. Attempting to host reasoning-distilled weights stored in native \texttt{bfloat16} on older \textbf{NVIDIA V100} nodes (compute capability 7.0) triggered immediate vLLM initialization failures. We successfully resolved this by enforcing explicit Slurm routing policies:
\begin{verbatim}
sbatch --constraint=gpu_a100 run_agent_scaling.slurm
\end{verbatim}
This guaranteed execution strictly on Ampere architectures.

\subsection{Small Model Limitations: The Sub-15B Parameter Boundary}
\label{sec:small_model_limits}

In addition to the main benchmark sweeps, we attempted to deploy two sub-15B models on the HPC cluster: \textbf{Gemma 1.1 2B-IT} and \textbf{CodeGemma 7B-IT}. These experiments surfaced three distinct, compounding failure modes that collectively define a practical sub-15B boundary for the unmodified ECN-BENCH pipeline under local vLLM execution.

\subsubsection{Failure Mode I: Context Window Overflow (\texttt{HTTP 400})}

Both Gemma 1.1 2B and CodeGemma 7B natively support a maximum context length of 8,192 tokens. However, the batch Slurm scripts were initially configured with \texttt{--max-model-len 4096}. When the pipeline processes events with dense prompt chains (e.g., events T1 and C1), the concatenated prompt length exceeds this limit, causing the vLLM API server to reject requests with a fatal \texttt{HTTP 400 Bad Request} error. This failure was resolved by patching all Slurm templates to enforce \texttt{--max-model-len 8192}.

\subsubsection{Failure Mode II: KV-Cache VRAM Saturation (OOM)}

Extending the context window to 8,192 tokens dramatically increases the Key-Value (KV) cache size. With initial \texttt{--gpu-memory-utilization 0.20}, the vLLM server crashed with OOM errors during KV cache initialization. Minimum stable utilization thresholds are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Gemma 1.1 2B-IT:} \texttt{--gpu-memory-utilization 0.40}
    \item \textbf{CodeGemma 7B-IT:} \texttt{--gpu-memory-utilization 0.45}
\end{itemize}
These values provide sufficient VRAM headroom for the KV cache while avoiding the full-utilization allocation that can itself trigger allocation failures in the Apptainer container environment.

\subsubsection{Failure Mode III: Guided-Decoding JSON Schema Incompatibility}
\label{sec:schema_bypass}

The most fundamental and irrecoverable failure mode is a structural incompatibility between local vLLM's guided-decoding backend and sub-15B AWQ-quantized models. The ECN-BENCH pipeline is architecturally dependent on \textbf{Stage 1 persona generation}, in which the benchmark calls the model with a strict \texttt{json\_schema} response format to extract a JSON list of 30 diverse stakeholders from the event's seed context.

In the standard configuration, this guided-decoding schema is enforced via the \textbf{outlines}~\cite{vllm_guided} backend within vLLM. However, on the HPC cluster, loading and compiling the outlines grammar for AWQ-quantized models (which involve lower-precision weight layouts) causes one of two failure outcomes:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Compilation hang:} The outlines grammar compilation allocates excessive CPU and VRAM resources, causing the Slurm job to stall indefinitely during server initialization.
    \item \textbf{Silent degrade:} Even when the server initializes, small-parameter models lack sufficient instruction-following capacity to reliably produce schema-valid JSON arrays when guided decoding is effectively disabled or malformed. The model returns raw text, partial arrays, or structurally invalid objects.
\end{enumerate}

In either case, the Stage 1 extraction fails to return a parseable list of stakeholder objects. The ECN-BENCH protocol runner raises a terminal \texttt{ValueError: Intelligent persona generation failed}, immediately aborting the job before any simulation begins. Both Gemma 1.1 2B-IT and CodeGemma 7B-IT exhibited this failure mode consistently across all 30 events under the original unmodified system code.

\subsubsection{The Sub-15B Exclusion Criterion}

The three failure modes above interact multiplicatively: resolving the context-window overflow does not prevent the VRAM saturation, and resolving both does not prevent the guided-decoding failure. The root cause is architectural: sub-15B models in the AWQ quantization format, when served locally via vLLM on the HPC cluster, cannot satisfy the structured output requirements that ECN-BENCH's two-stage persona extraction pipeline demands.

We therefore formalize the following \textbf{Sub-15B Exclusion Criterion} for the ECN-BENCH HPC benchmark:

\begin{quote}
\textit{Models with fewer than 15B active parameters are excluded from local vLLM HPC execution unless one of the following holds: (a) the system code is patched to support non-strict JSON fallback parsing at Stage 1, or (b) the model is served through a cloud-guided inference API (e.g., OpenRouter) that natively enforces JSON schema constraints without relying on the outlines compilation backend.}
\end{quote}

This criterion is consistent with empirical observations from the main scaling sweeps (Appendix~\ref{sec:results}), where even 7B--8B frontier models (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B) required significant engineering effort to produce well-structured JSON outputs within the 3,000-agent persona generation loop. The 2B--7B regime represents a capability floor below which the emergent persona-diversity necessary for meaningful crowd simulation cannot be reliably initialized.




\section{Extended Future Work: Testbed Follow-Ups}
\label{sec:extended_future_work}

These items extend the original three-hypothesis testbed rather than the paper's central sharpness-loss/\allowbreak instrument-reliability finding; Section~\ref{sec:conclusion} lists the two prerequisites (restoring evaluator schema enforcement in place, and $k \geq 3$ repeated evaluator passes) that should be done first.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item[(iii)] \textbf{Isolate length from what co-varies with it.} Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} establishes a monotone within-campaign dose--response between \texttt{evidence\_text} length and Evaluator J's flat rate ($5\% \to 25\% \to 65\%$), and shows the effect is absent on the same inputs under other evaluators. What it cannot separate is raw character count from the speaker count, claim count and positional conflict that rise with it in this corpus. Truncating and padding \texttt{evidence\_text} to matched lengths---holding speaker count fixed in one arm and length fixed in another---would settle which is operative. This is now a confirmatory experiment on a measured effect rather than a test of a conjecture.
    \item[(iv)] \textbf{Re-run Condition A independently for every model}, so that lift becomes a within-model contrast and the original Prediction Lift hypothesis becomes answerable.
    \item[(v)] \textbf{Repair the convergence telemetry}: guarantee a probed distribution at every checkpoint, fail loudly rather than emitting the $0.758277$ placeholder, and replace the vacuous monotonicity flag with a test requiring a minimum count of valid readings.
    \item[(vi)] \textbf{Report flat-unit rate as a first-class diagnostic} in every campaign, and exclude or explicitly impute those units rather than scoring them as confident errors.
    \item[(vii)] \textbf{Export per-unit action counts} into released summaries so the interaction analysis becomes reproducible from artifacts rather than cluster-side queries.
    \item[(viii)] \textbf{Decompose the Brier score} into reliability, resolution and uncertainty so sharpness effects are separable from skill effects in general, not only in the two-condition comparison used here.
    \item[(ix)] \textbf{Replace the performance-derived contamination criterion} with an external one---resolution date relative to each model's documented training cutoff---which the seed corpus already supports and which breaks the circularity of Appendix~\ref{sec:contamination}.
    \item[(x)] \textbf{Expand the catalogue} to $E \geq 92$ post-cutoff events for the power required by the DM test, and add replicates to the configuration sweeps so scaling claims acquire variance estimates.
    \item[(xi)] \textbf{Recover Qwen2.5-14B as a fourth campaign. Done in full.} \texttt{ground\_truth} was backfilled from the authoritative catalogue (\texttt{data/events\_raw.json}, not the disagreeing copy under \texttt{ECN-HPC-DEPLOY/data/}); 6 of 30 events were additionally found to have been simulated against the wrong topic and excluded (2026-08-14), leaving a scorable 24-event campaign. Those 6 events (T3, T4, T5, T6, C6, T8) were re-simulated against the correct dossiers and rescored 2026-08-21 (Appendix~\ref{sec:fourth_model_status}), after a first re-simulation attempt reproduced the identical stale-catalogue bug and had to be redone; Qwen2.5-14B is now compared against the other three models on the full 20-event globally-clean set all four share (Section~\ref{sec:four_model_extension}), not the 17-event subset an earlier draft used. The remaining gap is the action-density scan: the six recovered events' simulation artifacts (persona files, \texttt{.db} traces) were transferred off the cluster and released on 2026-08-26, but the 2026-07-27 scan they would correct read 180 databases that no longer exist, so it cannot be repeated as taken.
    \item[(xii)] \textbf{Redesign the 7-dimension rubric} with finer-grained ordinal scales and event-specific calibration prompts targeting $\kappa \geq 0.6$, re-measured only after item (i), since the current $\kappa$ figures conflate rubric design with the schema-disabling defect.
    \item[(xiii)] \textbf{Investigate social-interaction forcing mechanisms} to activate dormant architectures, and revisit opinion-dynamics modelling---DeGroot, Friedkin--Johnsen, or otherwise---only once item (v) makes convergence measurable.
    \item[(xiv)] \textbf{Complete the single-agent baseline. Partly done.} Section~\ref{sec:cot_baseline} runs it, but only two of its four arms use the campaign's own checkpoint, and both of those reach the model through a hosted API at FP16 rather than the cluster's quantised serving. Neither Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1 nor Qwen2.5-14B is served by the API provider used here, so the two remaining arms must be run against the cluster's own vLLM and Ollama endpoints---which would also remove the precision and serving-stack differences from the matched arms, and cost no external inference. Separately, the contrast needs $E = 59$ events for $80\%$ power at the effect size observed (against $30\%$ at $E = 17$); this is a weaker requirement than item~(x)'s $E \geq 92$, which is set by a different and smaller effect.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Data Availability}

All quantitative claims in this paper are derived from artifacts released in \ecnarchive. The archive is arranged in reading order and each directory carries its own \texttt{README}; \texttt{PROVENANCE.md} records which claims survived the audit of Section~\ref{sec:evaluator_replication} and which were withdrawn, \texttt{DATA\_DICTIONARY.md} defines every field of every file format, and \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} gives the exact commands that regenerate the tables and figures below. Those commands were verified end to end against the archive: all five analyses reproduce output byte-identical to the released files. For each of the four campaigns we release the simulation transcripts and per-unit scored records: \texttt{event\_results.json} (the July 2026 pass, Evaluator J, DeepSeek-R1-14B), \texttt{event\_results\_evaluatorB\_openrouter.json} together with \texttt{event\_results\_evaluatorA\_repeat\{1,2,3\}.json} (the four independent Evaluator~A reads of Section~\ref{sec:four_model_extension}), and \texttt{event\_results\_evaluatorC\_gptlunapro.json} (the independent Evaluator~C read). The two evaluator passes are kept in separate files rather than merged, because the derived metrics stored in each are only valid for that pass's own probability vectors. The rollup behind Section~\ref{sec:four_model_extension} and Table~\ref{tab:four_model_clean} is released as \texttt{reproducibility\_analysis\_master.json}, and the run-integrity audits of Appendices~\ref{sec:persona_audit}--\ref{sec:evidence_depth} read the per-unit SQLite \texttt{trace} databases. These are released in full for Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B ($120$ databases per model). For Qwen2.5-14B they are released for the six events re-simulated 2026-08-21 ($18$ units, $24$ databases), together with a three-unit fragment of the original 2026-07-27 campaign (event~C15); the transcripts for that campaign's remaining $21$ events were removed by the cluster's scratch-filesystem retention policy before they could be transferred, and are unrecoverable. Their scored records are unaffected and are released in full. One consequence is documented rather than repaired: the all-campaign action-density counts of Table~\ref{tab:action_density_30event} come from a 2026-07-27 cluster-side scan of Qwen2.5-14B's then-30-event campaign, 6 of which were later found to have been simulated under the wrong topic (\S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status}); that scan could not be redone against the corrected traces, and carries the reproducibility caveat noted there.

\begin{sloppypar}
The three post-campaign experiments are released with the generator, the analysis and the figure script for each, so that every number in them can be recomputed from the raw runs rather than taken from a summary. The cross-evaluator degeneracy-transfer probe of Section~\ref{sec:volume_mechanism} is \texttt{run\_\allowbreak degeneracy\_\allowbreak transfer.py} and \texttt{analyze\_\allowbreak degeneracy\_\allowbreak transfer.py} over \texttt{degeneracy\_\allowbreak transfer.json}. The $2\times4$ truncation grid is \texttt{build\_\allowbreak truncation\_\allowbreak inputs.py}, \texttt{run\_\allowbreak truncation\_\allowbreak grid.py} and \texttt{analyze\_\allowbreak truncation\_\allowbreak grid.py} over \texttt{event\_\allowbreak results\_\allowbreak trunc\_*.json} and \texttt{truncation\_\allowbreak results.json}. The single-agent baseline of Section~\ref{sec:cot_baseline} is \texttt{run\_\allowbreak single\_\allowbreak agent\_\allowbreak cot\_\allowbreak baseline.py}, producing \texttt{single\_\allowbreak agent\_\allowbreak cot\_\allowbreak baseline\_\allowbreak results.json} ($240$ runs, all fields retained except the chain-of-thought text, which is truncated to $300$ characters per run), analysed by \texttt{analyze\_\allowbreak cot\_\allowbreak baseline.py} into \texttt{cot\_\allowbreak baseline\_\allowbreak analysis.json}. That last file carries all three CoT aggregation variants and both pooling scopes, not only the ones tabulated here, so a reader can check the sensitivity of \S\ref{sec:cot_baseline} directly.
\end{sloppypar}

The event catalogue, seed dossiers, injection bank and scoring rubric are released so that the benchmark can be run against new models, alongside the simulation engine, the HPC protocol runner and the SLURM job scripts as executed. The archive also retains the superseded and invalid artifacts the audit produced---the defective repair of \S\ref{sec:outage}, the wrong-topic re-simulation of \S\ref{sec:fourth_model_status}, and the two divergent event catalogues whose disagreement caused both---so that every defect reported here is independently checkable rather than merely asserted.

The archive is permanently available at \url{\ecnarchiveurl}.
